<meta>This page continues a discussion on the preservation of health and the different types of pleasure, likely from Marsilio Ficino’s "Three Books on Life" (De Vita Libri Tres). The previous context likely warned against carnal excess.</meta>

# ON LONG LIFE

...you should avoid. She <note>Referring to Venus or carnal pleasure</note> promises you barely two pleasures, and those indeed lethal, rather than truly bestowing them. I, however, by the benefit of the father and the brother, promise five to you: five I provide—pure, perpetual, and healthful. The lowest of these is in **smell**; the higher in **hearing**; the more sublime in **sight**; the more eminent in **imagination**; and in **reason**, the most excellent and divine. 

¶ The greater the delight perceived in touch and taste, the more serious the damage that frequently happens to life. Conversely, the more pleasure you derive daily from smell, hearing, and sight—as well as from imagination and often from reason—the longer you extend the threads of life. 

¶ But just as I warned you to beware of treacherous Venus <note>original: *Venerem*; here representing carnal lust and the physical act of procreation</note> in the allurements of touch and taste, so too must you beware of Saturn in that more secret and overly persistent delight of the contemplative mind. For there, he frequently devours his own children <note>In mythology, Saturn (Cronus) ate his children to prevent them from overthrowing him. In Renaissance medical astrology, Saturn governs "melancholy" and deep intellectual labor, which was thought to "consume" the physical vitality of scholars.</note>. For those whom he snatches away with the enticements of his sublime contemplations—recognizing them there as his own—he eventually cuts away from the earth with a certain scythe, if they linger there too long; thus he often steals earthly life from the unwary. In this, at least, he is more indulgent than Venus: for while Venus gives the life she takes from you to another <note>A reference to the belief that the "vital spirit" spent in procreation is lost to the parent but given to the child</note>, restoring nothing to you for your loss, Saturn, in exchange for the earthly life from which he separates you, returns a celestial and everlasting life.

->g.i:<- <note>This is a "signature mark," a guide for the bookbinder to ensure the pages were folded and bound in the correct order.</note>

<summary>This page explores the hierarchy of the senses, arguing that intellectual and "higher" sensory pleasures (sight, sound, smell) prolong life, while "lower" carnal pleasures (touch, taste) shorten it. It concludes with a warning about the "melancholy" of Saturn, which can consume a scholar's physical life but grants spiritual immortality.</summary>

<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, Saturn, Venus, Melancholy, Senses, Reason, Longevity, Renaissance Medicine, Astrology</keywords>

--- Page 2 ---
In this very respect, **Venus** and **Saturn** seem to be similar to one another; for truly, she who rejoices in Aquarius also reigns in Libra <note>In astrology, Venus is "exalted" in Libra, while Saturn is the traditional ruler of Aquarius and also exalted in Libra. This shared planetary dignity links their influences.</note>. Both he and she trouble mankind with the desire for procreation: and it harms those so troubled, so that the same may benefit posterity. But she indeed makes the body fertile and stimulates its productivity. He, however, urges the mind, pregnant with its own seed, toward birth <note>The author is using a Neoplatonic metaphor: Venus governs the physical birth of children, while Saturn governs the "birth" of divine ideas and intellectual works.</note>. 

You, therefore, should be mindful of the proverb **"nothing in excess,"** <note>original: "ne quid nimis," a famous classical maxim urging moderation.</note> and restrain the desire of both while they are "in labor" with the constant reins of prudence. Although he <note>Saturn</note> harms much more quickly and severely those whom he weighs down with weariness, lethargy, sorrow, anxieties, and superstition, rather than those whom he elevates to high things, far above the physical strength and the customs of mortals. I truly advise you above all to observe what the just **Jupiter** taught **Pythagoras** and **Plato**: that human life is maintained in a certain equal proportion of the soul itself with the body.

V <note>This single letter at the bottom of the page is a catchword, intended to help the binder or reader transition to the first word of the next page.</note>

<summary>This page discusses the shared astrological influences of Venus and Saturn on physical and intellectual creation, warning that while Saturn can lead to profound wisdom, he also risks causing deep melancholy and despair if not balanced by prudence.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from general medical theory to a specific discourse on longevity (De Vita Longa), focusing on the therapeutic use of music and wine. The author adopts the persona of a physician-philosopher, likely Marsilio Ficino, given the Neoplatonic emphasis on "spirits."</meta>

# **ON LONG LIFE.**

¶ Cutting through the long-winded digressions: I too have come here as a physician. If flavors extracted from things that are no longer living—likewise scents from spices already dried and devoid of life—are thought to contribute much to life, why then do you doubt that scents from plants still clinging to their own living roots will marvelously accumulate the powers of life? 

Finally, if vapors exhaling from merely <term>vegetal life</term><note>The lowest level of the soul in Aristotelian biology, responsible for growth and nutrition.</note> greatly benefit your life, how much do you think songs will benefit you? These are airy indeed, for an entirely airy spirit; harmonious for a harmonious one; still warm and living for a living one; endowed with sense for the <term>sensual spirit</term><note>The 'spiritus animalis' or animal spirit, which governs the senses and movement.</note>; and conceived by reason for the rational spirit. 

Therefore, I hand over to you this lyre fashioned by me, and with it a <note>original: "cantumque cum ipsa phoebeum"</note> Phoebean song—a solace for your labors and a pledge of a long life. For just as things that are perfectly tempered in quality and aromatic at the same time blend the <term>humors</term><note>The four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) believed to determine health and temperament.</note> among themselves and temper the <term>natural spirit</term><note>The subtle substance produced in the liver that governs nutrition and growth.</note> within itself; so do scents of this kind temper the <term>vital spirit</term><note>The substance produced in the heart that carries life-force and heat through the arteries.</note>; and so again do similar harmonies temper the <term>animal spirit</note><note>The highest spirit, produced in the brain, responsible for thought and perception.</note>. While you tune the strings on the lyre and the sounds, and while you temper the tones in your voice, consider your own spirit to be tempered within in the very same way. 

¶ And lest I be more stingy than <note>original: "Venere... quae sine Baccho friget"</note> Venus herself, who grows cold without Bacchus, receive from me this nectar from Father Liber <note>Another name for Bacchus, the god of wine.</note> himself. Those among you who are especially "cold" in temperament, in similar seasons, every fourteenth day should take two ounces of <term>Vernaccia</term><note>A powerful, often sweet white wine from Italy.</note> or sweet <term>Malmsey</term><note>A rich, fortified wine originally from Greece (Monemvasia).</note> wine with one ounce of bread.
                                                                    <note>g.ii. likely refers to two grains or a specific medicinal weight for the bread or an additive.</note>

<summary>This page argues that living scents, music (the 'Phoebean song'), and specific wines like Malmsey act as medicine by tuning the "spirits" of the human body. The author links the harmony of musical strings directly to the internal harmony of the patient's soul and health.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the pharmaceutical instructions for a restorative tonic, likely from Marsilio Ficino's 'Three Books on Life.' The previous page likely began the recipe for the 'nectar' or 'ambrosia' mentioned here.</meta>

...three hours before a meal. Take once, however, a single drachm <term>drachm: an ancient unit of weight, roughly 3.5 grams or a teaspoon</term> of "sublime water" <note>original: "sublimis aquae"; likely referring to distilled alcohol or aqua vitae</note> distilled from wine, mixed with half an ounce of rose syrup <note>original: "iuleb rosacei"</note>. They may also most conveniently rub this liquid into the skin and use it as a scent to be inhaled.

And so that I may bring you ambrosia as well as such nectar, I bestow upon you this further medicine received from Jupiter. Take four ounces of Chebulic myrobalans <term>myrobalans: a dried, plum-like fruit from India, highly prized in Renaissance medicine for purging 'black bile'</term>; three ounces of rose-sugar; and of candied ginger, one ounce in winter, but half an ounce in summer. Cook these three ingredients gently with honey of Emblic myrobalans <note>another variety of the medicinal fruit, believed to strengthen the heart and stomach</note> and adorn the mixture with seven leaves of gold <note>Gold was frequently used in 'potable gold' recipes, believed to transfer the Sun's life-giving properties to the patient</note>. Take a morsel <term>bolus: a small, soft medicinal ball or pill</term> on an empty stomach four hours before dinner. Take this daily for at least a full year, so that your youth may be renewed like that of an eagle. Thus far, let us imagine that Mercury himself has spoken.

## Confirmation of the above: and why we ought to avoid constant thinking and sexual intercourse. Chapter XVI.

Astrologers say that Venus and Saturn are enemies to one another. However, since in heaven—where all things are moved by love and where there is no lack—hatred cannot exist, we interpret "enemies" to mean those who are different in their effects. Let us set aside other matters for the present. Behold: Saturn has placed pleasure at our center, while Venus has placed it at the circumference. Pleasure is indeed a kind of food for the spirits. Therefore, from opposite directions, Venus and Saturn hunt <note>the Latin "aucupantur" suggests a bird-catcher or hunter laying a snare</note> the flight of our spirit...

<summary>This page concludes a medicinal recipe for "ambrosia"—a youth-renewing tonic of gold leaf and ginger—before transitioning into a philosophical discussion on how the planets Saturn and Venus influence the human spirit.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, astrology, Saturn, Venus, myrobalans, longevity, gold leaf, medical recipes, Marsilio Ficino</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the delicate balance of the human spirit, specifically addressing how opposing astrological influences—Saturn and Venus—affect the mind and body. The previous section likely introduced how the spirit can be pulled in different directions by these forces.</meta>

->**ON LONG LIFE**<-

[They are pulled apart.] One <note>Venus, representing physical pleasure and sexual activity</note> lures through her pleasure toward external things. The other <note>Saturn, representing deep contemplation and intellectual labor</note> meanwhile calls back through his own nature toward internal things. Therefore, they distract the <term>spirit<note>In Renaissance medicine, the 'spirit' (spiritus) was a thin, vaporous substance that connected the physical body to the immaterial soul.</note></term> if they move at nearly the same time, and they dissipate it. For this reason, nothing is more pestilential to a contemplative or a curious seeker than the <term>Venereal act<note>original: "Venereus actus"; referring to sexual intercourse, which was thought to deplete the body's vital spirits.</note></term>; conversely, nothing is more foreign to one pursuing pleasure than care and contemplation. We count the natural philosopher and the religious contemplator in the same category. And in a similar degree, we include anyone deeply pensive in their business affairs and subject to heavy cares.

From this it follows again, that if we wish to relieve or otherwise console someone occupied by <term>Saturnian<note>Referring to the influence of the planet Saturn, associated with melancholy, deep study, and heavy responsibility.</note></term> matters—either too busy with contemplation or weighed down by care—by trying sexual acts, games, and jokes as if they were distant remedies, we strive in vain and even with loss. And conversely, if we wish to moderate someone lost in sexual activity or loosened by games and jokes, we cannot easily correct them through Saturnian severity. 

The best discipline is this: to use the influence of <term>Phoebus and Jupiter<note>original: "Phębi Iouiſqꝫ"; referring to Apollo (the Sun) and Jupiter, who represent the healthy middle ground of music, light, and social harmony.</note></term>—who are the middle ground between Saturn and Venus—to call back to the center those people leaning toward one extreme or the other through similar studies and remedies. But let us finally act as physicians: just as a flame is commonly extinguished in two violent ways—either blown out by winds or, on the contrary, compressed by ashes—so we dissipate the spirit either quickly by a sexual effect or suffocate it gradually by a Saturnian one.

                                                                    g.iii. <margin>Signature mark for the printer</margin>

<summary>This page explains the conflict between deep intellectual work (Saturn) and physical pleasure (Venus), arguing that both extremes can destroy the human spirit if not balanced by the moderate influences of Apollo and Jupiter.</summary>
<keywords>Saturn, Venus, Apollo, Jupiter, spirit, contemplation, sexual activity, health, Renaissance medicine, Marsilio Ficino</keywords>

# ON LONG LIFE.

...passing away: or even by a resemblance to parents, from whom they were generated when those parents were already growing gray.

## On the medicines of the elderly. And again on habitation and diet.
### Chapter XVII.

A rule of the **Chaldeans** <note>original: "Chaldeorum"; referring to ancient Babylonian or Near Eastern sages often associated with hidden medical and astrological knowledge</note> should perhaps be tested for the recovery of youth: to gradually purge the foreign **humors** <note>humors: in ancient medicine, these are the four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) whose balance determined a person's health and temperament</note> absorbed into the body—both the internal ones, using suitable medicines, and the external ones, through rubbings, baths, and the provocation of sweat. Meanwhile, the body should be gradually replenished with healthy and sustaining nourishment.

There are some, however, who promise to completely draw out all old and putrid humors using certain **lozenges** <term>trochisci: medicinal tablets or discs</term> made from viper flesh, or with prepared **hellebore** <note>a potent and toxic plant used in antiquity as a drastic purgative to "clear" the body of disease</note>. They claim that once these are purged and a very healthy humor is recreated through beneficial foods, youth is restored. Those who are more cautious feed the hellebore to hens, and then feed the meat of those hens to the person <note>This was a common "filtering" method to reduce the toxicity of the plant while retaining its medicinal properties</note>. I believe that such a treatment is so dangerous that it should be attempted in youth rather than in old age—lest, perhaps, we experience that "youth" promised by **Medea** to the elderly **Pelias** <note>In Greek mythology, the sorceress Medea tricked the daughters of King Pelias into cutting him up and boiling him in a cauldron by promising it would make him young again; he simply died</note>. For **Hippocrates** <note>The "Father of Medicine" (c. 460 – c. 370 BCE)</note> asserts that even the young grow old quickly from medicines that purge too intensely. But where diet is not enough, you may more safely use an enema, or **manna** <note>a sweet, mild laxative derived from the sap of the flowering ash tree</note>, or especially washed **aloe**. If your bowels are more constricted, manna with broth...

<summary>This page explores ancient methods for rejuvenating the elderly by purging "corrupt humors" and replenishing the body with nutrients. It includes a stern warning against high-risk purgatives, citing the mythological tragedy of Pelias as a cautionary tale.</summary>

<keywords>rejuvenation, humors, Chaldeans, hellebore, Medea and Pelias, Hippocrates, purgatives, manna, geriatric medicine</keywords>

...the power of capon and <term>myrobalan</term> <note>an astringent plum-like fruit from India, highly prized in medieval medicine for balancing the body's humors</note>. Otherwise, you will keep yourself young even in old age with this purgative. 

Take one ounce of washed <term>aloes</term> <note>a bitter plant resin used as a laxative</note>, two drams of emblic myrobalans, the same amount of chebulic myrobalans, likewise two drams of purple roses, and the same amount of <term>mastic</term> <note>a resin from the mastic tree used for digestion</note>; make these into pills with Malmsey wine. This should be done when the moon is fortunately positioned, enjoying a favorable aspect from Jupiter, especially if the moon itself or Jupiter possesses "fixed houses" <note>referring to the zodiac signs of Taurus, Leo, Scorpio, or Aquarius, which were thought to provide stability and long life</note>. For these things contribute greatly to the longevity of life. 

You can also usefully combine <term>rhubarb</term> here with the aloe—specifically, half a part of aloe and half a part of rhubarb. And whenever it is necessary, take one pill in the morning, up to three or five, and drink a little wine afterward. 

When, however, you fear "phlegm" <note>pituitam; one of the four bodily humors, associated with coldness and sluggishness</note> more, you can conveniently include in these pills a third part of <term>agaric troches</term> <note>original: trochiscorum agarici; medicinal lozenges made from larch fungus, used to clear the chest and brain</note> along with two-thirds of aloe, leaving out the rhubarb. But for many years now, I have found that first composition of pills to be the safest for every age. 

At the same hour, make a preparation of this sort: Take one ounce each of emblic, belliric, Indian, and chebulic myrobalans; two ounces of cinnamon; one ounce also of <term>leopard's bane</term> <note>original: doronici</note>; one ounce of purple roses; two ounces of red sandalwood; one dram of saffron; a third part of a dram of musk; and the same amount of <term>ambergris</term> <note>a rare, aromatic substance from the sperm whale used as a fixative and restorative</note>. Afterward, [mix] white [sugar?] with rose water and juice...

->[blue decorative mark] **ON LONG LIFE**<-

...pour sugar of citron. Cook it: make **boluses** <note>A bolus is a large medicinal pill, often the size of a pea or larger, intended to be swallowed or chewed.</note> and wrap them in gold leaf. We have found through useful experience that those taking and administering this four hours before lunch find it strengthens their **vital power** <note>original: "virtutem"; in this context, it refers to the body's innate strength or the "virtue" of the biological systems.</note> and serves to brighten and stabilize the spirit. It will be especially beneficial if you drink a small amount of golden wine <note>Possibly a reference to white wine or wine in which gold has been steeped, a common alchemical tonic.</note> afterward. 

It will also be helpful to frequently soak warmed bread in pure golden wine and rose water, seasoning it further with a little cinnamon and a generous amount of sugar. You should also frequently mix these two with almond milk and a little bread. For mixtures of this kind reflect a **Jovial** <note>Refers to the planet Jupiter ("Jove"). In medical astrology, "Jovial" substances were thought to be life-affirming, temperate, and balanced, countering the "Saturnine" tendencies of old age.</note> nature.

Beyond all the things we have explained or at least indicated above, all city-dwellers <note>original: "urbani"; refers to those living a refined, sedentary, or urban life, who were thought to have different health needs than rural laborers.</note> must diligently beware of these: intense heat and frost. Beware of any cold, whether it occurs after a period of heat or during the night. Likewise, avoid fogs, winds blowing from marshes, or those rushing in from narrow passages. 

Similarly, avoid places where the air either moves too violently or does not move at all. Avoid any dwelling that is overly damp, as well as stench, listlessness, and sorrow. The **followers of Mercury** <note>Those whose professions are ruled by the planet Mercury, specifically scholars, writers, and bookish men, who were considered prone to "melancholy" or "dryness."</note> should be even more careful, and the elderly most careful of all. 

Furthermore, after the elderly have lightly rubbed their whole body in the morning, they should soothe it against the discomforts of the air and of labor with warm oil, or with a somewhat bitter wine into which they have first infused myrrh, rose, and myrtle. They should frequently keep sage in their mouths, as it is most beneficial to the nerves and the teeth. And when the strength of the teeth—

...tion, they are compelled to return to liquid nourishment just like infants: let them beware of very soft things. Indeed, let them temper <note>original: "referant," likely meaning to balance or mix</note> milk with a moderate amount of wine. Let them use fire only as a medicine—specifically, as much as the necessity of expelling cold and arousing the **innate heat** <term>Innate heat (calor innatus) was believed to be the vital warmth present from birth that powers digestion and life</term> demands. Otherwise, the fire may dry out the **natural humor** <term>Natural humor (humorem naturalem) refers to the essential bodily fluids that keep the organs moist and healthy</term> as if by devouring it.

But let them follow the sun as long as it is pleasant, treating it like food, while avoiding **distillation** <note>In older medicine, "distillation" referred to the "dropping" of excess fluids from the head, such as catarrh or mucus caused by environmental changes</note>; and likewise, let them avoid the heat of mid-day. Let them love gentle movements, which are quite necessary for stirring up the vital heat. However, they should hate the labors of the body, and much more so those of the mind. Nor should they endure long thirst, fasting, or wakefulness.

[Red bracket to the left of the following two lines, red underline under "Chapter XVIII."]
### On the nourishment of the spirit, and the preservation of life through odors. Chapter XVIII.

**L** <note>Large blue initial letter</note> we read that in certain hot regions, which are fragrant everywhere with a great abundance of scents, many people with slender bodies and weak stomachs are nourished as if by odors alone. Perhaps this is because the very nature of the place reduces almost all the juices of herbs, fruits, and apples into scents; and also, it dissolves the humors of human bodies there into **spirit** <term>Spirit (spiritus) was considered a very subtle, vaporous substance in the body that acted as a bridge between the physical organs and the soul</term>. Since, therefore, both odor and spirit are a kind of vapor, and "like is nourished by like..."

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the physiological nature of the "spirit" (spiritus) and its relationship to nutrition, likely following a section on how different substances affect the body's vital forces.</meta>

->### ON LONG LIFE<-

...[is nourished], for indeed both the spirit and the spiritual man receive a great deal of nourishment from scents. Truly, this nourishment—whatever form it takes, whether through aromas or warming applications <note>original: "fomentum" — a medicinal application or soothing vapor used to comfort the body</note>—is especially necessary for the elderly and those of a thin habit. By this means, we may somehow compensate for a lack of more solid and substantial food. 

Some people are inclined to doubt whether the spirit is actually nourished by scents. I, however, hold the opinion that it is perhaps nourished only in this way: unless heavy foods are eventually refined into vapors through digestion, the spirit itself—which we have defined as being a vapor—receives no nourishment from them. Thus, wine full of fragrance <note>original: "odore plenum"</note> restores the spirit instantly, while other substances can only refresh it after a long time and with great difficulty. Indeed, we call that vapor into which cooked foods are finally transformed "scent," because every scent is certainly a vapor. Unless this vapor, drawn inward from food, pleases the spirit with its particular aroma, it provides almost no nourishment to the spirit.

For this reason, we strongly agree with our esteemed <term>Avicenna</term> <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), the Persian physician whose "Canon of Medicine" was a standard textbook for centuries</note> when he says: the body is nourished by sweetness, but the spirit by a certain "aromaticity" (to use his term). For the solid density of the body can only grow through a nature that is itself dense, such as is found in sweetness. Conversely, the delicate refinement of the spirit can be refreshed by nothing other than a kind of smoke and vapor, in which that very aromaticity flourishes. Aromatic... <note>The text breaks off mid-word: "Aromaticitas"</note>

<summary>This page argues that the human "spirit" is a vaporous substance nourished primarily by scents and the aromatic qualities of food, rather than solid matter. Drawing on the authority of Avicenna, the author explains why fragrant wine is more restorative than heavy food for the elderly and the frail.</summary>

<keywords>Avicenna, spirit, aromatherapy, digestion, longevity, wine, vapors, aromaticity, medieval physiology</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the physiological and spiritual effects of flavors and elements on the body's internal organs. It focuses on the specific needs of the heart and liver, and transitions into a major discourse on the vital importance of air quality.</meta>

...we call the aromatic quality fragrant, sharp, and in a way astringent <note>original: 'stipticam'; referring to a drying or puckering sensation.</note>. Therefore, since the liver provides nourishment to the body through the blood, it is greatly strengthened by sweetness. The heart, however, because it both creates the spirit <note>The 'spiritus' or 'spirit' in Renaissance medicine was a subtle, vaporous substance that acted as the intermediary between the physical body and the soul.</note> and generates nourishment for that spirit, rightly desires aromatic things. It is beneficial, however, for aromatic things intended for the heart to be seasoned with sweetness, and for sweet things for the liver to be mixed with aromatics; meanwhile, one should avoid excessive sweetness.

¶ Why say more? Galen <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–216 AD), the most influential physician of antiquity after Hippocrates.</note> himself, following Hippocrates <note>The famous Greek physician of the 5th century BC.</note>, believes the spirit is nourished not only by odor but by air—not simple air, I say, but rather air that is suitably mixed. If we believe these men, we will judge that no choice of food, nor of any other thing, is more necessary for life than the choice of air suited to us. For air is always and very easily influenced by the qualities of both earthly and heavenly things; it surrounds us with an immense (so to speak) magnitude, and by its perpetual motion, it penetrates us from all sides, wonderfully drawing us into its own quality. This is especially true of the spirit, particularly the **vital spirit** flourishing in the heart. Into its innermost recesses, the air flows both continuously and suddenly. Thus, the air immediately affects the spirit just as the air itself has been affected; and through the vital spirit—which is both the material and the source of the **animal spirit** <note>The 'animal spirit' (from the Latin 'anima' meaning soul/mind) was believed to be produced in the brain from the vital spirit, governing movement and the five senses.</note>—it likewise affects the animal spirit. The quality of this spirit is of the greatest importance for gifted men of this kind...

...for those who labor much with the spirit <term>spirit: In the medical tradition of this era, "spirit" (spiritus) was not just a religious concept but a physical, vaporous substance that connected the soul to the body, carrying life and thought through the nerves and arteries.</term>. Therefore, to no one does the selection of pure, luminous air, pleasant odors, and music pertain more than to them. For these three are judged to be the principal nourishers of the <term>animal spirit: The most refined of the three spirits, believed to reside in the brain and govern sensation and movement.</term> Truly, well-chosen air is the most powerful factor for life. For many born in the eighth month <note>original: "octavo mense nati"; ancient medical theory held that babies born in the eighth month were less likely to survive than those born in the seventh or ninth, except in exceptionally healthy climates like Egypt.</note> live in Egypt. And some survive in the temperate regions of Greece by the benefit of the healthiest air. This is what Aristotle tells, and Avicenna <note>A famous Persian physician (c. 980–1037) whose "Canon of Medicine" was a standard textbook in Europe for centuries.</note> confirms it.

¶ But indeed, just as the body is composed of various elements and must be nourished by various foods—though not all at the same meal—so the spirit, being similarly composed, must be delighted and cherished by a certain variety of always-well-chosen air. It must also be refreshed daily by a similar variety of selected odors. For air and odor seem to be a certain kind of spirit themselves.

¶ Now truly, Alexander and Nicolaus the Peripatetics <note>Followers of the school of Aristotle; likely referring to Alexander of Aphrodisias and Nicolaus of Damascus.</note>, together with Galen <note>The dominant medical authority of the Roman era whose theories of the "four humors" shaped medicine for 1,500 years.</note>, conclude that the vital spirit and the animal spirit are nourished for this reason, both by odor and by air: because each is mixed and of a similar nature to the spirit itself. They say both, once inhaled, penetrate into the <term>praecordia: The area of the body around the heart and diaphragm, once thought to be the seat of vital heat and emotion.</term> There, they are "cooked" <note>original: "coqui"; the medical process of "concoction," where the body uses heat to transform raw materials like food or air into useful substances.</note> and tempered for life, and then diffused through the arteries. There, having been cooked again, they nourish (as they say) both spirits, but especially the animal spirit.

...to suit the natural spirit, as it is more bodily in nature; however, things that are subtle, pure, and bright are better suited to the vital spirit, and especially to the animal spirit. <note>In Renaissance medicine, the "natural spirit" (centered in the liver) governed growth, the "vital spirit" (centered in the heart) governed heat and emotion, and the "animal spirit" (centered in the brain) governed thought and sensation.</note> Nor should it seem strange that a spirit so thin is nourished by equally thin things. Indeed, many small fish are nourished by the clearest water, and basil <note>Latin: *ocimum*</note> lives, grows, flowers, and emits its scent in similar water. I will pass over which elements some say nourish the Chameleon and the Salamander. <note>According to ancient lore, the chameleon lived on air and the salamander on fire.</note>

Let us return to our own concerns. It is certainly of the greatest importance what kind of air we breathe and what kind of scents we inhale. For the spirit within us becomes just like those things. Indeed, the soul enlivens us through life only insofar as the spirit maintains a harmony that resonates with the soul. 

The spirit is truly the first and most important thing that lives within us; it almost lives alone. Does it not often happen that in some sudden accident or emotion, life, sensation, and movement suddenly desert the limbs? This occurs because the spirit has retreated to the inner chambers of the heart. Often, these functions return to the limbs immediately through rubbing and scents, as the spirit returns there. It is as if life resides in the spirit itself—being a volatile thing—rather than in the <term>humors</term> <note>The four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, black bile) believed to determine health and temperament.</note> or the limbs. Otherwise, because of the dense nature of these parts, life would enter and leave the limbs much more slowly. Therefore, whoever desires to prolong life in the body, cultivate the spirit first of all: this...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the preservation of health and the prolongation of life, focusing specifically on the refinement of the blood and the vital spirit through sensory inputs like smell and sound.</meta>

# ON LONG LIFE

Increase your blood with nutrients that build it up—those that are namely temperate and clear. Cherish this blood with air that is always well-chosen. Feed it daily with sweet odors. Delight it with sounds and songs.

But meanwhile, beware of odors that are too hot. Flee from those that are too cold. Seek out those that are temperate. Balance cold with hot, and dry with moist. Indeed, know that every odor, because it is the most subtle part of a substance, possesses some degree of heat; and expect odors to nourish most effectively when they come from things that are themselves nourishing. For example, the aromatic pear, the peach, and similar fruits; but even more so from fresh, warm bread; especially from roasted meats; and most of all from wine. 

Just as a flavor that is wonderfully pleasing provides the body with abundant and rapid nourishment, you should consider that odors act in the same way upon the <term>spirit</term> <note>In Renaissance medicine, the "spirit" (spiritus) was a subtle, airy substance that mediated between the soul and the body, carrying life force through the arteries.</note>. I am pleased to remind you again: Democritus, when he was on the very verge of death, in order to oblige his friends, sustained his life for four days simply by inhaling the scent of warm bread. He would have preserved his spirit even longer, had he wished to do so.

There are some who say he accomplished this with the scent of honey. For my part, if he did indeed use honey, I believe he poured honey dissolved in white wine over the warm loaves; for the scent of honey is not to be disregarded. Indeed, honey is the "flower of flowers" <note>original: "Flos flor[um]"</note>; it provides no small amount of nourishment through its sweetness and its enduring quality.

<summary>This passage argues that the "vital spirit" can be nourished through pleasant smells and sounds, citing the legend of the philosopher Democritus who supposedly delayed death by smelling warm bread.</summary>
<keywords>Longevity, Aromatherapy, Democritus, Vital Spirit, Dietetics, Honey, Renaissance Medicine</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the preservation of the body’s substances and moves into the therapeutic use of aromatics (scents) to regulate the "vital spirits."</meta>

...preserves things whole from decay <note>original: "putrefactione"; in humoral medicine, decay or "putrefaction" was the cause of many diseases.</note>. Therefore, if anyone knows how to use this as food in such a way that the passages of the body are not clogged by excessive sweetness, nor the <term>bile|one of the four humors, associated with heat and irritability</term> increased by such heat, he will certainly possess a support for a longer life. At the very least, then, apply this condiment to things that are cold and moist.

But to bring you back to the subject of scents. Wherever you fear suffocation and an excessive compression of the <term>spirits|vital spirits; the subtle vapors thought to carry life and sensation through the body</term>—which frequent sorrow and lethargy portend—you should embrace scents that surround you. <margin>...sweet... also... make... if...</margin> But when you fear the flight of exhaling spirits, take in scents infused into your nourishment instead. 

And if you take any scents externally, allow them to be moved only toward the left ribs like a shield <note>Applying scents to the left side of the chest was intended to protect and nourish the heart, the seat of the vital spirits.</note>. Do you not see how suddenly the <term>womb|matrix</term> itself rushes upward or downward toward scents? <note>This refers to the ancient medical theory of the "wandering womb," which was believed to move within the body in response to pleasant or foul smells.</note> How quickly the spirit flies toward the mouth and nostrils, enticed by the bait of a sweet odor? 

Therefore, when the spirit is found to be either meager or very fleeting—as faint-heartedness and bodily weakness (occurring even from a small cause) often reveal—entice it; or rather, feed it and retain it with scents. Choose the scent of wine above all others. For the exhaling odor nourishes the "mixed spirit" through its nature, which both nourishes the body greatly and swiftly, and delights the senses with pleasure. Such a wine is, above all others, warm, moist, fragrant, and clear.

For just as hemlock is a poison to man, so wine is a poison to hemlock—though not if drunk at the same time, but rather if consumed shortly after.

### The medicine of the Magi for the elderly.
# Chapter XVIII.

**T**he Magi, those observers of the stars, came to Christ—the leader of life—with a star as their guide. They offered a precious treasure of life: **Gold**, **Frankincense**, and **Myrrh**. They dedicated these three gifts, representing the three lords of the planets, to the Lord of the stars. 

**Gold** was given for the temperament of **Jupiter**, which is the most perfectly balanced of all celestial bodies. **Frankincense** was given specifically for the **Sun**, as it burns with both the heat and the fragrance of Phoebus <note>original: "phębeo"; Phoebus is a classical poetic name for the Sun god, Apollo</note>. **Myrrh**, finally, was given for its power to strengthen and preserve the body, corresponding to **Saturn**, the most firm and enduring of all the planets. 

Therefore, come here to these wise Magi, all you elderly people; they bring gifts to you as well that will prolong your life. These are the same gifts with which they are recorded to have once worshipped the Author of Life. Come, you seniors who now find old age a heavy burden to endure. Come also, all you others who are nearly at the threshold of old age...

...the fear of what is coming troubles you. Receive, I pray, these life-giving gifts with alacrity. Take two ounces of frankincense, one of myrrh, and half a drachm <note>A drachm was a unit of weight roughly equal to 3.5 grams</note> of gold beaten into leaves. Grind these three together, blend them, and form them into pills with a certain pure golden wine. This should be prepared at an opportune time, when Diana <note>The Moon; original: "Diana"</note> enjoys the favorable aspect of Phoebus <note>The Sun; original: "Phębi"</note> or Jupiter. Take thereafter a small portion of such a great treasure at every dawn. Wash it down with a small drink of pure wine—unless perhaps the summer heat has intensified. In that case, it is better to drink rose water. If, however, anyone among you fears the heat more at any time, let him add chebulic myrobalan <term>Chebulic myrobalan: A dried fruit from the Terminalia chebula tree, highly valued in medieval medicine for its "cooling" properties and ability to purge "black bile"</term> or emblic <term>Emblic: Also known as Indian gooseberry, used in various medicinal preparations to balance the body's internal environment</term> equal to the combined weight of the frankincense, myrrh, and gold. This will without doubt protect the natural humor <note>In pre-modern medicine, "humors" were the bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) that determined health and temperament</note> from putrefaction <note>The "decay" or corruption of these fluids, which was believed to lead to aging and disease</note>. This will drive away the dissolution of the humor much longer. This will nourish, confirm, and strengthen the three spirits <note>Ancient physiology divided the body's life-force into three "spirits": the Natural (centered in the liver), the Vital (heart), and the Animal (brain)</note> within you. This, in turn, will enliven the senses, sharpen the intellect, and preserve the memory.

### Chapter 19. On avoiding the dangers imminent from every seven-year cycle of life.

**S**ince astronomers have distributed the individual hours of the day to the planets in their respective order, and similarly the seven days of the week, and have even arranged the duties of the planets by months during the development of a fetus: why should we not also arrange them by years? So that just as—

<summary>This page provides a medicinal recipe using gold leaf and resins to preserve the body's "spirits" and "humors," then introduces an astrological theory regarding the dangers of seven-year life cycles (climacterics).</summary>
<keywords>Medical Recipe, Alchemy, Astrology, Humors, Frankincense, Myrrh, Gold leaf, Planetary Aspects, Longevity, Seven-Year Cycles</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the preservation of health through astrological timing, focusing on the planetary rulers of different stages of life.</meta>

-># ON LONG LIFE<-

Just as Saturn ruled the infant while hidden in the womb during the first month, and the Moon ruled in the last, so immediately after birth—with the order now reversed—let the Moon lead during the child's first year. In the second year, if you wish, Mercury; in the third, Venus; in the fourth, the Sun; in the fifth, Mars; in the sixth, Jupiter; and in the seventh, Saturn. And from then on, a similar order is repeated throughout one’s life.

Therefore, in every seventh year of life, a very great change occurs in the body. For this reason, these years are the most dangerous, since Saturn is generally "foreign" <note>original: *peregrinus*; in astrology, a planet is "peregrine" when it lacks specific strength or "dignity" in its current position, often implying it acts with unpredictable or wandering influence.</note> to us; furthermore, at that time, governance passes directly from Saturn (the highest of the planets) back to the Moon (the lowest of the planets). 

Greek astronomers call these years <term>climacteric</term> <note>original: *climatericos*; from the Greek word for "ladder rungs." These were seen as critical, "step-like" turning points in a person's life.</note>. We call them "scalar" or "step-like," or even "decisive" years. It may be that in cases of illness, the planets rule over the <term>humors</term> <note>The four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, black bile, and yellow bile) believed to determine health and temperament.</note> or the natural disposition of the patient day by day in this same order. This is why every seventh day is called a "judicial" day <note>A day used by physicians to judge the progress or outcome of a disease.</note> for the same reason—and the fourth day as well, because it sits in the middle of the seven-day cycle. 

If, therefore, you desire to extend your life into old age, uninterrupted by such "steps," you should diligently consult an astrologer whenever you approach any seventh year. Learn from what direction danger might threaten you. Then, either visit a physician or summon your own prudence and **temperance**. For even Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), the most influential astronomer of antiquity, whose works were the foundation of Renaissance astrology and medicine.</note> admits that the threats of the stars can be averted by these remedies. He even adds that the stars' pro-

...we have written. Indeed, regarding that celestial favor I was just mentioning for the sake of a long youth—now, speaking as far as a poet might, and acting as far as a physician may—we shall seek it from Phoebus and Bacchus.

The Sun’s youth is eternal, belonging to Phoebus <note>Phoebus is a common epithet for Apollo, the god of the Sun, music, and healing.</note> and Bacchus.
¶ For it is fitting for both gods to have unshorn hair <note>original: "intonſis crinis". In classical mythology, long, flowing hair was a symbol of perpetual youth and vital energy.</note>.

Phoebus and Bacchus are always inseparable brothers; they are almost one and the same. Phoebus is the very soul of that celestial sphere <note>Referring to the Sun.</note>. The sphere itself is Bacchus; or rather, Phoebus is the entire circuit of the sphere. Bacchus, however, is the fiery circle within this circuit; or more truly, Phoebus is the nourishing light in this fiery globe. Bacchus exists there as the healthful heat <term>salutaris calor</term> <note>In Renaissance medicine, "vital heat" was the internal flame of the body that governed digestion and life itself.</note> derived from that light. Therefore, they are always brothers and companions, almost always one and the same. For what of this? If the Sun in springtime is indeed Phoebus, then he awakens the songs of birds with his own melody and tempers the seasons with his lyre; yet in autumn, the same Sun exists as Bacchus, the author of wine.

¶ Father Liber <note>original: "pater ille Liber". Liber was an ancient Roman god of fertility and wine, often used interchangeably with Bacchus/Dionysus.</note>, who loves the hills, brings us three things for the preservation of youth. First, these very sun-drenched hills. Second, the most delightful wine from these hills. Third, a perpetual sense of security <note>original: "securitatem". ->ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS.<-
[Paragraph mark] Preface to the Book On Life from the Heavens.

**M**ARSILIO Ficino the Florentine to the most serene King of Pannonia <note>original: "Pannonię"; refers to the Kingdom of Hungary and surrounding territories</note>, always invincible.

Most fortunate King of all, the ancient philosophers most diligently investigated the powers of the celestial bodies and the natures of lower things. Since they believed that a man knows in vain if he does not know for himself, they seem to have rightly directed their entire investigation primarily toward obtaining for themselves a life from the heavens. They judged, I believe, that both the elements and the things composed from them would be known in vain, and that the movements and <term>influences</term> <note>original: "influxus"; the astrological theory that celestial bodies emit a physical or spiritual fluid that affects earthly life</note> of the heavens were observed too rashly, unless these things, known together and joined, might eventually lead them to life and happiness. 

Such contemplation was beneficial to them, it seems, first for their present life. For Pythagoras, Democritus, and Apollonius of Tyana <note>a Greek Neopythagorean philosopher known for his travels and reputed wonder-working</note>, and whoever else studied this most intently, achieved prosperous health and a long-lasting life through the use of the things they had learned. Furthermore, it contributed to their future life, both through the spreading of their glory among posterity and through enjoyment with God in eternity. Indeed, from the marvelous order of the whole world, they finally came to know its Ruler, and they loved Him, once known, above all else. 

To you indeed, your greatness of soul promises a future glory through all ages...

<summary>In this preface, Marsilio Ficino dedicates his work to King Matthias Corvinus, arguing that the study of astrology and natural philosophy is only valuable if applied to improving one's health and achieving spiritual happiness.</summary>
<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, Matthias Corvinus, astrology, natural philosophy, Pythagoras, Democritus, Apollonius of Tyana, health, celestial influence</keywords>

...generosity, a perpetual victory is promised. Divine mercy also grants a blessed life with God in eternity to your remarkable piety and justice. Finally, as far as I have been able to conjecture from certain signs, the lucky stars have decreed for you a prosperous and sufficiently long life among mortals. 

However, your own diligence, along with the care of physicians and astrologers, can undoubtedly ensure that what the stars promise, they also fulfill with the firmest faith and with an even more abundant increase in your <term>legacy</term> <note>original: "progenie", which can refer to offspring or the continued success of a family line</note>. Indeed, all the most learned astrologers and physicians confess that this can be achieved through science and <term>prudence</term> <note>In this context, "prudence" refers to practical wisdom and the ability to make right choices for one's health and affairs.</note>. 

Therefore, since I recently composed a commentary on a book by Plotinus <note>Plotinus (c. 204–270 AD) was the founder of Neoplatonism; his philosophy was central to the intellectual rebirth of the Renaissance.</note> regarding <term>how to draw favor from the heavens</term> <note>original: "de favore celitus hauriendo". This involves the "natural magic" of aligning one's spirit and body with planetary influences to attract health and success.</note>—which was among the books of Plotinus intended for the great Lorenzo de' Medici <note>Lorenzo "the Magnificent" de' Medici (1449–1492), the ruler of Florence and a legendary patron of the arts and philosophy.</note>—I have decided to select this work from among my other commentaries, with Lorenzo’s own approval. And I have decided to dedicate it especially to your Majesty. I hope indeed that while the course of your life...

<meta>This page serves as the opening address to the third book of Marsilio Ficino’s influential work, "Three Books on Life" (De vita libri tres). It establishes a welcoming, therapeutic tone for the reader.</meta>

# ON THE CELESTIAL LIFE

The Words of Marsilio Ficino to the Reader of the following book.

Greetings, ingenious guest. Greetings once again, whoever you are that hungers for well-being <note>original: "salutis"; in the Renaissance, health of the body and salvation of the soul were often seen as intertwined</note>, you who approach our threshold. I pray, eager guest, observe first of all how hospitable I am. It was surely the duty of the one entering to offer a greeting <note>original: "salutem"; this also translates as "health" or "welfare"</note> immediately upon being welcomed. Yet I, anticipating that greeting, bade you be well as soon as you were within my sight. I have freely received you as you enter, though you are still a stranger to me. If you linger a while here with me, I will grant you that promised health—if God breathes His favor upon us. 

You have therefore found a home <note>original: "hospitium"; refers here to the hospitality of the book itself, personified as a host</note> that is friendly toward everyone, and now full of love toward you. If by chance you bring anything with you that is contrary to love; if you hold any hatred, I pray that you set it aside before you touch these life-giving medicines <note>Ficino views his philosophical and astrological advice as a form of spiritual and physical medicine</note>. For love gave life to you, and pleasure deep within. Life, in turn...

<summary>This page features Marsilio Ficino’s welcoming preface to the reader, framing the subsequent text as a "hospitable home" and a source of life-giving medicine. He emphasizes the need for a loving and open mind before the reader engages with his teachings on celestial health.</summary>

<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, On the Celestial Life, Renaissance medicine, hospitality, spiritual health, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s defense of "astrological medicine," moving from a general theory of celestial influence to specific practical applications and a personal anecdote regarding a child's survival.</meta>

...brings. If perhaps some of these things please you less, then indeed, set those aside. But do not, for that reason, reject the rest. Finally, if you do not approve of **astronomical images** <note>original: "imagines astronomicas"; these are astrological talismans or amulets designed to capture planetary influences for healing.</note>—which were, in any case, devised for the health of mortals and which I myself do not so much endorse as describe—then certainly, with my permission and even, if you wish, my recommendation, discard them. 

But do not neglect **medicines** that are at least strengthened by some **celestial aid**, unless you happen to neglect life itself. For I have found through long and frequent experience that there is as much difference between medicines of this kind and others made without **astrological selection** <note>original: "delectu astrologico"; the practice of gathering herbs or preparing remedies only when the stars and planets are in favorable positions.</note> as there is between pure wine and water. 

For example, a certain infant born in the eighth month after conception—born in Florence in the month of March, at night, while **Saturn was ascending and retrograde** <note>original: "Saturno retrogrado"; in astrology, a retrograde planet appears to move "backward" through the zodiac. Saturn was typically associated with illness and death; its retrograde state during a birth was considered a very dangerous omen for the child's survival.</note>—was half-dead. Yet, through such diligence of ours (or rather, of God), he seemed to be restored to life rather than merely saved. 

And he has now completed nearly three years in good health. Now, if I were to tell more stories of this same kind, I would be speaking the truth. I will present them not boastfully (which is entirely foreign to the character of a philosopher) but rather as an encouragement. Truly, we have now spoken enough to you, partly to win you over and partly to exhort you; from here on, therefore, let us speak with **Plotinus** <note>Plotinus (c. 204–270 AD) was the founder of Neoplatonism. The author, likely Marsilio Ficino, viewed his own work as a continuation of Plotinus's philosophy.</note>, so that we may ultimately consult for your benefit more carefully.

In all things treated by me here and elsewhere, I wish it to be asserted only as much as is **approved by the Church** <note>This is a standard "orthodoxy clause" used by Renaissance scholars to protect themselves from accusations of heresy by the Catholic Church while discussing sensitive topics like astrology.</note>.

<summary>The author defends the use of astrological timing in preparing medicine, citing a miraculous case of a premature infant in Florence saved by these methods, before concluding with a formal submission to Church authority.</summary>
<keywords>astrological medicine, talismans, Saturn retrograde, Florence, Plotinus, medical ethics, Church doctrine</keywords>

->THE BOOK OF MARSILIO FICINO THE FLORENTINE<-
->**ON OBTAINING LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS**<-
->COMPOSED BY HIM AMIDST HIS<-
->COMMENTARIES ON<-
->PLOTINUS<-

¶ In what, according to Plotinus <note>a major Neoplatonic philosopher whose works Ficino translated from Greek to Latin</note>, the power attracting favor from the heavens consists—namely, that the Soul of the World, and the souls of the stars and the <term>daemons</term> <note>original: "Daemonum"; here meaning intermediary spirits or guiding intelligences, not "demons" in the modern sense</note> are easily enticed by appropriate bodily forms. ¶ Chapter One.

**S**<note>The original manuscript features a large decorative initial 'S' in red and blue.</note>uppose only these two things existed in the world: on one side, **Intellect**; on the other, **Body**. If **Soul** were absent, then Intellect would not be drawn toward Body, for Intellect is entirely immobile and lacks the impulse for motion, being as far distant from Body as possible. Nor would Body be drawn toward Intellect, as Body is ineffective and unsuited for motion by itself, and is far removed from Intellect. But if Soul is placed between them, being conformable to both, an attraction will easily occur from both sides toward the other. ¶ First of all, Soul herself is the most easily led of all things, because she is the "first mobile" <note>the primary source of movement in the universe</note>, moving of her own accord and spontaneously. Furthermore, since she is—as I have said—the "middle of things," she contains all things within herself in her own way,

<summary>Marsilio Ficino begins his treatise on celestial influence by arguing that the Soul acts as the necessary mediator between the divine Intellect and the physical Body, allowing for movement and attraction between them.</summary>
<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, Plotinus, Soul of the World, Anima Mundi, Neoplatonism, celestial influence, Intellect and Body, daemons, Renaissance philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of how the World Soul acts as a mediator between the eternal Ideas and the physical world, explaining the mechanics of how celestial influences are "drawn down" into matter.</meta>

...and from both sides by a near relation. Therefore, it is reconciled to all things: even equally to those things that are distant from one another, for they are certainly not distant from the Soul itself. For besides the fact that on one side the Soul is conformed to divine things, and on the other to perishable things, and inclines toward both with affection, it is entirely within and simultaneously everywhere. 

Added to this is the fact that the Soul of the World possesses at least as many **seminal reasons** <note>original: "rationes seminales"; these are the "blueprints" or active life-forces within the Soul that translate divine Ideas into physical shapes</note> of things from the divine as there are **Ideas** <note>eternal, perfect forms existing in the Divine Mind</note> in the Divine Mind. By these same reasons, the Soul fabricates just as many species in matter. 

¶ Whence each species, through its own proper seminal reason, corresponds to its own proper Idea; and through this reason, it can often easily receive something from that higher realm, since it was originally produced from there by means of this very reason. Therefore, if a thing should ever degenerate from its proper form, it can—through this medium close to it—be formed once again, and through that medium be easily reformed from above. 

And if indeed you rightly apply many things that are scattered, yet conform to the same Idea, to a certain species of things or to an individual member of it, you will soon draw down a singular gift from the Idea into this material (thus opportunely prepared) through the seminal reason of the Soul. For it is not the **Intellect** <note>the highest, most abstract part of the mind</note> itself that is properly drawn, but the **Soul**. Let no one, therefore, think that certain divine powers <note>original: "numina"</note>—which are entirely segregated from matter—are drawn by specific materials of the world. Rather, it is **Daemons** <note>original: "Demones"; in this context, these are not "demons" but intermediary spirits or natural forces that inhabit the air and stars</note> and the gifts of the animated world and of the living stars. 

¶ Let no one, again, wonder that the Soul can be, as it were, enticed through material forms. Since <margin>...

->### ON THE CELESTIAL LIFE<-

...it creates for itself suitable baits <note>original: "escas"; refers to material objects that attract spiritual influence</note> of this kind, by which it might be attracted; and it always and willingly dwells in them. Nor in the whole living world is anything found so misshapen that a soul is not present to it, or in which the gift of soul is not also present. Therefore, the correspondences <note>original: "congruitates"</note> of such forms to the reasons <term>ratios: the divine patterns or "blueprints" within the World Soul</term> of the World Soul were called "divine lures" by Zoroaster <note>The ancient Persian prophet, highly regarded by Renaissance Neoplatonists as a source of "ancient theology"</note>; and Synesius <note>A 4th-century Neoplatonist philosopher and bishop</note> confirmed these to be magical attractions.

Finally, let no one believe that at a certain time, all gifts are drawn from the soul into a specific type of matter; rather, according to the opportunity, only the gifts of the seed from which such a species sprouted, and of similar seeds, are drawn. Thus, a man, by employing only human things, does not claim for himself the specific gifts of fish or birds, but rather human and similar ones.

However, when those things belonging to a particular star or Spirit <term>Daemon: in this context, a neutral intermediary spirit or celestial power associated with a planet</term> are employed, he undergoes the specific influence <note>original: "influxum"; literally a "flowing in" of celestial energy</note> of that star and Spirit, just as wood prepared with sulfur <note>Sulfur was known for its extreme flammability; Ficino uses this to show how "priming" an object makes it catch celestial "fire" instantly</note> is made ready for a flame that is everywhere present. And this happens not only through the rays of the star and Spirit themselves, but also through the World Soul itself, which is present everywhere. In this soul, the "reason" of any star or Spirit flourishes—partly as a "seminal" reason for generating, and partly as an "exemplary" reason for knowing. For this World Soul, according to those authorities older than the Platonists, constructed <note>The Latin word "edifica-" is truncated here, likely completing as "edificavit" (built/constructed)</note> [the world] by its own reasons...

<summary>Marsilio Ficino explains how the World Soul animates all things and how specific material "baits" or correspondences can be used to attract celestial influences. --- Page 32 ---
<meta>The page begins mid-word, continuing from the previous page's verb, likely "posuit" (he placed) or "constituit" (he established).</meta>

...[he placed] beyond the stars in the heavens certain figures, and parts of them such that they themselves are also certain figures; he also impressed specific properties upon all of these. In the stars, the figures, the parts, and the properties, all the species of inferior <note>meaning earthly or lower-order things in the Great Chain of Being</note> things are contained, as well as their properties. 

He established forty-eight universal figures: specifically, twelve within the zodiac and thirty-six outside of it. Likewise, within the zodiac, there are thirty-six according to the number of faces <term>faces: also known as decans, these are ten-degree subdivisions of a zodiac sign, each ruled by a different planet</term>. Again, in that same place, there are three hundred and sixty according to the number of degrees. For in every degree there are several stars, from which the images <note>original: "imagines," referring to astrological symbols or constellations</note> are constructed there. Similarly, he divided the images outside the zodiac into several figures according to the number of their own faces and degrees. 

Finally, from these universal images, he established certain relationships and proportions toward other universal images, which themselves also exist as images there. Moreover, each of these kinds of figures possesses its own continuity, formed by the rays of their stars being cast upon one another with a specific, unique property. From these most perfectly ordered forms, the forms of inferior things depend, being organized from that celestial source. 

But even those celestial forms, though distinct from one another, proceed from the reasons <note>original: "rationibus," here referring to the formative principles or "logoi" that act as blueprints for reality</note> of the Soul which are joined to each other; and though they are in some way changeable, they proceed from stable principles. But insofar as these celestial forms do not comprehend themselves, they are referred back to the forms existing in the Mind <note>referring to the Divine Intellect or "Mens," the highest level of reality in Neoplatonic thought</note>.

<summary>This page outlines a Neoplatonic cosmological hierarchy, explaining how forty-eight universal constellations and their subdivisions (decans and degrees) act as the formative blueprints for all earthly life. It describes how these celestial images are generated by the World Soul and ultimately derived from the Divine Mind.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between the World Soul, the stars, and individual life, following previous explanations of how celestial influences descend into material forms.</meta>

-># ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS<-

...whether animal or something more eminent, they comprehend themselves; these, though manifold, are reduced to the most simple One and Good, just as celestial figures are reduced to the pole. But let us return to the soul.

¶ Therefore, when the soul generates the specific forms and powers of lower things, it effects them through its own "reasons" <note>original: "rationes" — the formal principles or blueprints within the Soul that govern the creation of physical things</note>, under the support of the stars and celestial forms. However, it exhibits the unique gifts of individuals—which are often as miraculous in some people as they are in entire species—similarly through seminal <note>referring to "seed-like" generative powers</note> reasons. This is done not so much under the support of celestial forms and figures, but rather through the position of the stars, the pattern of their motions, and the "aspects" <term>aspects: the geometric angles between planets (like squares or trines) that astrologers believe influence earthly events</term> of the planets, both among themselves and in relation to the stars higher than the planets.

¶ Indeed, our soul, beyond the specific powers of our individual limbs, brings forth a common power of life everywhere within us. This happens most especially through the heart, as if it were the fountain of the fire closest to the soul. Similarly, the World Soul <note>original: "anima mundi" — the Neoplatonic concept that the entire universe is a living being with its own soul</note>, flourishing everywhere, unfolds its power of common life everywhere, primarily through the sun. Hence, some locate the soul—both in us and in the world—entirely in every limb, yet most especially in the heart and the sun.

¶ But always remember that just as the power of our soul is applied to our limbs through the spirit <note>original: "spiritus" — a subtle, vaporous substance that acts as a bridge between the physical body and the immaterial soul</note>, so the power of the World Soul is spread through all things under the World Soul via the "fifth essence" <term>fifth essence: also known as the quintessence or aether, a celestial substance distinct from the four earthly elements (earth, water, air, fire)</term>, which flourishes everywhere as a spirit within the body of the world. This power is most especially infused into those things which [contain] the most of this kind of spirit...

...has drawn out. 

Moreover, this **fifth essence** <term>Fifth Essence (quintessence): In ancient philosophy, the pure, celestial element that exists beyond the four earthly elements of earth, air, fire, and water. It was believed to be the "spirit" or "virtue" of a substance.</term> can be increasingly absorbed within us if one knows how to isolate it when it is mixed with other elements, or at least by frequently using those things which abound in it—especially the purer ones. Such as choice wine, sugar, balsam, and gold, as well as precious gems, <term>myrobalans: Dried, plum-like fruits from India, highly prized in medieval medicine for their supposed life-extending properties.</term> and things that smell most sweet or shine brightly. 

Especially effective are those things which possess a warm, moist, and clear quality within a **subtle substance** <note>original: "subtili substantia," referring to matter that is refined and easily absorbed by the spirit rather than the heavy, "gross" matter of the body.</note>. Besides wine, the whitest sugar is of such a kind, especially if you add gold to it, along with the scent of cinnamon and roses.

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page appears to be from Marsilio Ficino's "Three Books on Life" (De Vita Libri Tres), specifically Book III, "On Obtaining Life from the Heavens." It follows a discussion on how celestial influences can be captured for health and well-being.</meta>

-># ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS.<-

They say these things are to be applied externally and taken internally according to one's strength, especially on the day and during the hour of the Sun, and while the Sun is ruling in the specific configuration of the heavens. Solar things <note>Items traditionally associated with the Sun’s astrological power</note> are truly all those small gems and flowers which are named heliotropes <note>original: "elitropia"; literally 'sun-turners'</note> because they turn toward the sun. Likewise: gold, orpiment <note>a golden-yellow mineral pigment</note>, golden colors, chrysolite, carbuncle <note>a deep red gemstone, such as a garnet or ruby</note>, myrrh, frankincense, musk, amber, balsam, yellow honey, aromatic cane, saffron, spikenard, cinnamon, aloe wood, and other spices. The ram, the hawk, the rooster, the swan, the lion, the scarab beetle, and the crocodile; also men who are blonde, curly-haired, often bald, and magnanimous. These can be adapted for use partly as food, partly as ointments and fumigations <note>medicinal vapors or incense</note>, and partly for general daily use. These things should be felt and thought of frequently, and above all, they should be loved. And the greatest amount of light must be sought.

If you suspect the stomach is deprived of the warmth of the liver, draw the power of the liver to the stomach, both by rubbing and by warm compresses, through those things which agree with the liver: through chicory, endive, spodion <note>original: "spodium"; a medicinal powder made from burnt minerals or ivory</note>, agrimony, liverwort, and animal livers. Similarly, so that your body is not deprived of the influence of Jupiter, move your body on the day, during the hour, and under the reign of Jupiter, and meanwhile use Jovial <note>items associated with the planet Jupiter</note> things: silver, hyacinth <note>likely a blue or reddish gemstone in this period</note>, topaz, coral, crystal, beryl, spodion, sapphire, and green and airy <note>sky blue</note> colors. Use wine, sugar, white honey, and especially focus your thoughts and affections on Jovial things—that is, excellent things—horses, religious and law-giving men, and spend time among men of this kind, as well as those of a sanguine <note>original: "sanguineos"; referring to the cheerful, energetic temperament associated with the 'blood' humor</note> nature.

i.ii. <note>Printer's signature mark</note>

For these things were made from heaven; they are constantly governed from there, and they are primarily prepared for those celestial things. And what is greatest of all: the world is a living being <note>original: "mundus animal." This refers to the *Anima Mundi* or World Soul, a central concept in Neoplatonic philosophy where the universe is seen as a single, interconnected organism</note>, more unified within itself than any other animal, provided it is the most perfect animal. Therefore, just as in us the quality and movement of any limb—especially a principal one—affects the others, so the actions of the principal members in the world move everything else. The lower members easily receive from the higher ones, which are ready to give of their own accord. For the more powerful a cause is, the more ready it is to act, and therefore the more inclined it is to give. Thus, a small preparation on our part...

...applied moreover, is sufficient for receiving celestial gifts, provided that each person accommodates themselves especially to that celestial body to which they are especially subject. 

But before we consider the individual nature of each of us, we must consider the nature of the human species itself. The Arabic astrologers <note>original: "astrologi arabes"; Ficino frequently cites thinkers like Al-Kindi and Albumasar who synthesized Greek astrology with Islamic philosophy.</note> agreed that this species is Solar <note>Influenced by the Sun.</note>. I conjecture this to be true from man’s upright and beautiful posture, from his refined <term>humors|The four primary bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, black bile, and yellow bile) that Renaissance medicine believed governed health and personality.</term>, the clarity of his spirit, the sharpness of his imagination, and his pursuit of truth and glory. 

I add, moreover, a Mercurial nature to this, because of the vigorous movement of a versatile mind. And since the human race is born naked, unarmed, and in need of everything, it procures all these things for itself through its own industry—which is the specific characteristic of Mercury <note>The Roman god of wit, communication, and craft.</note>. I also add a Jovial <note>Influenced by Jupiter (Jove).</note> nature, because of the balanced <term>complexion|The specific "mixture" of qualities—hot, cold, moist, dry—within a body.</term> of the human body and the existence of laws. Furthermore, we receive life in the second month of gestation, over which Jupiter rules, and we are born in the ninth <note>original: "nouo," likely a transcription error for "nono" (ninth), the standard astrological month for birth under Jupiter's second rule.</note> month, when he again takes command. Therefore, the human species will be able to claim even greater gifts for itself from these three planets especially, if it aligns itself more and more every day to them through things associated with the Sun, Mercury, and Jupiter. 

But what of the others? Saturn does not easily signify the common quality and lot of the human race, but rather a person set apart from others: someone either divine or brutish, either blessed or crushed by extreme misery. Mars, the Moon, Venus affect...

...acts and actions common to humans and other living beings alike. Let us return, therefore, to the Sun, Jupiter, and Mercury. We have indeed spoken of some things pertaining to the Sun and Jupiter; but I know not how we overlooked things pertaining to Mercury.

...[Saturn] indeed rules over stability and perseverance; **Mars**, however, rules the efficiency of movement. We are sometimes compelled, for the sake of these qualities, to request protection from them—provided, of course, that the astrological times are similarly observed. From the former [Saturn], we seek favor through certain materials that are in some way earthy, dark, and leaden; also through dark jasper, magnetite, *camonum* <note>original: camonum; likely a reference to a type of onyx or carnelian used in cameos</note>, and chalcedony; and in some part through gold and golden marcasite <note>a mineral often used in alchemy, sometimes referring to iron pyrites</note>.

From **Mars**, however, we seek favor through fiery and red things, such as red copper, all sulfurous substances, iron, and bloodstone <note>original: lapidem sanguineum; a stone traditionally used to stem bleeding and associated with the planet Mars due to its color</note>. Nor should you doubt that Saturn has some share in gold, for it is thought to possess it on account of its weight. Furthermore, gold is similar to the **Sun** and is present in all metals, just as the Sun is present among all the planets and stars.

Now, if someone should argue that **Saturn** and **Mars** are harmful by nature—a claim I would never believe—they must nevertheless be used, just as physicians sometimes make use of poisons. This is what **Ptolemy** proves in the *Centiloquium* <note>original: Centiloquium; a medieval collection of 100 astrological aphorisms traditionally attributed to the astronomer Claudius Ptolemy</note>. Therefore, the power of Saturn will be beneficial when taken cautiously, much like the substances used by doctors that bind and contain; or even those that numb the senses, such as opium and mandrake.

The power of **Mars** may also be used, like euphorbia and hellebore <note>term: hellebore; a plant used in ancient medicine to treat "melancholy" or madness, though highly toxic</note>. The most cautious in this matter seem to have been the **Brahman** <note>the priestly and scholarly caste of India, admired in the Renaissance as "gymnosophists" or ancient sages</note> and **Pythagorean** magi. Because of their diligent study of philosophy, they feared the tyranny of Saturn...
