[Decorative red flourish] ->ON LIFE FROM HEAVEN<-

But also much more so through certain properties implanted in things from heaven, and hidden from our senses, and finally barely known to reason. For since properties of this kind and their effects cannot consist of elemental virtue <note>The physical powers of the four elements: earth, air, fire, and water.</note>, it follows that they proceed uniquely from the life and spirit of the world <term>spiritus mundi</term> <note>A subtle substance that Neoplatonists believed linked the physical world to the divine/celestial realms.</note> through the very rays of the stars. Therefore, through these rays, our own spirit is affected most greatly and most quickly, and is powerfully exposed to celestial influences. 

By this reasoning, surely the emerald, hyacinth, sapphire, topaz, ruby, unicorn horn, and especially the **bezoar stone** (as it is called among the Arabs) are endowed with hidden properties of "graces" <note>Special spiritual or medicinal powers.</note>. And therefore, they work not only when taken internally, but also if they touch the skin; once warmed there, they maintain their power and instill a celestial force into the human spirits. By this force, the spirits protect themselves against plague and poisons. 

The fact that these and similar things perform such feats by celestial virtue is proven by the argument that even when taken in a tiny weight, they have a massive effect. This is something hardly ever granted to a purely elemental quality <note>Physical qualities like heat or cold usually require a large amount of material to have a large effect.</note>, except perhaps for a very "heavenly" fire. For a material virtue, in order to act much, requires much material; but a **formal virtue** <term>forma</term> <note>In Renaissance philosophy, the "form" is the essential, non-physical blueprint of a thing that gives it its unique powers.</note> is most powerful even with the smallest amount of matter. 

By a similar virtue, the **Peony of Apollo** <note>original: "phębea peonia." The peony was sacred to Apollo (Phoebus), the god of healing.</note>, when touching the flesh, arms the spirit against the **falling sickness** <note>A historical term for epilepsy.</note> by a vapor infused into the spirit within. Similarly, coral and chalcedony work against the illusions of **black bile** <note>Melancholy or depression, thought to be caused by an excess of the "black bile" humor.</note>, especially those influences associated with Jupiter and Venus...

¶ They consecrate the **Agate stone** to Mercury. For this reason, physicians agree that it is effective for eloquence, improving vision, and acting against poisons. The **Aetites stone**, or the "Eagle-stone" <term>aetites: a "pregnant" stone containing another loose stone inside, traditionally used in midwifery</term>, receives its power from Lucina—that is, from Venus and the Moon—so that when it is moved toward the womb, it triggers birth quickly and very easily. Rhazes <note>Al-Razi, the famous 9th-century Persian physician</note>, with Serapio confirming, says that he has frequently experienced this himself.

By the virtue of the Sun, **ginger** infused in feasts wards off fainting. **Gentian** <note>original: "Sentiana"</note> calms the madness of a dog and puts snakes to flight. 

**Verbena** <note>vervain</note> is said to provide prophecy, joy, purifications, and vision. **Rue** and **zedoary** <note>a root similar to ginger</note> act as an antidote <term>theriac: a "universal" medicinal paste used against poison and disease</term> against poisons. **Frankincense** supports the vital and animal spirits against dullness, forgetfulness, and fear. **Sage** and **mint**, truly, by the virtue of Jupiter: the former puts paralysis to flight, while the latter strengthens the mind by its very scent. By the same virtue, **Cinquefoil** <note>original: "Pentaphillon," meaning five-leaf</note> resists...

...against poisons <note>original: "nenis", likely part of "venenis"</note>: and one of its leaves drunk twice daily in wine cures a one-day fever; three leaves cure a tertian fever <note>a fever that recurs every three days</note>; four, however, cure a quartan fever <note>a fever that recurs every four days</note>. Ancient priests used this herb in their purifications on account of its own purity. **Agnus Castus** <note>Also known as Chasteberry; the name literally means "Chaste Lamb"</note>, by the dominance of Saturn, halts sexual movement. **Jasper**, however, stops the flow of blood.

Wonderful effects are accustomed to occur when an elemental property serves a hidden property <note>In Renaissance medicine, "elemental" refers to the physical qualities of hot, cold, moist, or dry, while "hidden" or "occult" refers to powers granted by the stars</note>, just as in the **myrobalan** <note>a dried fruit used in medieval medicine for its astringent properties</note> used for strengthening the spirit and the body. Not only does that celestial virtue act, but also its great astringent power and its significant aromatic virtue, which wonderfully prevents putrefaction and dissolution and strengthens the spirit.

Indeed, so that **saffron** <note>original: "crocus"</note> may seek the heart, dilate the spirit, and provoke laughter, not only does the hidden power of the **Sun** wonderfully effect this, but the very nature of saffron—subtle, expansive, aromatic, and bright—leads to the same result. What I say regarding "simples" <note>medicines made from a single ingredient</note>, I wish to be understood as being said similarly about "composites" <note>medicines made from multiple ingredients</note>.

Tell me then, how does **Theriaca** <note>A famous ancient "universal antidote" consisting of dozens of ingredients, including viper flesh and opium</note> help us against poison? It does not simply draw out the poison. For it binds the bowels; nor does it entirely change the nature of the poison so suddenly. For the poison is not so weak and changeable. Rather, by a certain matching proportion, it suddenly strengthens the **vital spirit** <note>The "spiritus," a subtle substance that carries life through the body</note>—which is very thin and changeable—to such an extent that the spirit itself, now strong as an active agent, uses the theriaca like a tool to immediately overcome the poison, change it, and keep it away from the heart <note>original: "procordiis"</note>.

But by what proportion or virtue does theriaca accomplish this? By a virtue similar to that of **Jupiter** and **Phoebus** <note>The Sun</note>, which it acquires from the mixing of many things together according to a certain proportion...

I am speaking of the <term>styptic|astringent or contracting</term> and aromatic qualities. The former [the styptic] firms the spirit, while the latter [the aromatic] nourishes it. 

Therefore, <term>theriac|a complex ancient herbal jam used as a universal antidote and longevity tonic</term> has gained a marvelous power against encroaching old age and poison, with these three virtues working together toward the same end. One is indeed celestial, acquired through skillful mixing. Another is also celestial, but naturally inherent in its individual parts. The third is entirely elemental. But that first one I mentioned—the one acquired from the heavens—would be even more wondrous if it were not only blended in proportions related to <term>Jupiter and the Sun</term> <note>original: "Iouia Solariqʒ"</note> and from similar materials, but if a favorable time were also chosen for its preparation by observing the stars. For just as a body relates to place and time, so do motion and action relate to time. Therefore, just as specific bodies and their forms coalesce and are preserved in certain places and times, so too do certain actions gain their effectiveness from specific, proper times. <meta>This page continues the discussion on the influence of celestial harmony on earthly matters, specifically addressing the philosophical foundations of astrological timing (electional astrology).</meta>

->( ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS )<-

Proclus <note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was a Greek Neoplatonist philosopher who argued that earthly things "participate" in heavenly qualities.</note> explains this. Pythagoras <note>The famous Greek mathematician and philosopher who proposed the "harmony of the spheres."</note> indeed, turning his attention to this, named the good itself and the perfection of things "Opportunity." <note>original: "oportunitatem," referring to the concept of *Kairos*—the opportune or "right" moment for action.</note> For the first principle, according to Pythagoras and Plato, is the measure of all things, such that it has distributed different places and times to different bodies and actions. From this it follows that just as certain things are successfully born, grow together, and are preserved in no other place than here, and at no other time than then; so also material action, motion, or this or 그(that) outcome does not obtain efficacy and perfect effect at any other time than when the harmony of the heavens resonates together from all sides toward that same end. This harmony is indeed thought to possess such great power that it bestows a marvelous virtue <note>In this context, "virtue" means an inherent power or medicinal/magical efficacy.</note> not only upon the labors of farmers and the arts of physicians—achieved through herbs and spices—but even upon <term>images</term> <note>In Renaissance magic, "images" were astrological talismans made of specific metals or stones carved at precise moments to "catch" planetary influence.</note> which are made by astrologers from metals and stones. But images now require a chapter of their own.

As far as choosing the hours for actions and works is concerned, this is greatly confirmed by Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), the most influential astronomer and geographer of antiquity.</note> where he says in the *Centiloquium* <note>original: "centiloqo," a text of 100 astrological aphorisms then attributed to Ptolemy.</note>: "he who chooses what is better seems to differ in no way from one who has this by nature." In these words, he seems to confirm the power both of the celestial beings and of our own will and choice. Albertus Magnus <note>Albert the Great (c. 1200–1280), a medieval scholar and saint who wrote extensively on the natural sciences and astrology.</note> also says in the *Mirror*: <note>The *Speculum Astronomiae* (Mirror of Astronomy), a text defending the use of certain types of astrology within a Christian framework.</note> "For the freedom of the will is not restrained by the choice of a commendable hour; rather, in the beginnings of great things..."

## On the power of images according to the ancients, and of medicines acquired from heaven. Chapter XIII.

**P**tolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), the foundational authority on astronomy and astrology in the Middle Ages and Renaissance.</note> says in the *Centiloquium* <note>original: "centiloqo"; a famous collection of 100 astrological aphorisms attributed to Ptolemy.</note>, that the forms of lower things are subject to celestial faces. And he says that ancient wise men used to fabricate certain images at the time when planets entered similar faces in the heavens, which acted as patterns for the inferior things. This is indeed confirmed by Haly <note>Likely Haly Abenragel or Haly Abenrudian, influential Arabic astrologers.</note> in that same place, saying: a useful image of a serpent can be made when the Moon enters the celestial Serpent <note>The constellation Draco, or perhaps the Lunar Nodes often called the "head and tail of the dragon."</note>, or regards it favorably. Similarly, an effective image of a scorpion can be made when the Moon enters the sign of Scorpio, and this sign holds one of the four angles <note>The "angles" are the most powerful houses in a birth chart: the 1st, 4th, 7th, and 10th.</note>. He says that this was done in Egypt in his own time, and that he was present: where, from a seal of a scorpion made in a Bezoar stone <note>A "stone" or mass found in the digestive systems of animals, highly prized as a universal antidote to poison.</note> in this manner, the figure was impressed into frankincense; this was given in a drink to a person whom a scorpion had actually stung, and they were cured immediately. Hahamed the physician affirms that this is usefully performed, and Serapion confirms it. Furthermore, Haly relates that a wise man known to him there, by similar industry, created images that moved—the kind of thing we read was made, I know not how, by Archytas <note>Archytas of Tarentum, a Greek philosopher said to have built a steam-powered wooden pigeon.</note>. Such things Hermes Trismegistus <note>The legendary "Thrice-Great" founder of Hermeticism, alchemy, and magic.</note> also mentions: that the Egyptians were accustomed to make them from certain materials of the world, and were used to inserting the souls of Daemons <note>original: "Demonũ"; in this context, "daemons" refers to intermediary spirits or celestial intelligences, not necessarily "demons" in the modern sense.</note> into them at the opportune time; even the soul of his grandfather Mer-

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the animation of statues and the use of celestial influences, likely referencing Hermes Trismegistus (Mercury) from the preceding text.</meta>

->### ON THE LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS<-

...[Mer]cury; likewise of a certain Phoebus <note>The god Apollo</note>, and of Isis and Osiris; that they descended into statues in this way to be beneficial to men, or even harmful. This is similar to the story that Prometheus, through a certain clay model, snatched away life and celestial light. Furthermore, the Magi who follow Zoroaster used a certain golden ball <term>strophalos: a spinning top or wheel used in ancient Greek magic associated with the goddess Hecate</term> marked with celestial characters to call forth spirits from Hecate. A sapphire was inserted into it, and they turned it with a whip made of bull’s hide while chanting incantations. However, I will gladly omit the chants. For Psellus the Platonist <note>Michael Psellus, an 11th-century Byzantine scholar who wrote on demonology and Greek philosophy</note> also disapproves of them and mocks them.

The Hebrews also, raised in Egypt, learned to build the Golden Calf; as their own astrologers believe, this was done to capture the favor of Venus and the Moon against the influence of Scorpio and Mars, which was hostile to the Jews <note>Ficino is referencing the idea that the Golden Calf was an astrological talisman rather than purely an idol of worship</note>. Porphyry also, in his letter to Anebo, testifies that images are effective; he adds that aerial spirits <term>daemons: in the Platonic tradition, these are intermediary spirits between gods and humans, not necessarily evil</term> were immediately accustomed to being drawn in by certain vapors exhaling from their own specific fumigations.

Iamblichus confirms that in materials that are naturally in harmony with the higher powers—when these materials are opportunely and rightly collected and blended from all sides—the powers and effects not only of the heavens but also of spirits and divine beings can be received. Proclus and Synesius <note>Late Antique Neoplatonist philosophers</note> agree entirely. Indeed, there are wonderful works for health performed by physicians skilled in astrology through things...

...composed of many ingredients, that is, powders, liquids, ointments, and **electuaries** <note>A medicinal paste made by mixing powders with honey or syrup, commonly used in ancient and Renaissance medicine.</note> can be made. These seem to possess a more plausible rationale and are better understood than **"images"** <note>original: "imagines". In this context, these are astrological talismans or figurines carved into stone or wood to capture the power of specific stars or planets.</note>. 

This is first because powders, liquids, ointments, and electuaries—when prepared at the appropriate astrological moment—receive **celestial influences** more easily and quickly than the harder materials from which "images" are usually fashioned. <margin>material</margin> <margin>fated</margin> <margin>divine</margin> <margin>received</margin> <margin>which</margin> <margin>...</margin> <margin>...</margin> <margin>...</margin> <margin>...</margin> <margin>subtle</margin> <margin>and</margin> <margin>...</margin> <margin>heaven</margin> <margin>many</margin> <margin>...</margin> <margin>power</margin> <margin>internal</margin> <margin>remaining</margin> <margin>received</margin> <margin>as if</margin> <margin>similar</margin> <margin>plants</margin> <margin>within</margin> <margin>medicine</margin> <margin>almost</margin> <margin>vigor</margin> <note>The marginal notes are fragments of Latin words, likely serving as a quick index for the reader. They have been translated into their most likely English equivalents based on the stems.</note>

Secondly, it is because these medicines are either taken internally—having already been affected by the heavens—and are transformed into our very selves, or at least they are applied externally where they cling more firmly and finally penetrate the body. 

Furthermore, "images" are constructed from only one or very few ingredients. In contrast, those medicinal compounds can be blended from a great many things as one chooses. For example, if a hundred "gifts" of the **Sun** or **Jupiter** <note>The "gifts" refer to the specific virtues or physical qualities associated with these planets, such as vitality and heat from the Sun, or growth and justice from Jupiter.</note> were scattered across a hundred different plants, animals, and similar things, you could gather these hundred discovered items together and combine them into a single form. In such a mixture, you would seem to possess almost the entirety of the Sun or Jupiter. 

You surely know that a lower nature <note>Referring to the terrestrial world.</note> cannot grasp all the powers of a superior nature <note>The celestial or divine realm.</note> in just one single thing. Therefore, those powers are dispersed among the many various natures found here with us. Consequently, these powers can be more effectively collected through the work of **physicians** and similar practices than through the use of "images."

Accordingly, "images" carved from wood perhaps possess little power. For wood is perhaps too hard to easily receive celestial influence, and it is less tenacious in retaining it once received. Moreover, once it has been torn from the womb of Mother Earth, it loses almost all the vigor of its worldly life shortly thereafter, and easily [turns] into qualities...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how earthly substances receive and retain celestial influences, specifically focusing on the hierarchy of materials (the "Chain of Being") and why hard materials like stones and metals are particularly effective "receptacles" for heavenly power.</meta>

->[Decorative flourish] ON THE CELESTIAL LIFE<-

...is transmuted into another. Small stones and metals, however, although they seem harder for receiving a celestial gift, nevertheless retain it longer, as Iamblichus <note>original: "Iamblichus" — a 3rd-century Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote extensively on theurgy and how divine powers inhabit physical objects</note> confirms, once they have received it. Indeed, by their very hardness they retain for a very long time the traces and gifts of the world's life even after they have been uprooted—qualities they once possessed while they were still clinging to the earth. Therefore, for this reason at least, they are judged to be suitable materials for receiving and holding celestial things.

It is also probable, as I said in the previous book, that things so beautiful <note>Referring to precious gems and metals</note> could not be produced under the earth except by some great effort of the heavens; and that the power once impressed upon them by that effort endures. For the heaven has labored for a very long time in "cooking" and condensing these things.

But since you cannot easily combine many such things, you are forced to search diligently: which metal among the rest belongs most powerfully to the "order" <note>The "rank" or "category" of a specific planet's influence</note> of a certain star, or which stone is highest in that order, so that at least in one supreme thing of a whole genus or category, you might grasp the rest of its powers. By using such a receptacle, you might borrow celestial influences agreeable to it. 

For example, in the **Solar order**, under the "Phoeban man" <note>original: "homine phaebeo" — a person whose nature is dominated by the Sun/Apollo</note>, the highest place is held:

| Genus | Highest Examples |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Among animals** | The hawk or the cock |
| **Among plants** | Balsam or the laurel |
| **Among metals** | Gold |
| **Among stones** | The carbuncle <note>A glowing red gemstone, often garnet or ruby</note> or the pantaura <term>pantaura: a legendary "all-gold" magnetic stone described by ancient writers as having solar properties</term> |
| **Among elements** | Warm air |

For fire itself is considered to be "Martian" <note>belonging to the nature of the planet Mars</note>. When we said that the influx of the Sun, or Jupiter, or Venus should be increased, we understand this by means of the common... <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing "common relationship" or "common ratio"</note>

### The orders of things hanging from the stars, such as Solar things and similar; and how the spirit becomes Solar. Chapter 14.

**I** have indeed said elsewhere that from above, from each and every star (to speak in the Platonic manner), a series of things proper to it hangs down to the very lowest elements. Under the heart of the Scorpion <note>The star Antares</note> we can place demons <note>Intermediary spirits or 'daimons,' not necessarily evil</note> and men of that kind, as well as the animal called the scorpion. We can also place the herb Asterion—that is, the "star-herb"—which has the shape of a star and glows at night; <margin>property</margin> <margin>all things</margin> <margin>in...</margin> <margin>of the soul</margin> <margin>gemstones</margin> <margin>each</margin> <margin>Solar</margin> <margin>with...</margin> <margin>I have said</margin> <margin>being</margin> <margin>similar</margin> <margin>well</margin> <margin>another</margin> <margin>reasoning</margin> <margin>of them</margin> <margin>Proclus</margin> <margin>of the field</margin> <margin>of the field</margin> <margin>to be</margin> <margin>under</margin> <margin>of the son</margin> <margin>to be</margin> <margin>of the air</margin> <margin>of the stars</margin> <margin>of the goddess</margin> <margin>was</margin> physicians report that this herb has the quality of a rose and possesses a wonderful power against diseases of the genitals. 

Under the celestial Serpent or Serpent-bearer <note>The constellation Ophiuchus</note> they place Saturn, and in a certain way Jupiter. After that come the demons who often take on the forms of serpents. Likewise, men of a similar nature, living serpents, the herb serpentaria <note>Snakeweed</note>, the stone draconite (said to be born in the head of a dragon), and also what is commonly called serpentine stone. And besides these things, I shall bring forward more in what follows. 

Under the Solar star—that is, Sirius—they place the Sun first; then demons of a solar nature <note>Latin: phoebeos</note>, who Proclus <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote on the 'hierarchies' of the gods and stars</note> testifies have sometimes appeared to men in the form of lions or roosters. Following these are men and solar beasts of a very similar nature. Then solar plants, and likewise metals, and gemstones, and hot vapor and air. By a similar reasoning, they believe that from any star of the firmament, through some planet, a woven context of things descends step by step under its [authority]...

# Things that serve as images.
## Chapter XV.

**S**hould you have obtained those **little stones** <note>original: "lapillos"</note> which a little while ago we described as **Phoebian** <note>original: "phębeos"; belonging to Phoebus Apollo, the sun god, implying solar properties</note>, there will be no need to engrave images upon them. You shall therefore hang them from the neck, encased in gold with threads of saffron-colored silk, when the Sun passes through Aries or Leo, and is either rising or occupying the **midheaven** <note>original: "medium caelum"; the highest point in the sky in an astrological chart</note>, and is in aspect with the Moon.

¶ Proclus <note>A 5th-century Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote on "sympathy," the idea that earthly things are connected to heavenly ones</note> describes much more powerful little stones in the sequence of the Moon. The first is indeed **Selenite** <note>original: "Selinitim"; named for Selene, the Greek goddess of the Moon</note>. This stone not only imitates the Moon in its shape, but also in its motion, for it moves in a circle along with the Moon. If you happen to find this, and hang it from the neck encased in silver with a silver thread while the Moon is entering Cancer or Taurus and occupies the **angles** <note>original: "angulos"; the four most powerful points in a horoscope</note> suitable to her, you will thereafter make your own **spirit** <note>original: "spiritum"; in this context, the subtle vital force that links the body and soul</note> lunar. This occurs while the lunar stone itself, once warmed by your body, continuously sows its own virtue into your spirits.

¶ He [Proclus] recounts another little stone by the name of **helioselinon** <note>original: "helioselinon"; a Greek name combining "Helios" (Sun) and "Selene" (Moon)</note>. This stone naturally possesses the **image** <note>OCR ends with "imagin_"; likely "imaginem"</note> of the Sun and the Moon joined together...

<summary>This page explains how certain "solar" and "lunar" stones do not require engraving because they naturally possess celestial virtues, specifically describing how to wear them according to astrological timings to influence one's own vital spirit.</summary>

--- Page 95 ---
<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino's discussion on how to attract celestial influences through physical objects. He has been explaining how the world is a single living organism and how we can "gather" its powers.</meta>

->## ON THE CELESTIAL LIFE##<-

...to assemble the members <note>original: "Isidez congregare" — an allusion to the myth of Isis gathering the scattered pieces of Osiris, used here as a metaphor for collecting the fragmented powers of the cosmos.</note> as if they were those of Isis. But if only we might easily find a solar stone somewhere, or a lunar one—stones that are so very powerful in the hierarchy of those luminaries, just as we have the magnet and iron under the series of the northern pole.

Indeed, they say that Apollonius of Tyana <note>A famous Neopythagorean philosopher and wonder-worker from the 1st century AD.</note> found such a solar stone among the Indians, named the <term>Pantauras</term> <note>A legendary gemstone described by ancient writers as having the power to attract other stones, much like a magnet attracts iron.</note>, which gleams like fire. It is said to be born four paces deep under the earth, and to contain so much spirit that the stone swells, often causing the earth to split where such a stone is conceived. It draws other small stones to itself just as a magnet draws iron. 

But this "Herculean stone" <note>original: "lapis hic Herculeus" — a traditional name for the lodestone or magnet.</note> seizes us for contemplation even more vehemently at present. We see in the sailors' compass—that indicator of the pole—a balanced needle which, having been touched at its tip by a magnet, moves toward the Bear <note>The constellations Ursa Major or Ursa Minor, which designate the North Pole.</note>, because the magnet draws it thither. This occurs because the power of the Bear prevails in this stone; from the stone, this power is transferred into the iron, and thus the Bear draws them both. This power was not only infused into the magnet from the beginning, but it is also continually quickened by the rays of the Bear. 

Perhaps amber <note>original: "succinum"</note> behaves in a similar way toward the opposite pole and toward chaff <note>Ficino is observing static electricity; when rubbed, amber attracts light objects like straw or chaff.</note>. But tell me meanwhile: why does the magnet everywhere attract iron? It is not because they are similar; otherwise, a magnet would attract another magnet much more strongly, and iron would attract iron. Nor is it because the magnet is superior in the "order of bodies," for in fact, metal is considered superior to a mere small stone. What is the reason then? Both are indeed enclosed within the "order" that follows the Bear. But the magnet holds a higher rank in that specific property of the Bear, while the iron holds a lower...

<summary>Ficino discusses the occult properties of stones, comparing the legendary "Pantauras" stone to the magnet. He explains magnetic attraction as a result of the celestial influence of the North Star (the Bear) acting upon both the lodestone and iron.</summary>

<keywords>magnetism, lodestone, Apollonius of Tyana, Pantauras, Ursa Major, celestial influence, amber, sympathetic magic, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>The text continues a discussion on the sympathetic attraction between "higher" and "lower" entities, likely following a description of how magnets attract iron.</meta>

...indeed iron. Furthermore, in that same context of things, the higher entity draws that which is lower and turns it toward itself; or in some other way it agitates it, or affects it by a power previously infused. In turn, the lower is converted toward the higher by that same infusion, or is otherwise agitated, or entirely affected.

<note>The author is describing "sympathy," a Renaissance concept where everything in the universe is connected. Higher things (like planets) "infuse" their power into lower things (like minerals or people).</note>

Thus, in the solar series <note>the hierarchy of things influenced by the Sun</note>, the lower person admires the higher. In the Jovian order <note>related to the planet Jupiter</note>, he venerates him. In the Martian order <note>related to Mars</note>, he fears him. 

In the Venusian order <note>related to Venus</note>, the lower is seized toward the higher by the fire of love and abandons himself. In the Mercurial order <note>related to Mercury</note>, this one always learns from or is persuaded by that one. In the Lunar order <note>related to the Moon</note>, motion is more often initiated by the higher in the lower. In the Saturnine order <note>related to Saturn</note>, rest is initiated.

As for me, since I had explored these matters thus far, I was greatly pleased. While I was still a young man, I thought to engrave the figure of the Celestial Bear <note>Ursa Major, the Big Dipper</note> onto a magnet as best I could, at a time when the Moon would look favorably upon it; and then I intended to hang it from my neck by an iron thread. I hoped that by doing this, I would finally come to possess the power of that star. 

But when I had explored the matter more deeply, I eventually found that the influences of that star were mostly Saturnine and Martian <note>Saturn and Mars were traditionally considered "malefic" or difficult planets in astrology</note>. I learned from the Platonists that evil <term>daemons<note>original: "daemones"; here referring to intermediary spirits that can be either good or bad, though the author specifies these are "mali" or evil.</note></term> are mostly northern. This the Hebrew astronomers also admit, placing harmful and Martian spirits in the north, but propitious and Jovian spirits in the south. I learned from the theologians and from Iamblichus <note>A 4th-century Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote extensively on rituals and theurgy</note> that the makers of talismans <note>original: "imaginum fictores", literally 'makers of images'</note> are often possessed by evil spirits and deceived. 

I indeed saw a small stone...

->[Blue initial D] **ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS.**<-

...to Florence, brought from India. It was extracted there from the head of a dragon, round in the shape of a coin, and naturally marked with a great many dots like stars. When doused with vinegar, it moved slightly in a straight line, or rather obliquely; soon it was carried in a circle until the vapor of the vinegar evaporated. I indeed judged that a small stone of this kind possesses the nature and, as it were, the figure of the **celestial Dragon** <note>original: "caelestis draconis"; this refers to the constellation Draco</note>; it even receives its motion, insofar as through the spirit of the vinegar or a stronger wine, it is made more familiar to that Dragon or the firmament. Therefore, I believe that whoever might wear this stone and douse it often with vinegar would perhaps receive some power from that Dragon, which entwines with twin coils—the Great Bear on one side and the Lesser Bear on the other.

¶ There also exists near the constellation **Scorpio** the **Serpent-bearer** <term>Ophiuchus; an ancient constellation representing a man grasping a snake</term>, who appears as a man girded by a serpent: holding the head of the serpent in his right hand and the tail in his left, with his knees as if bent and his head tilted slightly back. I have read indeed that the Magi <note>Ancient Persian priest-astrologers</note> advised the King of the Persians to engrave this image upon a **hematite** stone <note>original: "emathiti"; a reddish-black iron oxide mineral</note>, which he should then enclose in a gold ring. He was to do this, however, in such a way that he inserted the root of **Serpentaria** <term>likely Dragonwort or Snakeroot; a plant believed to cure snakebites</term> between the small stone and the gold. For one wearing this ring would be safe against poison and poisonous diseases—provided, of course, that you make it while the **Moon** is looking upon the Serpent-bearer. **Pietro d'Abano** <note>original: "Petrus Aponensis"; a 13th-century physician and astrologer famous for his "Conciliator," who explored the boundaries between science and magic</note> confirmed the power of this image. I, however, believe that if that ring has such power, it is not so much due to the figure engraved upon it as it is through materials of this kind; and in this way...

<meta>The previous page discussed the influences of the stars on earthly substances. This page continues the argument by contrasting natural alchemical processes with the artificial creation of astrological talismans.</meta>

-> ### ON THE CELESTIAL LIFE <-

...which we assigned in the preceding chapters; partly because many things of this kind, when they are gathered by a physician and an astrologer, are crushed, mixed together, and <term>concocted</term> <note>original Latin: *concoquuntur*. In Renaissance medicine, this refers to a process of "cooking" or ripening substances using heat to reach a state of perfection or medicinal potency.</note> under a specific star. During this process, through the method of "cooking" and fermentation, they gradually take on a new form. They acquire this very form through a certain celestial kindling, while the stellar rays are then acting within them; for this reason, the form itself is called celestial.

But a metal or a small stone, when it is engraved in a single moment, does not seem to receive a new quality, but only a new shape. Nor does that transformation proceed through the necessary stages of <term>digestion</term> <note>original: *digestionis*. Here, it means the slow, natural transformation of matter, similar to how the body processes food.</note> that natural change and generation usually observe. Since celestial nature—acting as a guide for our lower nature—usually proceeds with a certain natural pace and "breathes life" into things as they develop in that way, most people rightly doubt that <term>images</term> <note>astrological talismans or sigils engraved on stones or metals to capture planetary power</note> of this kind possess any celestial power. 

I myself often doubt this; and if it were not that all of antiquity and all astrologers believed they possessed a wonderful power, I would deny they had it. I would not, however, deny it entirely. For I believe—unless someone persuades me otherwise—that they possess at least some power for maintaining good health, especially due to the nature of the specific material chosen. Nevertheless, I judge that a much greater power resides in medicines and ointments that have been "breathed upon" by the favor of the stars. I will explain in the following sections what I meant just now by "the nature of the chosen material."

Those things which, according to the opinions of the <term>Magi</term> <note>the "wise men" or priest-philosophers of the ancient world, often associated with natural magic</note> or astrologers, can be cited in favor of images to interpret <term>Plotinus</term> <note>a major 3rd-century Neoplatonist philosopher who influenced Ficino's views on the soul and the universe</note>, I will briefly present next—provided I first warn you here: do not...

Furthermore, if you judge it permissible for a man to use inferior things <note>earthly materials like herbs or stones</note> for prosperous health, judge it permissible to use superior things <note>celestial influences</note> as well. And thus, it is permissible to temper inferior things according to the rule of superior things by the physician’s skill, just as they were tempered by God from the beginning.

### On the power of the heavens. On the strength of the rays, from which power images are thought to be strengthened.
## Chapter XVI.

**T**he almost immense magnitude, power, and motion of celestial bodies cause all the rays of all the stars to penetrate the mass of the earth—which is like a mere point compared to the heavens—instantly and very easily, straight through to the center; a fact which all astronomers admit. And there, as it pleases the **Pythagoreans** and **Platonists** <note>Followers of Pythagoras and Plato who believed in a mathematically ordered universe and the "World Soul"</note>, they [the rays] are strongest: both because they touch the center straight from every direction, and because they are all collected together into a narrow space. By their intensity, the dry matter of the earth there, far removed from moisture, is utterly ignited; and being ignited, it is thinned and dispersed: it breathes out fires and sulfur alike through passages on every side. But they think this fire is very dark, and like a certain conflagration devoid of light; just as in the heavens there exists a light devoid of conflagration. Fire, however, between the celestial...

<meta>This page marks the beginning of Book I of Marsilio Ficino’s *De Vita Libri Tres* (Three Books on Life). In this opening preface, Ficino addresses his patron, Lorenzo de' Medici, and introduces the playful yet medical tone of the work through the mythological figure of Bacchus.</meta>

# Preface of Marsilio Ficino the Florentine to the Book *On Life*, dedicated to the magnanimous Lorenzo de' Medici, Savior of the Country.

**B**<margin>b</margin> ACCHUS <note>Bacchus is the Roman name for Dionysus, the god of wine and divine ecstasy.</note> is sung of by the poets as the high priest of priests and the "twice-born." Perhaps they signify by this that a future priest must be reborn immediately upon being initiated; or that the mind of a perfected priest, being deeply intoxicated by God, already appears to have been reborn. 

Or perhaps, in a humbler sense, they mean that wine—the "offspring" of Bacchus—is generated once in the vine, just as Bacchus was in his mother Semele <note>In Greek myth, Semele was the mother of Dionysus; she died when Zeus revealed his lightning, but the unborn god was saved.</note>, with its clusters of grapes ripening under the sun <note original: "Phębo">. Then, it is reborn a second time after the "lightning bolt" of the vintage season, maturing in its own vessel just as pure wine was once kept in the thigh of Jove <note original: "Iouis femore"; referring to the myth where Zeus/Jupiter sewed the infant Dionysus into his thigh until he was ready to be born.</note>. 

But we must not speak of sacred mysteries at the present moment, for we are soon to assist those who are weary with physical <term>natural or medical</term> aid instead. Nor should we proceed with a style enslaved to gravity; rather, let it be free <note original: "libero"> and playful, since we have somehow begun our work with Father Liber <note original: "Libero patre"; Ficino is making a pun on the god's name, Liber, and the Latin word for 'free'.</note>. 

And I say "somehow" rightly so; for perhaps a wiser man would have begun a medical work with Phoebus <note original: "Phębo"; Apollo, the god of medicine and the sun</note>, the first of doctors, rather than with Bacchus. But what of it? If there is any omen in my mouth now, by some chance bringing forth the name of Bacchus, let it be this: Bacchus heals, perhaps more healthfully through a certain nourishing wine and a most joyful sense of security, than Phoebus does with his herbs and incantations. 

In whatever sense you take these things, Bacchus, that leader of priests, [possesses] twin...

<summary>In this preface, Ficino uses the mythology of Bacchus (the god of wine) to introduce his medical treatise, arguing that joy and "divine intoxication" are as vital to health as traditional herbal medicine. He dedicates the work to his patron, Lorenzo de' Medici, framing the scholarly life as one requiring both physical and spiritual renewal.</summary>

<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, Lorenzo de' Medici, Bacchus, Phoebus Apollo, medical philosophy, Renaissance humanism, wine as medicine, rebirth, Semele</keywords>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication of Marsilio Ficino's Three Books on Life (De Vita Libri Tres) to Lorenzo de' Medici. Ficino is explaining his "double lineage" and how he came to write books on both philosophy and medicine.</meta>

...is said to have had, as it were, two mothers. But Melchizedek, that highest priest, had scarcely one mother and one father. <note>Melchizedek is a mysterious biblical figure often described as "without genealogy"; Ficino contrasts this divine singularity with the dual nature of his own life's work.</note> I, the least of priests, have had two fathers: Ficino the physician and Cosimo de' Medici. <note>Marsilio's biological father was Diotifeci d'Agnolo, the personal physician to the Medici family. Cosimo de' Medici was his patron and spiritual father.</note> From the former I was born; from the latter I was reborn. The former commended me to Galen—both as a physician and a follower of Plato <note>original: "tum medico, tum Platonico"</note>. But the latter [Cosimo] consecrated me to the divine Plato. And this one [Cosimo], just like the other [my biological father], destined Marsilio for medicine. Galen, indeed, for the medicine of bodies; but Plato, truly, a <term>physician of souls</term> <note>A core Neoplatonic idea that philosophy serves to heal the spirit just as medicine heals the flesh.</note>.

**I** <note>original: Red initial "I"</note> have for a long time, therefore, under Plato, practiced the salutary medicine of souls. When, after the translation of all his books, I soon composed eighteen books on the immortality of souls and eternal happiness <note>This refers to Ficino's massive philosophical work, the "Platonic Theology."</note>: in this way, I satisfied, to the best of my strength, my father the physician. Thinking next that I must satisfy my medicinal father <note>Meaning either his biological father's legacy or the medical tradition itself.</note>, I composed a book on caring for the health of scholars. <note>This is Book I of this volume, "On Caring for the Health of Men of Letters."</note> Furthermore, after these things, scholarly men desired not only to be well at certain times, but also, while being well, to live a long life. To these people, therefore, I then gave a book on long life. <note>Book II of this volume.</note> However, they distrusted earthly medicines and remedies in such a great matter. I added a book on obtaining a life both healthy and long from the heavens <note>Book III, "On Obtaining Life from the Heavens," which discusses astrological influences and the "spirit" of the world.</note>: so that from the living <term>body of the world</term> <note>original: "mundi corpe uiuo" — referring to the Anima Mundi (World Soul) which Ficino believed animated the entire universe.</note> itself, a certain more vigorous life might be propagated into our body—as if into some limb of the world—just as if from a vine. I pray, most indulgent Lorenzo, forgive these books of medicine: if, while I wish to be a physician, I know not how, even without wishing...

<summary>Marsilio Ficino explains his dual heritage from his biological father (a physician) and Cosimo de' Medici, justifying his transition from writing pure philosophy to writing medical and astrological works for the benefit of scholars.</summary>
<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, Cosimo de' Medici, Lorenzo de' Medici, Galen, Plato, medicine of souls, De Vita Libri Tres, Platonic Theology, World Soul, health of scholars</keywords>

<meta>This page appears to be a dedicatory preface from Marsilio Ficino to Lorenzo de' Medici for his work "De Vita Libri Tres" (Three Books on Life). It follows a discussion on the intersection of medicine and the arts.</meta>

# ON THE HEALTHY LIFE

...even if I am not often a good poet. For even Phoebus <note>original: "Phębus"; another name for Apollo, the Greco-Roman god of both healing and music.</note> is the same discoverer of medicine and master of poetry: he bestows both of his arts upon us, not so much through herbs as through the lyre and song. Furthermore, Venus herself, according to the astrologers, produces the musician and the physician equally. But enough of this for now. While I carefully look after the lives of scholars and similar citizens, I neglect the health of my books, as long as I allow them to remain separated from one another. Therefore, out of duty toward them, I now for the first time join them into one body. Since its limbs are now joined into one form, let life be present in it immediately. 

However, this <term>physical work</term> <note>The author is punning on "physical" meaning both "about physics/medicine" and "having a material body."</note>—which is, as it were, my own body—cannot receive life unless it is mine. Truly, life of this sort depends solely on my soul. This soul, however, has for a long time lived with you, magnanimous Lorenzo <note>Lorenzo de' Medici (1449–1492), the de facto ruler of the Florentine Republic and Ficino's patron.</note>, my patron: especially in that part of your most magnificent house where, together with Plato, that work of ours on the <term>immortality of souls</term> <note>Refers to Ficino's major philosophical work, the "Platonic Theology."</note> is kept, which was dedicated to your name long ago. 

But that soul of mine, although it leads a life in a certain blessed homeland, as it were, with you, is nevertheless restless in the meantime (as the theologians maintain) until it receives this physical work as its own body. Accept therefore, best Lorenzo, after those books on the soul, these books also on the body; and with the same inspiration with which you favored those earlier, now breathe favorably upon these. For in this way, this "body" will live under your spirit, by its own soul; and in turn, our soul, now united with this body of its own, will find rest within your household.

<meta>This is the opening page of Marsilio Ficino's "De vita libri tres" (Three Books on Life), published in 1489. It contains the general title, the breakdown of the three books, and the beginning of the dedication to his friends and his patron, Lorenzo de' Medici.</meta>

# THE GENERAL TITLE

->The **Book of Life**. Divided into Three Books. The First: on a **healthy life**. The Second: on a **long life**. The Third: on a **life from the heavens** <note>original: "uita cęlitus"; this book discusses astrological and spiritual health</note>.<-

## On the care of the health of those who devote themselves to the study of letters.

**MARSILIO Ficino** the Florentine, to **Giorgio Antonio Vespucci** <note>A Dominican friar and tutor to Amerigo Vespucci</note> and **Giovanni Battista Boninsegni**, men distinguished for their integrity and learning, **Sends Greetings** <note>original: "S.D." for 'Salutem dicit', a standard Latin greeting</note>. 

We have conversed much with you in these times, walking about in the Peripatetic manner <note>following the tradition of Aristotle's "Peripatetic" school, where philosophy was discussed while walking</note>, concerning the care of the health of those who continually apply themselves to the study of letters. I have decided to commend these matters to you especially, condensed into a brief summary. Nor will I myself approve of this little work until I have learned that it has been approved by you, who are most esteemed men and friends; nor will I allow it to undergo the most refined judgment of our **Lorenzo de' Medici** <note>The de facto ruler of Florence and Ficino's primary patron</note>. For this work will, above all, provide for his prosperous health if it should be needed. Indeed, it will hardly ever be seen to look after our scholars here unless it has first looked after their patron, our **Maecenas** <note>referring to Gaius Maecenas, the famous Roman patron of poets; Ficino uses this as a title for Lorenzo</note>. Read, therefore, diligently; and take care of your health...

<summary>This page serves as the frontispiece and dedication for Ficino's "Three Books on Life," outlining its structure and addressing his colleagues regarding the unique health risks faced by intellectuals.</summary>
<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, health of scholars, Lorenzo de' Medici, Renaissance medicine, Peripatetic philosophy, Humanism, Giorgio Antonio Vespucci</keywords>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page appears to be from a Renaissance-era treatise on the scholar's life, likely Marsilio Ficino’s "Three Books on Life" (De Vita Libri Tres), specifically the section regarding health and the astrological/philosophical guides for students.</meta>

-> ON THE HEALTHY LIFE <-

...most diligently. Once health is taken away, the sublime doors of the Muses <note>The nine Greek goddesses of the arts and sciences, representing the highest levels of intellectual and creative pursuit.</note>—unless Almighty God by some wondrous power both leads and opens them—either are never reached by us at all, or are certainly knocked upon in vain. Indeed, we wish this physical <term>physical: Here referring to "Physica," the study of nature and the body</term> discussion of ours to look primarily at this argument: namely, that if for the sake of attaining wisdom, bodily health is so greatly to be sought, much more must the health of the mind—by which alone wisdom can be grasped—be sought. Otherwise, those who strive to capture wisdom with an unhealthy mind seek knowledge altogether ignorantly. Hippocrates <note>The ancient Greek physician often called the "Father of Medicine."</note> promises health of the body, but Socrates <note>The famous Athenian philosopher, representing here the health of the soul or character.</note> promises health of the soul. But only He <note>Referring to Jesus Christ.</note> provides the true health of both, who exclaims: "Come to me, all you who labor and are burdened, and I will refresh you." <note>original Latin: "Venite ad me omnes qui laboratis, & onerati estis: atque ego vos reficiam." (Matthew 11:28)</note> "I am the way, the truth, and the life." <note>original Latin: "Ego sum via, veritasque & vita." (John 14:6)</note>

-> The Nine Leaders of Scholars <-

**W**hoever enters upon that rough, arduous, and long journey—which indeed barely leads at last to the lofty temple of the nine Muses through constant labor—seems to stand in need of exactly nine leaders for this journey. Of these, the first three lead us in heaven; the following three in the soul; and the last three on earth. First, in heaven, Mercury <note>The Roman messenger god, associated here with the planet Mercury and the intellectual spark of inquiry.</note> either impels or exhorts us to begin the journey of the Muses by investigating. For to Mercury is attributed the task of investi-

<margin>a.iiii.</margin> <note>This is a signature mark, a printer's code used to ensure the pages were bound in the correct order.</note>

<summary>This page argues that mental and physical health are essential prerequisites for wisdom, identifying Christ as the ultimate healer and introducing a system of nine guides—beginning with Mercury—who lead scholars toward intellectual mastery.</summary>

<keywords>health, wisdom, Muses, Hippocrates, Socrates, Mercury, scholarship, Nine Guides, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the intellectual and spiritual "guides" necessary for a scholar to succeed, likely from Marsilio Ficino's 'Three Books on Life' (De Vita Libri Tres), specifically focusing on the health of students.</meta>

...the whole duty of [investi]gation. Then **Phoebus** <note>original: "Phębus"; another name for Apollo, the god of light, reason, and the sun.</note> himself illuminates both the seeking minds and the things sought with most abundant splendor, so that what was being sought is clearly found by us. Most gracious **Venus**, mother of the Graces, approaches; and with her wholly nourishing and joyful rays, she so seasons and adorns every matter that whatever was sought through the prompting of **Mercury** <note>The god of messages, eloquence, and inquiry.</note> and had already been found through the guidance of Phoebus—now surrounded by a certain wonderful and healthful beauty of Venus—delights and benefits us always.

There follow three guides for this journey within the soul: namely, an **ardent and stable will**, **sharpness of intellect**, and a **tenacious memory**. The final three are found here on earth: a most prudent <term>paterfamilias</term> <note>The male head of a household, responsible for the discipline and physical environment of the family.</note>, a most proven teacher, and a most skilled physician. Without these nine guides, no one has ever been able, nor will ever be able, to reach the temple of the nine **Muses** <note>The Greek goddesses of inspiration in the arts and sciences.</note>. 

Almighty God and nature especially granted us the first six guides from our beginning. However, our own diligence must provide the last three. Many of the ancients and wise men have treated the rules and duties pertaining to the head of the household and the teacher regarding literary studies—especially "our" **Plato**, both frequently elsewhere and most diligently in his books *The Republic* and *The Laws*. Then **Aristotle** in his *Politics*. **Plutarch** and **Quintilian** also did so excellently. 

But thus far, students of letters have lacked only a **physician** who might extend a hand to those on their journey.

<summary>The author identifies nine essential 'guides' for the scholar—three divine (Mercury, Apollo, Venus), three internal (will, intellect, memory), and three earthly (head of house, teacher, doctor)—noting that while education and household management are well-documented by the ancients, the medical care of scholars has been neglected.</summary>

[In left margin: P]

**P**rincipally, just as much care as runners are accustomed to take of their legs, athletes of their arms, and musicians of their voices, at least that much care should students of letters have for the brain, the heart, the liver, and the stomach. Indeed, even more so, insofar as these organs are more excellent than those limbs, and scholars use these organs more frequently and for more important matters than those men use their limbs. Furthermore, any skilled craftsman cares for his instruments most diligently: the painter his brushes; the blacksmith his hammers and anvils; the soldier his horses and weapons; the hunter his dogs and birds; the lute-player <note>original: "citharedus"</note> his lute; and everyone else likewise. Only the priests of the Muses <note>a metaphorical term for scholars and philosophers</note>, only those seekers of the highest good...

<summary>This page introduces Marsilio Ficino's medical-philosophical treatise, urging scholars to care for their physical health—specifically the brain, heart, and stomach—with the same diligence a craftsman shows his tools.</summary>

--- Page 114 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the intellectual life, moving from general philosophical pursuits to the specific physiological "instrument" required for contemplation: the spirit.</meta>

...and hunters of truth are so negligent—oh, the shame!—and so unfortunate that they seem to entirely neglect that **instrument** by which they are able, in a sense, to measure and grasp the whole universe.

An instrument of this kind is the **spirit** itself. Among doctors, this is defined as a certain pure, subtle, warm, and clear vapor of the blood. Created from the more refined parts of the blood by the heat of the heart, it flies to the brain; there, the mind <note>original: "animus"</note> assiduously uses it to exercise both the internal and external senses. Therefore, the blood serves the spirit; the spirit serves the senses; and finally, the senses serve reason. 

Moreover, blood is produced by the **natural power** <note>original: "virtute naturali"</note> which flourishes in the liver and stomach. The thinnest part of the blood flows into the fountain of the heart, where the **vital power** <note>original: "vitalis virtus"</note> flourishes. From there, the created spirits ascend to the brain and—if I may put it this way—to the **citadels of Pallas Athena** <note>Pallas Athena was the Greek goddess of wisdom; the author uses this metaphor to describe the brain as the seat of the intellect.</note>. In these citadels, the **animal power** <note>original: "animalis... vis". From the Latin "anima" (soul); this refers to the "soul-power" that governs sensing and movement.</note>—that is, the power of sensing and moving—holds dominion.

Thus, the quality of one's contemplation is almost entirely dependent on the service of the senses. Furthermore, the senses are only as good as the spirit. The spirit, in turn, is of the same quality as the blood and those three powers we mentioned: namely, the **natural**, the **vital**, and the **animal**, by which, through which, and in which the spirits themselves are conceived, born, and nourished.

**Scholars are subject to phlegm and black bile.** <note>original: "pituite & atre bili". In the system of the Four Humors, black bile was associated with Melancholy, the "scholar's disease."</note>

Indeed, men who are desirous of learning must care most diligently not only for those limbs and powers, and the spirits—

...they owe me; but they are also commanded to most cautiously avoid phlegm and black bile, no differently than sailors avoid Scylla and Charybdis <note>In Greek mythology, two sea monsters located on opposite sides of a narrow strait; to avoid one was to risk the other.</note>. For as much as they are at leisure in the rest of their body, so much are they busy in their brain and mind. Because of this, they are compelled to produce phlegm, which the Greeks call <term>phlegma</term> <note>original: "flegma"; one of the four bodily humors, traditionally considered cold and moist.</note>. From this they also produce black bile, which those same people call <term>melancholy</term> <note>original: "melancolia"</note>. The former [phlegm] indeed often blunts and suffocates the intellect. But the latter [black bile], if it should be too abundant or catch fire <note>referring to "adust" or burnt melancholy, a state believed to cause frenzy</note>, vexes the soul with constant care and frequent delusions, and disturbs the judgment. So it may be said with good reason that scholars would be exceptionally healthy were it not for the trouble of phlegm; and they would be the most joyful and wisest of all, if they were not compelled by the fault of black bile to often grieve or sometimes lose their wits.

### How many causes there are by which scholars are or become melancholic.

**A**s for why scholars are melancholic, there are three primary types of causes. The first is heavenly; the second is natural; the third is human. 

The **Heavenly** cause exists because Mercury—who invites us to investigate doctrines—and Saturn—who brings it about that we persevere in investigating doctrines and preserve what we have found—are said by astronomers to be, in a certain way, cold and dry. Or, if perhaps Mercury is not cold, he is nevertheless often extremely dry due to his proximity to the Sun. Such is the nature that doctors call <term>melancholic</term>. Mercury himself and Saturn bestow this same nature upon their followers, the students of letters...

<summary>This page explains how the scholarly lifestyle produces phlegm and black bile, leading to mental distress, and begins to categorize the three causes of scholarly melancholy: celestial, natural, and human.</summary>
<keywords>Melancholy, Black Bile, Phlegm, Saturn, Mercury, Health of Scholars, Marsilio Ficino, Astrology, Humoral Theory</keywords>

--- Page 116 ---
they impart from the beginning, and preserve and increase it daily.

¶ The natural cause, however, seems to be this: that for the pursuit of sciences—especially those that are difficult—it is necessary for the soul to withdraw from external things to internal ones, as if moving from a certain circumference toward the center. And while it contemplates, it remains most stably fixed in that very "center of man," if I may call it that. 

Truly, to gather oneself from the circumference toward the center and to be fixed in that center is the particular property of the Earth itself, to which <term>black bile</term> <note>original: "atra bilis"; in ancient and Renaissance medicine, this was one of the four "humors" or bodily fluids. While often associated with sadness, it was also believed to be the source of deep, scholarly focus.</note> is very similar. Therefore, black bile provokes the soul to both gather itself into one and to stay fixed in that unity, so that it may contemplate continually. And being itself like the center of the world, it compels the investigation toward the very center of individual things. It carries the soul upward to comprehend the highest matters, since indeed it is most in harmony with <term>Saturn</term> <note>Saturn was traditionally the planet of the "melancholic" temperament. It was considered the highest of the planets, representing the furthest reaches of the intellect and the passage of time.</note>.

...is. Therefore, as the thinner and clearer parts of the blood are often consumed <note>original: "subtilioribus clarioribusque sanguis partibus." All these things tend to produce a <term>melancholic spirit|the subtle gases or "spirits" in the body that transmit thought and emotion</term> and a sorrowful and fearful mind. <meta>This page continues the introductory arguments regarding the relationship between genius and melancholy, specifically referencing the classical authorities of Democritus, Plato, and Aristotle mentioned in the preceding discourse.</meta>

# ON THE HEALTHY LIFE

Democritus, Plato, and Aristotle undoubtedly held this view. However, they do not seem to have sufficiently explained the underlying reason for such a significant matter. Nevertheless, with God as our guide, we must dare to investigate the causes.

¶ **Melancholy** <note>original: "Melancolía"</note>—that is, **black bile** <note>original: "atra bilis"</note>—is of two types. One is called "natural" by physicians. The other occurs through **combustion** <term>adustion: the process where a bodily humor is "overcooked" or burnt by excessive heat, changing its nature from helpful to harmful</term>. That natural kind is nothing other than a denser and drier part of the blood. The "burnt" kind, however, is divided into four species: it is produced by the combustion of either natural melancholy, or of the purer blood, or of yellow bile, or of salty phlegm. 

Whichever type is born of combustion harms judgment and wisdom. Indeed, while that **humor** <note>The four "humors" (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) were believed to govern human health and temperament.</note> is ignited and burns, it tends to make people agitated and raging. This is what the Greeks call *mania* <note>original: "maniam"</note>; we call it madness. But when the heat is finally extinguished, and the subtler and clearer parts have dissolved, only a foul soot remains. This makes people dull and stupid. This state is what they call melancholy, which is nearly the same as madness and senselessness.

¶ Therefore, only that **black bile** which we called "natural" leads us toward judgment and wisdom. Yet even this is not always the case. To be sure, if it exists alone in an excessively dark and dense mass, it darkens the **spirits** <term>spiritus: in Renaissance medicine, these are the subtle, airy vapors that act as the bridge between the physical body and the soul</term>, terrifies the mind, and blunts the intellect. 

¶ But if it is mixed with simple phlegm—once the cold blood has settled around the heart—it brings on sluggishness and torpor through a certain thick coldness. And as of each densest...

<summary>Marsilio Ficino distinguishes between "natural" melancholy (dense blood) and "burnt" melancholy (adustion), arguing that while the former can foster wisdom, the latter leads to mania or stupidity.</summary>
<keywords>melancholy, black bile, adustion, humors, mania, medical philosophy, Marsilio Ficino, natural melancholy</keywords>

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the physiological and psychological effects of the humors, specifically focusing on the "matter" of melancholy or black bile.</meta>

The nature of the matter is that when melancholy of this kind grows cold, it reaches the highest degree of chill. In this state, nothing is hoped for; all things are feared; one grows weary of looking up at the vault of the heavens.

¶ If black bile—whether simple or mixed—putrefies, it produces a quartan fever <term>quartan fever: a type of malaria characterized by a paroxysm that occurs every fourth day, historically attributed to an excess of black bile</term>, tumors of the spleen, and many ailments of the same kind. When it overflows excessively, whether alone or joined with phlegm <term>phlegm: one of the four bodily humors, associated with cold and moisture</term>, it makes the spirits <term>spirits: the "vital spirits," a vaporous substance thought to circulate through the body to facilitate life and thought</term> thicker and colder. It immediately affects the soul with weariness. It dulls the sharpness of the mind; nor does the blood "leap around the heart" <note>original: "neque salit arcadico circum praecordia sanguis" — a poetic way of describing sluggish circulation and lack of intellectual vitality</note>. 

It is necessary, however, that the black bile not be so scarce that the blood, yellow bile, and spirits lack a "bridle" <note>Renaissance medicine suggested that black bile provided the necessary weight and stability to prevent a flighty or unstable personality</note>. This would lead to an unstable intellect and a fleeting memory. Nor should it be so abundant that, weighed down by excessive weight, we seem to doze off and stand in need of "spurs" <note>metaphorical encouragement to action or thought</note>. Therefore, it is absolutely necessary that it be as refined and subtle as its nature allows. For if it is thinned out as much as possible by its own nature, it might perhaps even be present in large quantities without harm. Indeed, it could be so plentiful that it seems to equal the yellow bile at least in weight. 

¶ Let black bile abound, then, but let it be extremely refined. It should not lack the surrounding moisture of thinner phlegm, lest it dry out completely and become excessively hard. Yet it should not be mixed entirely with phlegm—especially if that phlegm is quite cold or abundant—lest it become chilled. Instead, let it be mixed with yellow bile and blood in such a way that one single substance is formed from the three, with twice as much blood as the other two... <note>the text cuts off mid-proportion</note>

...composed of a portion. Where eight parts of blood, two of yellow bile, and two portions of black bile exist. Let the black bile be kindled somewhat by those two, and once kindled, let it shine; let it not be burned. This is lest it—just as a harder material is prone to do when it boils too much—burns and agitates more vehemently. While, indeed, when it cools down, it likewise becomes cold to the extreme. 

For black bile <term>atra bilis; the humor associated with the earth element and a melancholic temperament</term> is like iron: when it is stretched far toward cold, it is cold to the extreme; when, on the contrary, it turns strongly toward heat, it is hot to the extreme. Nor should it seem strange that black bile can be kindled easily and, once kindled, burn more vehemently. Since we see lime <note>The author refers to quicklime, which reacts violently and generates intense heat when touched by water</note>, which is similar to it, boil and burn up immediately when drenched with water. 

Melancholy <note>original: "melancolia"</note> has such power toward either extreme by a certain unity of a stable and fixed nature. This extremity, indeed, does not happen to the other humors <note>The four vital bodily fluids: blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile</note>. When it is supremely hot, it produces the highest boldness, or rather ferocity. But when it is extremely cold, it produces fear and extreme cowardice. 

Yet when it is affected by varying degrees between cold and heat, it produces various dispositions, not unlike how particularly strong undiluted wine <note>original: "merum"</note> is accustomed to bring about various states in those drinking to intoxication or even just a little too freely. 

Therefore, it is necessary that black bile be appropriately tempered. When it is thus moderated, as we have said, and mixed with yellow bile and blood, because by nature...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the physical properties of humors and their influence on the human soul, specifically focusing on the "dryness" associated with intellectual brilliance.</meta>

It is dry, and as far as its own nature allows, it is extremely thin and easily ignited by those <note>Likely referring to the vital spirits or heat mentioned on the previous page.</note>. Because it is solid and very tenacious, once it is set ablaze, it burns for a very long time. Because it is most powerful in the unity of its tenacious dryness, it grows hot more vehemently. Just as wood, if both it and straw are set on fire, glows more intensely and for a longer duration than the straw. From this lasting and vehement heat, a great brilliance and a vehement, long-lasting motion proceed. This is the point of that saying by Heraclitus: "A dry light is the wisest soul." <note>original: "Lux sicca anima sapientissima." Heraclitus (c. 535 – c. 475 BCE) believed fire was the fundamental element and that a "dry" soul was superior and more intelligent than one "moistened" by base desires.</note>

## In what way black bile benefits the intellect

Someone might perhaps ask: what is the nature of the body of such a humor, blended from those three humors in the proportion we have described? It is almost the same in color as the gold we see, but it inclines somewhat toward purple. And when it is ignited—whether by natural heat or by the movement of the body or the mind—it glows and shines almost exactly like glowing, red gold mixed with purple. And just as a **rainbow** <note>Latin: *Iris*</note> draws out various colors, so it does from a burning heart. Someone will ask again, in what way does such a humor benefit the intellect? Certainly, the <term>spirits<note>In Renaissance medicine, "spirits" were subtle vapors that acted as the bridge between the physical body and the immaterial soul.</note></term> created from this humor are, first of all, subtle—no different than that water which they call the "water of life" <note>Latin: *aqua vitae*; a term used for distilled alcohol or brandy, which was a new and potent medicinal discovery at the time.</note>, or "water of the vine," and "burning water," whenever it is extracted from thicker, pure wine by distillation over fire, as is usually done. For the spirits under narrower...

When the passages of such <term>black bile<note>original: "atrae bilis." In the medical theory of the time, this was one of the four bodily humors. Fourthly, because they flow continuously from a solid and stable <term>humor<note>Ficino describes the black bile as a "stable moisture," meaning it provides a long-lasting fuel for the mind compared to other, more fleeting bodily fluids.</note></term>, they sustain their activity for a very long duration.

Relying on such a compliant tool, our soul investigates with great intensity. It perseveres in its investigation for a longer time. It easily finds whatever it has searched for: it perceives it clearly, judges it with sincerity, and retains those judgments for a long time.

Add to this that—as we indicated in the previous sections—the soul, by means of an instrument or stimulus of this kind, corresponds in some way to the center of the world. <meta>This page continues the discussion on maintaining the health of scholars and those dedicated to the Muses, focusing on the metaphorical "monsters" or vices that threaten the intellect.</meta>

->**ON THE HEALTHY LIFE.**<-

...Hercules must often be called upon: so that Apollo may pierce these sorts of monsters, which are enemies of Pallas <note>Pallas Athena, the goddess of wisdom</note>, with his arrows; so that Neptune may subdue them with his trident; and so that Hercules may crush and tear them with his club. The first monster indeed is sexual intercourse, especially if it exceeds one’s strength even slightly. For it suddenly exhausts the spirits <note>In Renaissance medicine, "spirits" were subtle vapors that carried life and thought through the body</note>, especially the more refined ones; it weakens the brain, and undermines the stomach and the midriff <note>original: "precordia", referring to the diaphragm and the region around the heart</note>. 

Nothing can be more adverse to the intellect than this evil. Why else did Hippocrates judge intercourse to be similar to the "falling sickness" <note>original: "comitiali morbo", an ancient term for epilepsy</note> if not because it strikes the mind, which is sacred? It is so harmful that Avicenna <note>A famous Persian physician and philosopher</note> said in his *Book on Animals*: "If more semen flows out through intercourse than nature can tolerate, it is more harmful than if forty times as much blood had flowed out." It was not without reason, then, that the ancients held the Muses and Minerva <note>Goddess of wisdom and strategic war</note> to be virgins. 

This relates to that Platonic story: When Venus threatened the Muses that if they did not honor the sacred rites of love, she would arm her son <note>Cupid</note> against them. The Muses replied: "O Venus, threaten Mars <note>The god of war</note> with such things; for your Cupid does not fly among us." Finally, nature has separated no sense further from the intellect than the sense of touch. 

The second monster is the overindulgence of wine and food. Indeed, if the wine is either excessive or too hot and potent, it will fill the head itself with humors <note>The body's vital fluids</note> and the most foul vapors. I will pass over the fact that drunkenness makes men insane. Excessive food, however, first to the stomach—

In the very act of digesting, the body recalls every natural power to itself. Because of this, it happens that one cannot attend to the head and to contemplation at the same time. Furthermore, food that is poorly <note>original: *inepte*</note> digested blunts the edge of the mind with many thick vapors and humors <term>humors: the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—which pre-modern medicine believed governed human health and personality</term>. Even if the food is digested well enough, as Galen <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–216 AD) was the most influential physician of the Roman Empire; his theories dominated Western medicine for over a millennium</note> says, a soul suffocated by fat and blood cannot perceive anything celestial.

¶ Finally, the third "monster" <note>The author uses "monster" (*monstrum*) to describe a particularly harmful or unnatural habit</note> is to stay awake deep into the night, especially quite often after dinner; because of this, you are also forced to sleep after the sun has risen. Since very many scholars indeed err and are deceived in this matter, I will explain at greater length how much it harms the intellect, and I will provide seven principal reasons:

1. The first derived from the heavens themselves;
2. The second from the elements;
3. The third from the humors;
4. The fourth from the order of things;
5. The fifth from the nature of the stomach;
6. The sixth from the spirits <term>spirits: in this context, "vital spirits" or "pneuma," believed to be subtle substances that carried life and thought through the body</term>;
7. The seventh derived from the imagination <note>original: *phantasia*</note>.

¶ To begin, the three planets—as we were saying in the previous sections—contempl... <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely "contemplantur" (contemplate)</note>
