->¶ ON THE HEALTHY LIFE.<-

...those who rise just as the sun begins to appear, or even as it is already rising, to contemplate and write, they alone observe with the sharpest vision and write and compose their discoveries with the greatest eloquence. ¶ The second reason, drawn from the elements, is this: When the sun rises, the air is moved, thinned, and made clear; but when it sets, the opposite occurs. Blood and the **spirits** <note>In Renaissance medicine, "spirits" (spiritus) were subtle vapors or fluids that circulated through the body to facilitate movement, sensation, and thought.</note> are by nature similar to the surrounding air and necessarily follow its motion and quality. ¶ The third reason, derived from the **humors** <term>Humors: The four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) believed by ancient and medieval doctors to govern health and temperament.</term>, is of this kind: At dawn, the blood is moved and reigns supreme; through this motion, it is thinned, warmed, and made clear. The spirits are accustomed to follow and imitate the blood. But as night approaches, that thicker and colder **melancholy** and **phlegm** <note>original: "pituita"</note> dominate; these undoubtedly render the spirits most unfit for contemplation. ¶ The fourth reason, drawn from the order of things, is this: Day is for wakefulness; night is assigned to sleep. For when the sun either approaches our hemisphere or moves above it, it opens the **passages** <note>original: "meatus"; refers to the pores or channels within the body.</note> of the body with its rays and expands the humors and spirits from the center to the periphery. This indeed excites and leads to wakefulness and action. Conversely, when the sun recedes, everything is constricted. This, by a certain natural order, invites sleep, especially after the third or fourth part of the night. Whoever, therefore, drowses in the morning, when the sun and the world are waking...

...[labo]rat: <note>This is a word fragment likely completing "laborat" (he labors) from the previous page.</note> but he stays awake late into the night, when nature now commands us to sleep and to rest from our labors. Here, without a doubt, such a person rebels against both the order of the universe and against himself, while he is disturbed and pulled apart by contrary movements at the same time. Truly, while he is moved by the universe toward the outermost parts, <note>Referring to the expansion of vital heat and spirits toward the skin/extremities during the day.</note> he moves himself toward the innermost parts. And conversely: while he is drawn by the universe toward the innermost parts, <note>In traditional medicine, heat and "spirits" were thought to retreat inward to the organs at night to aid rest and digestion.</note> he meanwhile pulls himself back toward the extremes. Therefore, by this perverse order and these contrary movements, both the whole body and the <term>spirits</term> <note>In Renaissance medicine, "spirits" were subtle fluids that acted as the bridge between the soul and the physical body, carrying out functions like thought and movement.</note> and the intellect are utterly weakened.

In the fifth place, we argue from the nature of the stomach in this manner. The stomach, by the long-lasting action of the daytime air, is greatly dilated as the pores are opened; and as the spirits eventually fly out, it is greatly weakened. Therefore, as night comes on, it requires a new supply of spirits by which it may be fostered. For this reason, whoever at that time begins long and difficult contemplations strives to pull those very spirits back to the head. These spirits, being thus distracted, satisfy neither the stomach nor the head. 

But it is especially harmful if, staying up late after dinner, we apply ourselves more attentively to studies of this kind. For then the stomach needs many spirits and much heat for the <term>concoction</term> <note>The process of digestion, which was likened to "cooking" food in the heat of the stomach.</note> of food. But these two—the spirits and the heat—are diverted to the head by such late-night study and zeal. As a result, they suffice for neither the brain nor the stomach. Add to this that the head, on account of such movement, becomes filled with the thicker vapors of the food.

And food in the stomach, when deprived of heat and <term>spirit<note>In Renaissance medicine, "spirits" (spiritus) were thought to be subtle, vaporous substances that acted as the bridge between the soul and the body, carrying out vital functions like digestion and thought.</note></term>, remains undigested and begins to rot. From this, it again fills the head with vapors and causes harm. Furthermore, in the morning hours, when one ought to rise so that each limb may be purged of all the waste products <note>original: "excrementis"</note> retained during sleep, a worst-case scenario occurs: the person who had utterly interrupted their digestion by <term>working late by lamplight<note>The original Latin "lucubrando" refers specifically to studying or working into the night by candlelight, a common but criticized habit among scholars.</note></term> is then forced to further delay the expulsion of those wastes by sleeping late into the morning. 

All physicians judge this habit to be as harmful to the intellect as it is to the body. Therefore, those who—contrary to nature—rightly use the night for the day and, conversely, use the day for the night like owls, do indeed imitate owls (even if they do so unwillingly): just as the eyes of owls grow dim under the light of the sun, so too does the sharpness of these people's minds grow dim under the brilliance of truth.

In the sixth place, he proves the same point by considering the spirits. Through the fatigue of the day, the spirits—especially the most subtle and refined ones—are eventually dissipated. Consequently, at night, only a few thick and heavy spirits remain, which are entirely unfit for literary studies. The intellect, relying on these crippled wings, can no more fly than can bats or horned owls. 

On the contrary, in the morning after sleep, the spirits are refreshed and the limbs are so strengthened that they require very little support from the spirits. At this time, many subtle spirits are available to serve the brain; they can obey more readily, as they are very little occupied with warming or governing the limbs. Finally, the seventh reason...

...thus it is derived from the nature of the <term>fantasy</term> <note>In the context of Renaissance psychology, "fantasy" (*phantasia*) refers to the faculty of the mind that receives and processes sensory images before they are analyzed by the intellect.</note>. Whether it should be called fantasy, or imagination, or thought, or by any any other name, it is distracted and disturbed during waking hours by many, long, and conflicting images, thoughts, and cares. This very distraction and disturbance is utterly opposed to the contemplation that follows, which requires a completely tranquil and serene mind. 

In truth, only by the quiet of the night is that agitation finally calmed and pacified. Therefore, we always approach the night with a disturbed mind; but when the night departs, we usually apply ourselves to our studies with a tranquil mind. However, those who attempt to judge things when their minds are excessively agitated are no different from those who suffer from vertigo: they think everything is spinning (as <note>original: "ut Plato inquit"</note> **Plato** says) when they themselves are the ones spinning.

# ON THE HEALTHY LIFE

**E**rom those things which were discussed in the previous sections, it is now established as nearly certain that we should begin our studies at the most opportune time: either exactly at sunrise, or at least one or at most two hours at the most before the sun rises. <note>The author emphasizes the "early bird" philosophy common in Renaissance health manuals, believing the morning air and light aided mental clarity.</note> But before you rise from your bed, first rub your whole body gently for a short while with your palms. Then rub your head with your fingernails, but do this a little more lightly. In this matter, let **Hippocrates** <note>Hippocrates (c. 460 – c. 370 BC) was an ancient Greek physician often called the "Father of Medicine."</note> advise you. For he says that through friction—if it be vigorous—the body is hardened; if light, it is softened; if much, it is reduced; if moderate, it is filled. <note>original: "impleri", here meaning to improve muscle tone or "fill out" the body's health.</note> When you have risen from bed, do not immediately apply yourself entirely to sudden reading and meditation. Instead, allow at least half an hour for any necessary cleansing. <note>This "expurgation" likely refers to morning hygiene and the relief of bodily functions.</note> Soon, prepare yourself diligently for meditation, which you shall prolong for about one hour according to your strength. Then relax the intensity of the mind for a little while; and meanwhile, with an ivory comb, diligently and moderately comb your head from the forehead toward the neck, drawing the comb forty times. Then rub the neck with a somewhat rough cloth. Finally, having returned to meditating, dedicate two additional hours, or at least one hour, to study. Studies can indeed sometimes be extended—though with some intermittent breaks being made—until the hour of noon. Furthermore, studies may sometimes continue—though rarely, unless we are forced to take food in the meantime—until about two hours after noon. For the sun is powerful around its rising; it is also powerful in the middle of the sky; in the region... <note>The text cuts off here, mid-sentence, likely continuing to explain the astrological or physical influence of the sun's position on health.</note>

...also that part of the sky which follows closely after the midheaven: which astronomers call the **ninth house**, and the **house of wisdom**. In this house, the sun greatly rejoices. Since all poets agree that **Phoebus** <note>original: "Phebum" — another name for Apollo, the god of light, prophecy, and the arts</note> is the leader of the Muses and the sciences, it is only right that if something especially profound must be devised, it should be contemplated during these hours. If the Muses are to be sought, they should be sought during these same hours with Phoebus as the guide.

For the remaining hours seem better suited for reading the works of the ancients and others, rather than for thinking out new and original ideas of one's own. We must always remember, however, that during any given hour, the intense focus of the mind should be relaxed at least once for a short while. For when the spirits <note>In Renaissance medicine, "spirits" were subtle vapors that carried life and thought through the body; over-focus was thought to "burn" them up.</note> are dissipated by such intense application, you will naturally become sluggish if you never cease to strain. While you are working with your mind, let your body rest. Fatigue of the body is bad, but fatigue of the mind is worse; the fatigue of both together is the worst of all, distracting a person with opposing pressures and destroying one’s very life. Finally, let meditation proceed no further than the point where it remains a pleasure. Indeed, it is better to stop just short of that point.

Waste matter must be most diligently purged from all the passages of the body. The skin of the whole body, and especially the head, should be thoroughly cleansed both by washing and by rubbing <note>original: frictione; a common medical practice of the time to open pores and stimulate the body</note>. One must avoid foods that are excessively cold. Furthermore, unless <term>black bile<note>original: atra bilis; one of the four "humors" or bodily fluids thought to control health and temperament. An excess was linked to depression and sluggishness.</note></term> prevents it, one should also avoid moist foods, as well as everything that is fatty, foul, viscous, oily, and sticky, or things that tend to rot easily. If the stomach is cold, whether by its natural temperament or due to old age, the drinking of water must either be given up entirely or certainly reduced.

Food should be consumed in moderation, but drink even more moderately. One’s dwelling should be high up and very far removed from heavy and cloudy air <note>The author refers to "miasma" or bad air, which was believed to carry disease</note>. Humidity should be driven out through the use of both fire and warm scents. Cold must be kept away from the head, and especially from the neck and the feet; for cold is a great hindrance to the <term>intellect<note>original: ingenio; referring to one's natural mental talent or sharpness.</note></term>.

It is beneficial to use a moderate amount of spices in colder dishes, especially nutmeg, cinnamon, and saffron. Also, candied ginger <note>original: Siziberis conditi</note> taken in the morning on an empty stomach is extremely beneficial for the senses and especially for the memory.

Furthermore, it is agreed that it is beneficial to infuse gold or silver that has been highly heated, and even to include their leaves in drinking cups or in the broth itself; likewise, it is beneficial to drink and take food from a gold or silver vessel. <note>Eating and drinking from precious metals was believed to transfer their "celestial" or incorruptible qualities to the patient.</note>

Mercury, Pythagoras, and Plato command that a discordant or mourning soul be composed and uplifted by the playing of the lyre and by song that is as steady as it is harmonious. I also—if I may compare the lowest things to the highest—frequently experience at home how much the sweetness of the lyre and song can prevail against the bitterness of black bile <term>black bile: known as *atra bilis*, this was believed to be the bodily fluid responsible for melancholy and depression</term>. 

We praise the frequent sight of clear water, or of the colors green or red; the enjoyment of gardens and groves; and a pleasant walk along rivers and through lovely meadows. We also highly approve of horseback riding, passive exercise <term>gestation: the practice of being carried in a litter or carriage to stimulate the body without exhaustion</term>, and gentle sailing. But above all, we recommend variety: easy occupations, various tasks that are not burdensome, and the constant company of agreeable people.

# ON A HEALTHY LIFE

...should be taken daily, and in moderation, light and seasoned moderately with cinnamon, mace, and nutmeg. However, dry food should always outweigh soft nourishment and drink in quantity, unless perhaps we greatly fear the dryness of black bile <note>original: "atrae bilis"; in the medical theory of the four humors, black bile was associated with melancholy and a dry, cold temperament</note>. Food should wait upon hunger (if it can be done conveniently), and drink upon thirst. Let greed for both be absent from the table.

Loathing and over-satiety should be far away. One must abstain from those things which, on account of excessive humidity or poisonous, oily, and viscous matter, weaken the stomach by relaxing it; or those things which are either too cold or extremely hot, or are difficult to digest because of their hardness. And [avoid] such things that for a long time after the meal leave a bothersome taste on the palate, or cause bloating, or fill the head with many vapors <note>In the medicine of this period, "vapors" rising from the stomach were believed to cause headaches and cloud the mind</note>. Above all, [avoid] everything that rots easily, whether outside or inside the bowels. Sweet flavors, or sharp ones if they are alone, we by no means approve. But we wish sweet things to be tempered by something sharp, pungent, or dry.

Mastic and dried mint, fresh sage, raisins, cooked quinces seasoned with sugar, chicory, rose, coral <note>ground coral was frequently used in Renaissance pharmacy for its supposed cooling and strengthening properties</note>, the lotus, and capers seasoned with vinegar are most friendly to the stomach. Furthermore, pomegranates with a flavor midway between sour and sweet; and in general, whatever things are moderately dry and somewhat harsh—which doctors call <term>styptics</term> <note>substances that contract body tissues and reduce secretions</note>—or those that are somewhat pungent, salty, or aromatic. However, <term>myrobalans</term> <note>a dried, plum-like fruit from India, highly valued by early modern physicians for their astringent qualities</note> surpass everything. Wine also...

dinner is taken, one hour of wakefulness seems to suffice. Sexual
intercourse <note>original: *coitus*</note> is ruinous to the stomach: especially if you lie together
immediately while sated, or while starving. The stomach
suffers in idleness; it rejoices in exercise—except while it
is full of food. Immediately after food, one should walk moderately...

# CONCERNING THE HEALTHY LIFE

### Concerning those things which nourish the principal members and the powers of the spirit

**S**ed now it seems better that we should bring forth some things from the physicians' workshop <note>original: "medicorum officina"; referring to the established body of medical knowledge and practice</note> into the open: things which might either keep whole or restore the strengths of the stomach, the heart, the brain, and the spirits of the intellect; and if either phlegm <note>pituita; one of the four bodily humors believed to cause sluggishness if in excess</note> or black bile <note>atra bilis; the humor associated with melancholy</note> should increase, or if nausea should threaten, they may drive them far away. All physicians, without controversy, have agreed that nothing is more beneficial than theriac <note>original: "tiriaca"; a famous and complex ancient herbal compound used as a universal tonic and antidote</note> for nourishing and strengthening both individual members and powers, as well as the spirits <note>In Renaissance medicine, "spirits" were subtle vapors that carried life and thought through the body</note> and the intellect. <margin>theriac</margin> 

Of this, therefore, we shall primarily use half a dram, or at least a third part of a dram, twice in any week in winter and autumn; but in summer and spring, once. It may be taken either alone or, if you prefer during cold and damp seasons, with a little clear, sweet wine. However, in warm and dry seasons—especially if one's temperament <note>original: "natura"; referring to the patient's humoral balance</note> or age be of a warmer nature—it should be taken with two or three ounces of rose water. This should be done on an empty stomach, six or seven hours before taking food. If theriac is lacking, we shall give mithridatium <note>original: "mitridatum"; a legendary complex remedy similar to theriac, named after King Mithridates VI</note>. But when we take theriac or mithridatium, one must abstain entirely from every "hot" thing <note>referring to foods categorized as 'hot' in the Galenic system, such as heavy spices or strong wines</note> on that day; and if it be summer or spring, "cold" things must be used.

In the second place, for the same purpose, aloe is approved by everyone, provided it is rightly chosen and washed. Take two drams of chebulic myrobalans <note>a dried fruit from the Terminalia chebula tree, used as a digestive tonic</note>; of purple roses, red sandalwood, emblic myrobalans <note>Indian gooseberry</note>, cinnamon, saffron, and citron fruit peel, [take] two [units] of each...

These three things we ourselves have tested in the use we prescribed—the theriac, and the aloe tempered as we described, and that confection—to benefit every limb, as well as the strengths and the <term>spirits</term> <note>In this medical tradition, "spirits" were subtle substances that carried life and sensation through the body</note>: to sharpen the senses and the intellect: and to strengthen the memory. ## Remedies against phlegm
**I**f we must fight more sharply against overflowing phlegm, we shall give pills at dawn made from the *hiera picra* of Galen <note>original: "hierapicra Galieni"; a famous ancient "sacred bitter" purgative containing aloes</note>, or those which Mesue <note>Yuhanna ibn Masawayh, a celebrated 9th-century Persian physician whose works were standard in European medical schools</note> calls "elephangine" <note>aromatic pills containing aloes and spices, named for their strength or perhaps for their use in treating "elephantiasis" (leprosy)</note>. Specifically, we give as many and as often as is necessary; or even, for a person of a more robust constitution, pills composed of equal parts of *hiera* and agaric troches <term>troches: small medicated lozenges or cakes; agaric is a type of fungus used as a laxative</term>. These should always be given with liquid rose-honey and oxymel <term>oxymel: a traditional medicinal syrup made of honey and vinegar</term>, and fennel water <gloss>that is, fennel</gloss>. This syrup is certainly most beneficial in digesting phlegm, both before and after taking the pills. If the other humors <note>the four fluids of the body: blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile</note> are also disturbed along with the phlegm, we shall purge them at the appropriate time with Mesue’s rhubarb pills, or the pills which are called "sine quibus" <note>original: "sine quibus"; short for *sine quibus esse nolo*, meaning "pills without which I do not wish to be," a popular general-purpose laxative</note> by later authors. However, we utterly detest all violent and sudden evacuation and purging. For it weakens the stomach and the heart, exhausts much of the "spirits" <note>the vital forces believed to circulate through the body</note>, confuses the humors, and darkens the spirits with the dark vapors of the humors.

## Distillation
<note>In early medicine, "distillation" (or catarrh) refers to the dripping of excess fluid—usually phlegm—from the head down into the throat, chest, or stomach.</note>

**W**hen the head is unstable with distillations because of phlegm, we shall sometimes give some of the pills we have just described at the hour of sleep. Furthermore, we shall order the patient to chew frankincense <margin>frankincense</margin> often at that hour and others. For it is wonderful for distillations and for all the senses...

### Headache

**I**F the head often aches, burdened by a cold humor <note>referring to an imbalance of the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile; a "cold" humor was often associated with excess phlegm</note>, besides those things we have related, we shall order the patient to hold in the mouth that confection which they call *diambra* <note>an aromatic compound medicine containing ambergris and spices</note>, or *diacori* <note>a remedy made with wild saffron or gladiolus</note>, or *plisarchoticon* <note>a complex medicinal electuary used for digestive and cephalic health</note>. Furthermore, chew mastic <note>a resin from the mastic tree used for breath and digestion</note> often. Additionally, smear the forehead, temples, and neck with the leaves of marjoram, fennel, and rue, pounded together with rose oil. Similarly, aloes, vinegar, oil, and rose water perfectly diluted.

### On the Care of Sight

**W**HEN the eyes are dim, yet do not redden, nor provide any other sign of heat, then indeed an eye-wash <note>original: "collirium"</note> made from the water of fennel, marjoram, celandine, and rue—with saffron and antimony added—is beneficial. But water of this kind must first be strained through a thick cloth. However, you should apply nothing to the eyes unless you have first frequently purged the body with "light-bringing" pills <note>original: "pilulis lucif" (luciferis); specialized pills intended to clear the vision by purging the head</note>. But if the dim eyes are red, immediately purge with pills made from fumitory <note>original: "fumo terre," literally "smoke of the earth," a plant used to treat skin and eye issues</note>. Soon, an eye-wash of rose water and sugar is helpful. Sometimes, indeed, with egg white, tutty <note>original: "tutia"; zinc oxide collected from the flues of zinc-smelting furnaces, used as an ointment</note>, and milk added, it helps at once. Generally, however, the daily use...

# ON THE HEALTHY LIFE.

Fennel <margin>Fennel</margin> preserves and sharpens the sight. Indeed, one ought to frequently hold its seeds in the mouth and eat its leaves. The "Small Triphera" <term>Triphera</term> <note>A traditional medicinal paste or electuary, often used as a restorative or to balance the humors.</note> described by Mesue <note>A reference to Mesue the Younger, a celebrated but semi-legendary 11th-century physician whose pharmaceutical manuals were standard in Europe for centuries.</note> is excellent. It is best to take a preserved Chebulic myrobalan <term>Chebulic myrobalan</term> <note>A plum-like fruit from the Haritaki tree, native to South Asia, highly valued in Renaissance medicine for its "cleansing" properties.</note> every day on an empty stomach, and with it, a little bread made of sugar and powdered fennel. This is, moreover, wonderfully beneficial for the intellect and for lengthening life. The use of Eyebright <term>Eyebright</term> <note>The herb Euphrasia, long associated with eye health due to the "doctrine of signatures," as its flowers resemble bloodshot eyes.</note> is also a singular protection for the eyes. In every case of either headache or dimness of the vision, the vapors <note>In the medical theory of the time, "vapors" were gases rising from the stomach to the head that could cloud the senses.</note> must be diverted backwards by means of frictions and cupping glasses. And if heat be the cause and blood is in abundance, we shall apply leeches to the neck and shoulders.

# On Restoring the Sense of Taste.

The stomach <note>The original Latin text features a decorative initial letter **S** containing an illustration of a stomach.</note> in those devoted to literary studies often loses almost all sense of taste. If this happens through a fault of phlegm <note>One of the four bodily fluids or "humors" believed to govern health; phlegm was considered cold and moist.</note>—which an acidic taste in the mouth or a large amount of sticky saliva indicates—after you have cleared the bowels with the medicines we described above, use aromatic rose preparations: namely, rose-sugar mixed with rose-honey and cinnamon. Or even use preserved ginger alone, or syrup of mint; but above all, use theriac <term>Theriac</term> <note>An ancient, complex "wonder-drug" containing dozens of ingredients, used as an antidote and general tonic.</note>. But if by chance an abundance of bile occurs—which a bitter mouth usually shows—likewise after a purgation of prepared aloe (as we have said) or rhubarb, take...

Also mint, either preserved in vinegar or diluted in the juice of a tart pomegranate.

<meta>This page appears to be from Marsilio Ficino’s "De Vita Libri Tres" (Three Books on Life), specifically Book I, which deals with the health of scholars and the management of melancholy. It continues a discussion on the safe evacuation of "black bile" (melancholy) from the body.</meta>

# ON THE HEALTHY LIFE

[Decorative red paragraph mark] Let it be a precept, as Galen <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–c. 216 AD) was a Greek physician whose theories dominated Western medicine for over a millennium.</note> taught, that we should not strive to draw out that [humor] suddenly; lest perhaps, after its liquid part has been drawn off through a finer opening, the remainder should reside there in an excessively thick and dry state. Instead, let it be gradually softened and digested, and then drawn out. Second, that in the meantime, the head and the whole body should be moistened as much as possible through more humid foods as well as sweet baths and moderate ointments and the like. This must be done with such caution, however, that neither a distillation <note>original: *destillatio*; a "dripping" or flow of waste fluids, such as catarrh, from the brain to the lower body.</note> be provoked, nor the stomach or liver be damaged, nor the passages of the body be obstructed. Third, and indeed most necessary of all, that the heart be continually cherished and strengthened by suitable things, partly taken internally, and partly applied externally to the chest or nostrils. Also, let things be looked at and heard, smelled and thought upon continually, which give delight. But let contrary things be kept far away.

## ON SYRUPS

[Decorative red paragraph mark] **M**any things indeed have been composed by many people against this humor <note>The "melancholic humor" or black bile, associated with depression and intellectual labor.</note>. I, however, for the present shall bring forward three kinds of remedies, the most select and safest of all, first received from the ancients, then confirmed by the more recent authorities, and sometimes even adapted by us for our own use. In the first kind is the composition of the best syrup. In the second, the most proven pills. In the third, the most health-giving <term>electuaries</term> <note>A medicinal paste made by mixing powdered drugs with honey or syrup.</note>. When these three are opportunely applied, the melancholic humor...

The syrup is of this kind. Take borage, bugloss, and the flowers of both, lemon balm, maidenhair fern, endive, violets, dodder, polypody, senna, and dodder of thyme: of each as much as can be held in a handful. Let them all be cooked in water, except for the dodder of thyme and the aromatic spices, until a third part is consumed. After the decoction has been strained, let it be cooked again moderately with sugar and the dodder of thyme. [blue pilcrow] Finally, the aromatic spices should be infused, namely the cinnamon and the saffron. At dawn, three ounces of this syrup should be drunk warmed, and at the same time two or three ounces of bugloss water: and together with these, at least two of the following pills should be taken, or more as suits each person: specifically in such a way that the bowels are moved a little every day.

-># ON THE HEALTHY LIFE.<-

[Our] invention, composed under the very influence of Jupiter and Venus: which draw out phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile <note>original: "pituitam, bilem, atram bilem"; these are three of the four bodily humors believed to govern health and temperament.</note> without discomfort; they strengthen each limb; they sharpen and illuminate the <term>spirits</term> <note>In Renaissance medicine, the 'spirits' were a subtle, vaporous substance that acted as a bridge between the physical body and the soul.</note>. They expand the spirits in such a way that they do not produce sadness by being constricted, but instead rejoice in expansion and light. Furthermore, they stabilize them, so that they do not vanish through too much stretching. 

Therefore, take twelve grains of gold, especially in its leaf form if it is pure; frankincense, myrrh, saffron, aloe wood <note>Also known as agarwood or oud.</note>, cinnamon, citron peel, lemon balm, raw scarlet silk, mint, white behen, and red behen <note>Behen: medicinal roots (likely Limonium and Centaurea) used as heart tonics.</note>, half a drachm of each; purple roses, red sandalwood, red coral, and three types of <term>myrobalans</term> <note>A type of dried medicinal fruit from India used as a gentle laxative to balance the humors.</note> (namely the emblic, chebulic, and Indian varieties), one drachm of each; and of properly washed aloes, a weight equal to all the other ingredients combined. Form these into pills using the most choice pure wine. 

There follow pills for dissolving melancholy that are somewhat stronger, yet not at all violent. Take peony, myrrh, Arabian stoechas <note>French lavender</note>, lemon balm, frankincense, saffron, and the three myrobalans (the emblic, chebulic, and Indian), one drachm of each. Of <term>troches</term> <note>Small medicated cakes or lozenges.</note> of agaric <note>A fungus used as a purgative.</note>, polypody fern, <term>epithymum</term> <note>Dodder of thyme, a parasitic plant used to purge "burnt" black bile.</note>, senna, and <term>lapis lazuli</term> (properly washed and prepared), and <term>Armenian stone</term> <note>A blue mineral used in medicine to treat melancholic disorders.</note> similarly treated, take three drachms of each. With two ounces of washed aloes and an excellent wine, fashion the pills. If a manifest heat should dominate along with the melancholy, those things which are in this composition...

...cold, they must be increased by a third part of their weight besides. I have composed these pills, as is fitting for those dedicated to literary studies, in imitation of the Greeks, Latins, and Arabs. I did not wish, however, to mix in stronger ingredients—such as hellebore <note>original: "veratrum"; a plant used in antiquity as a powerful, often dangerous purgative</note>, which Carneades "the Fanatic" <note>The skeptic philosopher Carneades was said to have taken hellebore before writing to sharpen his mind</note> used to use. For I advise only learned men, or those of a slightly stronger constitution; for such men, nothing is more harmful <note>original: "pestilentius"; literally "more pestilential"</note> than violent treatments. Therefore, I have omitted the "Indian pills," those known for containing lapis lazuli or Armenian stone <note>stones used in medicine to purge black bile, but considered harsh</note>, and the composition they call *hieralogodion* <note>a "sacred" medicinal compound used as a purgative</note>. 

If, finally, it is appropriate to include a simpler preparation which I use more frequently: Take one ounce of washed aloes; two <term>drams|a unit of weight, approximately 3.9 grams</term> each of emblic and chebulic <term>myrobalans|astringent, plum-like fruits used in medieval medicine to balance the humors</term>; two drams of mastic; and two of roses, especially purple ones. Make these into pills using wine. 

Furthermore, no one should ever use these pills—or any of those others we have approved—on their own, lest the body perhaps becomes too dried out. This drying is indeed the worst thing for <term>melancholy|a condition believed to be caused by an excess of "black bile," characterized by coldness and dryness</term>. Instead, take them either with the syrup we described above (following the teachings of partly <note>Mesue (Yuhanna ibn Masawayh) was a famous Persian physician; Gentile da Foligno was a prominent 14th-century Italian doctor</note> Mesue and partly Gentile da Foligno), or with one, two, or three ounces of a fragrant, light wine, as suits each individual. Or, they may be taken with a water of honey, raisins, and liquorice. Or, if a feverish heat dominates the body, take them with violet <term>julep|a sweet medicinal drink, usually made with sugar and floral water</term> and violet water. In general, I advise all learned men who are prone to black bile...

# ON THE HEALTHY LIFE.

Those who are more prone to this condition should use this purgation twice every year—specifically in the spring and autumn—for fifteen or twenty consecutive days: namely, with pills taken with <term>syrup</term><note>In Renaissance medicine, syrups were used to 'prepare' the humors before they were purged</note> and similar remedies. Whoever, indeed, is a little less liable to this disease will find it sufficient if they take the first or last pills throughout the year, once every week: in summer, indeed, with <term>julep</term><note>original: "iuleb"; a sweet, cooling medicinal drink usually made with sugar and water, often rose-flavored</note> as we have said, but at other times with wine.

# On Liquid Medicine.

**M**emory serves us well to remember that where the greatest danger of drying out looms, yet necessity compels one to purge, it is worthwhile to stop taking pills. Instead, when it is time to purge, one should sometimes infuse into the syrup itself (or a similar <term>decoction</term><note>A liquid medicine made by boiling down herbs in water</note> made in <term>bugloss water</term><note>Anchusa officinalis, a plant believed to gladden the heart and temper the melancholic humor</note>) one ounce—or at least half an ounce—of <term>diasena</term><note>A laxative compound based on senna leaves</note>, <term>diachatholicon</term><note>A "universal" or general laxative paste</note>, or <term>Persian triphera</term><note>An electuary (medicinal paste) used for various ailments, often containing fruits and spices</note>.

But if the body is more robust, or the bowels are more constricted and hard, one should add one or two <term>drams</term><note>A unit of weight; one dram is approximately 3.9 grams</note> of <term>electuary of hamech</term><note>A bitter medicinal paste specifically designed to purge "black bile" or melancholy</note>. Prepared <term>cassia</term><note>The pulp of the "pudding-pipe tree," used as a mild laxative</note> is also useful here, but <term>manna</term><note>The sweet, solidified sap of the flowering ash tree, used as a gentle, safe purgative</note> is more useful. These do not suit every type of melancholy, but especially that which is created by <term>adustion</term><note>original: "adustione"; literally "scorching." In humoral theory, this refers to humors that have become "burnt," making them toxic and dangerous</note>.

But if the melancholy is "natural" <note>Natural melancholy was the normal cool, dry humor associated with the earth, as opposed to the toxic "burnt" version</note>, these remedies are indeed suitable, but especially if a double or triple portion of <term>polypody</term><note>A type of fern root used to purge phlegm and melancholy</note> is added to the syrup, along with liquorice, saffron, and raisins. Likewise, let two ounces of liquid <term>honey of roses</term> be added to the medicine. Whene—

How the syrup should be taken, we have said in the previous sections. But the medicine should be taken with it for sixty days <note>literal Latin: "thrice twenty days."</note> 

¶ But if no melancholic humor <term>humor | the "fluids" of the body—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—that governed health and temperament</term> appears, but only a melancholic **complexion**, namely a cold and dry quality of the limbs, remember that it is not at all expedient to purge the bowels or to perform bloodletting. Instead, do only the remaining things which we have either said or will say. These pertain especially to moderately warming and greatly moistening the body, illuminating the **spirits** <term>spirits | subtle vapors or gases believed to circulate through the body to facilitate movement and thought</term> as much as possible, and soothing the limbs. 

¶ However, where the humor of black bile <note>original: atræ bilis</note> itself overflows, we do not only moisten the body and the humor, but we also loosen the bowels with that caution which we mentioned: but never violently. Indeed, **Plato** in the *Timaeus* <note>Plato’s influential dialogue regarding the physical world and the human body.</note> warns us that a long-lasting illness, such as melancholy, should not be provoked with overly strong medicines and troublesome drugs.

### On bloodletting.

**S**ome are rather bold in bloodletting, whom wise physicians greatly detest. For blood is the tempering agent of black bile, the fuel of the spirit, and the treasure of life. Only when an abundance of blood is indicated—either by excessive laughter and great boldness and confidence, or a ruddy color and swelling of the veins—is it appropriate to let blood for scholars <note>original: litteratis. Ficino's work was specifically written for the health of "literary men" or scholars, who were thought to be prone to melancholy.</note>...

## ¶ ON THE HEALTHY LIFE
When the situation requires, we ought to draw blood from the <term>spleen vein</term> <note>original: "uena lienis"; in humoral medicine, this vein—usually on the left hand or arm—was believed to be directly connected to the spleen's health.</note> of the left arm with a wide incision: four ounces in the morning and just as many in the evening. Then, after a few days—at least seven, at most fourteen—one should irritate the <term>hemorrhoids</term> <note>original: "mariscas"; also known as piles.</note>, first by a rougher rubbing and then by applying leeches (which they call blood-suckers), so that three or four ounces of blood may drip from there. Both of these procedures seem appropriate only for the more robust patients. For the weaker ones, however, if necessity demands, one should only irritate the hemorrhoids as we have taught. But it is permitted neither to loosen the bowels with medicines nor to let blood unless you have first softened the body with fatty and gentle enemas <note>original: "clysteribus"; liquid preparations injected into the rectum to clear the bowels or deliver medication.</note>. And let this be a general rule for those of a **melancholic nature**: effort must be made so that, if necessary, through the frequent use of such enemas, the lower bowel is always lubricated and purged.

### ¶ On Electuaries <note>A medicinal paste made by mixing powders with honey, syrup, or medicinal juices.</note>
**S**ubsequently follow the electuaries, among which I certainly praise that which Rhazes <note>A famous Persian physician (c. 865–925) whose works were foundational to European medicine.</note> called "The Exhilarant." Additionally, I recommend those which Avicenna <note>A Persian polymath (c. 980–1037) and author of "The Canon of Medicine."</note> composed in his book *On the Powers of the Heart*. But I praise much more that which Mesue <note>Likely referring to Mesue the Younger, an author of popular pharmaceutical texts in the Middle Ages.</note> describes thus: Take one pound by weight of raw silk recently dyed a scarlet color. Submerge it in the juice of sweet and pleasantly fragrant apples, as well as the juice of <term>bugloss</term> <note>A plant often used in historical medicine to "comfort the heart" and drive away melancholy.</note> and rose water—one pound of each. After twenty-four hours, cook all of this gently, until—

Take four ounces of sweet almonds; pine nuts—specifically those washed for the duration of one day; and cucumber seeds; two ounces of each. Four ounces of hard sugar, which they call "candy"—that is, white <note>original: "candum .i. candidum"</note>. One and a half pounds of another sugar, but of the whitest kind. Dissolve all of these in rose water and the juice of lemon and citron, in which red-hot gold and silver have been extinguished <note>A common alchemical and medical practice where hot metal was dipped into a liquid to "infuse" it with the metal's solar or lunar virtues without actually dissolving the metal.</note>. ### On Excessive Wakefulness

**S**often it happens to melancholics, especially those of a scholarly nature <note>original: "litteratis." The author reflects the Renaissance view that intense study "dried out" the brain, leading to melancholy and insomnia</note>, that because the brain is excessively dried out, they are worn down by long periods of wakefulness. Since nothing increases the evils of black bile <note>the "humor" responsible for melancholy</note> more than long wakefulness, one must hasten to remedy such a great evil with all diligence. Therefore, let them eat lettuce after other foods, together with a little bread and a tiny bit of saffron; and let them sip pure wine after the lettuce. Nor should they work by candlelight <note>original: "lucubrent" — to study or work late into the night</note> beyond an hour. And when they go to sleep, let them take a preparation of this kind, in which there are two ounces of white poppy seeds...

One ounce of lettuce seed. Half a dram each of <term>amomum</term> <note>original: "amomi"; an aromatic spice related to cardamom used in ancient medicine</note> and saffron. Six ounces of sugar. Dissolve and cook all these together in poppy syrup. Let them eat two drams of this; and let them taste a little poppy syrup or wine. You should anoint their forehead and temples with oil of violets and of <term>nenufar</term> <note>original: "nenufare"; the water lily, valued for its "cooling" properties in humoral medicine</note> with added camphor. Likewise with milk and oils of almond and violet. 

You shall bring the scent of saffron, camphor, and the peelings of sweet apples to the nostrils. Add a little vinegar, but plenty of rose water. You should also strew the bed with the leaves of cooling plants. Soothe the ears with solemn songs and sounds. You shall moisten the head often with washes of this kind: namely, water in which pieces of poppy have been boiled, along with the leaves of lettuce, purslane, mallow, roses, vine, willow, and reeds, with chamomile added. 

With sweet baths of such things, the shins and arms, and often the whole body, should be moistened. Moreover, drinking milk mixed with sugar—specifically on an empty stomach, provided the stomach tolerates it well—is extremely beneficial. These moistening remedies are wonderfully helpful to <term>melancholics</term> <note>those with an imbalance of "black bile," thought to be dry and cold; hence the focus on "moistening" and "cooling" treatments</note> even if they sleep enough. Remember that almond milk ought to be a very common sight at the table.

...too much melancholy occupying the head makes it heavy, and makes people dull and forgetful. Therefore, the head must be relieved of these burdens with those remedies which we said elsewhere are suitable for <term>phlegm</term> <note>one of the four humors; an excess was thought to cause sluggishness</note>. If those are not sufficient, resort to <term>Indian pills</term> <note>pills typically containing exotic ingredients like myrobalans or resins</note>, <term>cochia pills</term> <note>a standard purgative pill often containing aloes and colocynth</note>, and <term>hieralogodium</term> <note>a bitter medicinal paste used as a purgative</note>. Furthermore, turn to the <term>hiera</term> <note>from the Greek word for "holy," these were bitter, complex herbal formulas</note> of colocynth, or that of Archigenes, or Andromachus, or Theodorion. Or use the "pills of the Jew" which Mesue <note>originally: "Mesues"; referring to the influential 9th-century physician Yūhannā ibn Māsaūayh</note> describes in his chapter on headache.

Because if the patient's nature or age is of a colder temperament, and provided the summer heat does not prevent it: after purging the body, use that <term>anacardine</term> <note>made from the marking nut, Anacardium orientale, which was highly prized for treating memory loss</note> confection, which Mesue calls the "confection of the wise" in his book of antidotes. Use again that anacardine remedy which he reviews in the chapter on forgetfulness, according to the opinion of Rhazes <note>original: "filii Zacharię," referring to the Persian physician Abu Bakr al-Razi</note>. Give one dram early in the morning; but whoever takes it must completely abstain that day from anger, sexual intercourse, drunkenness, hard labor, and all hot things. These indeed are most powerful against dullness and forgetfulness.

But if you prefer more familiar ingredients, give ginger preserved in sugar, but mixed with a little frankincense; this greatly benefits the senses and memory, especially if these are added: honey of anacardium, honey of chebula <note>a species of myrobalan fruit used in Ayurvedic and Greek medicine</note>, sweet flag, cypress, ambergris, and musk. Also beneficial is <term>diambra</term> <note>a restorative compound containing ambergris and spices</note>, "ruling" pills, and <term>diacorium</term> <note>a preparation based on sweet flag</note>. But these should be held in the mouth for a long time; they should also be applied to the nostrils and ears. Also the scent of frankincense, marjoram, fennel, nutmeg, rue, and cloves is of no small benefit. Remember, however, that <term>theriac</term> <note>a famous, complex polypharmaceutical used as a universal antidote and tonic</note> in these and similar cases...

->### ON THE HEALTHY LIFE<-

...quite as we said from the beginning: it must never be placed second to any other remedy. Furthermore, for those who are dull-witted and forgetful, anoint the temples and the neck with an ointment of this kind. Take one ounce of elderberry oil <note>original: "olei sabucini"</note>, two ounces of oil of ben <note>original: "olei deben"; a clear, odorless oil from the Moringa tree used since antiquity as a base for perfumes and medicines</note>, half an ounce of spurge <note>original: "euforbii"; a potent resinous sap used as a stimulant</note>, and the same amount of castor <note>original: "castorei"; a pungent secretion from the beaver, highly prized in historical medicine for treating nervous ailments</note>. And if it should be necessary, you shall apply cupping glasses <note>original: "cucurbitulas"; small glass vessels used to create a vacuum on the skin to draw out "corrupt" humors</note> to the neck. Additionally, you shall cover and warm the crown of the head with crushed marjoram <note>original: "amaraco"</note>, frankincense, and nutmeg.

**C**are for the bodily spirit; cultivate the incorporeal; and finally, venerate the truth. Medicine provides the first; moral discipline the second; and religion the third.

**I**f men who are desirous of truth must care for the bodily spirit with such medical diligence—lest perhaps, being entirely neglected, it becomes an obstacle or serves ineptly those seeking the truth—then without a doubt, it is far more fitting to cultivate the incorporeal spirit (that is, the intellect itself) through the principles of moral discipline. For by this alone is truth itself, being incorporeal, apprehended. For it is a sacrilege <note>original: "nefas"</note> to cultivate only the servant of the soul (that is, the body) while neglecting the soul, which is the master and king of the body. This is especially true since it is the opinion of the Magi <note>Referring to the ancient Persian sages or the followers of Hermes Trismegistus, often cited in Renaissance philosophy</note> and of Plato that the whole body depends on the soul in such a way that unless the soul is well, the body cannot be well. Therefore Apollo, the author of medicine, judged not Hippocrates—though he was born of Apollo's own lineage—but Socrates to be the wisest. Indeed, since as much as Hippocrates [contributed] to the body, so [much Socrates contributed to the soul...]

<meta>This page continues a philosophical and theological argument, likely from a Renaissance Neoplatonic text such as Marsilio Ficino’s "On the Christian Religion." It bridges the gap between Classical Greek philosophy (Socrates and Plato) and Christian doctrine.</meta>

Moreover, Socrates devoted himself to the healing of the soul; yet, although he had attempted this, Christ alone achieved it. Therefore, if we are commanded by Socrates to cultivate our mind with the highest morals for this reason: so that we may more easily attain, with a serene mind, the light and truth sought by us through a natural instinct—how much more is it right to venerate the divine truth itself, especially through holy religion? 

To seek and to grasp this truth, the created mind was formed in no other way than the eye was made for beholding the light of the sun. And as our Plato says: just as sight perceives nothing visible whatsoever except within the splendor of the highest visible thing—that is, the sun itself—so the human intellect grasps nothing <term>intelligible</term> <note>original: "intelligibile"; in this context, it refers to truths or realities that can be understood by the mind but not seen by the physical eyes</note> except within the light of the highest intelligible being, which is God. This light is always and everywhere present to us; it is the light, I say, "which illuminates every man coming into this world" <note>A direct quotation of the Latin Vulgate, John 1:9</note>. In the light of which it is sung <note>The text breaks off at "can," likely the beginning of "canitur" (it is sung) or "canit" (he sings/prophesies)</note>...

<meta>This page serves as the opening dedication and the beginning of Chapter One of Marsilio Ficino’s *De Vita Longa* (On Long Life), the second book of his larger work *De Vita Libri Tres* (Three Books on Life). # ON LONG LIFE
## Preface to the Book on Long Life

<margin>m</margin> **MARSILIO Ficino** of Florence sends greetings to Filippo Valori, an excellent and most noble citizen. Although our Plato <note>Ficino was the primary translator of Plato's works into Latin during the Renaissance.</note> lives by his own <term>spirit<gloss>The Latin "genius" refers to a guiding spirit or the natural talent of an individual.</gloss></term> and will live (as I believe) as long as the world itself shall live, my own spirit nevertheless always impels me toward this: that after the worship of God, I should look after the life of Plato above all else. Toward this very goal, the **House of Medici** <note>original: "Medica domus." This is a characteristic pun by Ficino, playing on the family name "Medici" and the Latin word for medical or healing.</note> has for a long time aspired more than others. Toward the same end you also strive, my Valori—you who are most friendly to the Medici and quite eager for the glory and teachings of Plato. Therefore, whatever life I always desire for Plato, I desire the same for the Medici, and a similar one for Valori. 

For this reason, I urge and beseech you, my Valori: since you always favor the works of Platonic glory, please read and observe these <term>precepts<gloss>Rules or principles intended to guide behavior, in this case, medical and lifestyle advice.</gloss></term> of ours on prolonging life with great diligence. By these means, living a long life, you may be able to support the recently reborn teachings of Plato even longer, together with the magnanimous **Lorenzo de' Medici** <note>Lorenzo "the Magnificent," the de facto ruler of Florence and Ficino's patron.</note>. Farewell.

# MARSILIO FICINO OF FLORENCE
# THE BOOK ON LONG LIFE

A long life is necessary for the perfection of knowledge; which diligence also provides.

-> **TO THE PERFECT ART** <-
-> and Science, we are led <-

not so much by a natural talent for learning or a steady memory, but by the keenness of prudent judgment. Judgment, however, is so difficult—due to the ambiguity arising from a diversity of theories <note>original: "coniecturarũ" (conjectures/theories)</note>—that it must be confirmed by necessary experiment. Experiment is also deceptive <note>A reference to Hippocrates' famous first aphorism: "Life is short, art is long, opportunity fleeting, experiment perilous, judgment difficult."</note>, both because of the difficulty of judgment itself and because of the fleeting shortness of the time available for seizing the opportunity in an experiment. For these very reasons, we rightly conclude with Hippocrates that art is long; and that we cannot master it except through the length of a lifetime. Yet, a long life is not only promised at the start by the Fates; our own diligence also provides it. This is something that even astrologers admit when they discuss elections <term>elections: the branch of astrology used to find the most auspicious moment to begin an undertaking</term> and images <term>images: astrological talismans or figures engraved under specific planetary alignments</term>; and it is confirmed by the diligent care and experience of physicians. Through such foresight, not only those who are naturally strong, but even the sickly sometimes achieve a long life. It is no wonder then that a certain Herodicus <note>A Greek physician and teacher of Hippocrates, mentioned by Plato for using gymnastics to prolong his own frail life.</note>, a man of letters who was the weakest of all in his time (as Plato and Aristotle testify), reached his hundredth year through this kind of providence. Plutarch moreover tells of many with frail bodies who achieved a long life through diligence alone. I pass over for now how many weak individuals I myself have known who, by the gift of prudence, surpassed the years of the most robust men...

# ON LONG LIFE.

...having hoped. Therefore, it will be neither useless nor vain, after the book composed by us on **caring for the health of scholars** <note>This refers to the author's previous work, likely "De Vita Sana," focusing on the specific physical ailments of people who spend their lives studying.</note>, to hand down in addition certain precepts leading to a **long life** for minds eager for knowledge.

Indeed, we do not wish these instructions to be shared with the idle and the lazy. For why should we desire those people to live long? Since they do not even truly "live," it would be as if we were nourishing <term>drones</term><note>A classical metaphor for those who consume resources without contributing work, like non-honey-producing bees.</note> rather than bees. Nor should they be spread among men dissolved in the ruined lust for pleasures, who daily, being fools, prefer a brief pleasure to a long life. Nor should they be revealed to the wicked and unjust, whose life is the death of good men. But rather, they should be given only to **prudent and temperate men**, who, by the powers of their skillful intellect, will benefit the human race either privately or publicly.

¶ Vital heat is nourished by moisture:
¶ When it fails, **dissolution** occurs;
¶ When it exceeds, **suffocation** occurs.

**V**ita <note>The original text uses a decorative blue initial 'V' in a square.</note> Life indeed consists in <term>natural heat</term><note>A core concept in ancient medicine: the internal "fire" or metabolic energy that sustains life.</note>, like a light. The fuel of this heat is an airy and fatty moisture, acting much like oil in a lamp. If, therefore, moisture of this kind should happen to fail, or if it should exceed significantly, or become tainted, the heat is immediately weakened; and finally, once weakened, it is extinguished. 

If the heat is weakened and lost through a lack of moisture, death occurs by **dissolution** <note>Essentially a "drying out" or withering of the body's vital forces.</note>. If instead it is overwhelmed by an excess or vice of moisture, life perishes by **suffocation**. Suffocation happens through the overflowing or putrefaction of any <term>humor</term><note>The bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) believed to control health and temperament.</note>, especially when <term>phlegm</term> either increases or putrefies in some way...

<summary>This page introduces a treatise on longevity intended specifically for the wise and productive rather than the lazy or wicked. It explains the "lamp" metaphor of life, where vital heat (the flame) must be balanced by the body's moisture (the oil).</summary>

<meta>This page continues a discussion on longevity, specifically focusing on the balance of bodily "heat" and "moisture." ...so that not undeservedly it was called "phlegm seeking life" <note>original: *pituita petens vitam*. The author is likely referencing an ancient medical definition of a specific bodily fluid</note>. Therefore, the precepts for prolonging life are most necessary. These precepts equally avoid **dissolution** on one hand, and **suffocation** and **putrefaction** on the other.

I say they do this equally by a common reasoning. For if they happen to treat a person who is somewhat hot or dry, and whose passages <term>meatus: the pores or channels in the body through which spirits and humors flow</term> are indeed open: then the humors and subtle spirits are more prone to **dissolution** <note>original: *resolutioni*, meaning the scattering or evaporating of the body's vital forces</note>. But if they treat a body affected in the opposite way, they rather provide relief for **suffocation**. They strive especially in one or the other direction if the location or the time of year leans toward either extreme.

¶ But when counsel is given to the gifted and the studious, both precepts are almost equally necessary, and effort is applied similarly to both. For in these men, a sharp and hot intellect and the constant movement of the imagination seem to threaten **dissolution**; whereas bodily idleness and indigestion <term>original: *cruditas*, referring to the buildup of undigested, "raw" humors that block the body's internal channels</term> seem to threaten **suffocation**.

Therefore, physicians never work more intensely than in the care of such men. And although the entire discussion of the preceding book contributes greatly to prolonging life, nevertheless, so great a matter seems to demand a certain specific care, which I shall hereafter describe in a few words as best I can.

### ¶ How heat is to be tempered toward moisture, and vice versa, by a certain counsel of Minerva. Chapter III.

<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s discussion on the preservation of life, using the Neoplatonic and Galenic metaphor of the human body as a lamp. Minerva (wisdom) is speaking here about the "oil" of life (the radical moisture) and the "flame" (the innate heat).</meta>

# ON THE LONG LIFE

...Minerva meanwhile, the olive-bearing source of vital oil <note>The "vital oil" refers to the "radical moisture," a bodily fluid that Renaissance doctors believed was the fuel for life's flame.</note>, born from the head of nourishing Jove <note>Minerva, goddess of wisdom, was famously born fully formed from the forehead of Jupiter (Jove).</note>, smiles: because while we clearly perceive the quantity of her gift, we have not yet sufficiently seen its quality. Laughing then, she says: "I have given you oil in abundance, not only enough to feed the flame, but I have also poured it pure into the lamp, without <term>amurca</term> <note>Latin: *amurca*. This refers to the watery, bitter dregs or sediment found in unfiltered olive oil.</note>." So she spoke.

But we, stumbling in our path of speaking, have struck against something: since the lamp for our feet is her word, which we have not yet heard. From this lamp, therefore, let us learn that oil must be continually supplied to the flame, with such diligence that we do not overwhelm the light with a sudden flood. Nor, on the other hand, should we delay giving drink to the thirsty flame. But these two things we consider to have been sufficiently treated in the previous pages.

Two things remain for us: one of which we seem to have touched lightly; the other, we have scarcely touched at all. In the meantime, we are touching Pallas <note>Another name for Minerva.</note> in such a way that she, who is never accustomed to laugh, laughed at us. What then? 

First, we shall consider that even a very small flame is entirely devouring; and for that reason, that lamp shines longer in which the little flame is so tempered to the <term>wick</term> <note>The original text uses *ellychnium* and *lucinium*, both technical terms for a lamp wick.</note> that it does not gulp the oil, but sips it. And so we also, in every diet, will take care: lest, especially in youth, the <term>innate fire</term> <note>The *calidus innatus* or "innate heat," the vital energy believed to reside in the heart that consumes the body's moisture to sustain life.</note>, which is by its own nature voracious, grows too strong. It will indeed be enough to keep away both overflowing moisture and penetrating cold from here.

Then we will consider that the lamp is frequently extinguished when it has not dripped pure oil, but (if I may say so) dreggy oil. From which, a little later, "mushrooms" <note original="fungi">In this context, *fungi* refers to the carbon buildup or "snuff" on a lamp wick that dims the light.</note> grow thick from the dregs and extinguish the light.

<summary>Ficino uses the metaphor of a lamp to explain how the "innate fire" of the body must be carefully managed—neither flooded with too much nourishment nor allowed to dry out—to ensure a long life.</summary>
<keywords>Minerva, radical moisture, innate fire, dietary health, vital oil, lamp metaphor, Marsilio Ficino</keywords>

--- Page 164 ---
Now indeed we have received from Pallas <note>Pallas Athena, the goddess of wisdom, who famously gave the olive tree to Athens.</note> the nourishing oil, namely the aerial kind <note>In Renaissance medicine, "aerial oil" refers to the "radical moisture" or the vital fuel that sustains the "lamp of life" within the human body.</note>, which is most abundant and pure, and through a certain natural viscosity is tenacious and firm. Therefore, what is supplied to this as it is gradually consumed ought not only to be equal to it, but similar. To be similar, it must not only be aerial and fatty, but completely separated from the *amurca* <note>original: "amurca"; the bitter, watery dregs left over from pressing olives</note>—that is, from a certain dreg accumulated from the thicker earth and water. Therefore, to avoid this accumulation and dross, let us flee from such nourishments, and from idleness, indigestion, and filth. Meanwhile, let us worship Minerva <note>The Roman equivalent of Athena; she was famously born fully armed from the forehead of Jupiter.</note> with such moderation that she may indeed strengthen our head (from which she herself was born), but not weaken our nerves or stomach.

### For what reasons the natural moisture dries up; how foreign moisture overflows; and how necessary perfect digestion is for life. **Chapter 4.**

These things quickly dry out the natural moisture: <margin>External [things] dry... indigestion... <unclear>...</unclear> <unclear>...</unclear> <unclear>...</unclear> <unclear>...</unclear> Secondarily said... <unclear>...</unclear> For when <unclear>...</unclear> from these <unclear>...</unclear></margin> An excessive flow of blood. A violent loosening of the bowels. Long-lasting diarrhea. Profuse sweating. Widely opened pores <term>meatus: the channels or passages in the body through which fluids and spirits flow</term>. Sexual intercourse performed to the point of weakness. Panting thirst. Excruciating hunger. Long periods of wakefulness. Use of hot and dry things. Laborious movement of both mind and body. Anxiety. Anger. Grief. Air that is simultaneously drier and hotter, especially when heated by fire. A wind that is excessively dry, violent, and long-lasting. The opposites of these things increase moisture beyond measure.

-># ON THE LONG LIFE<-

Frequent drunkenness does both things: for by excessive heat it dries out the body, and then by moisture it suffocates the drunkard. But nothing is more harmful to both <note>referring to the balance of heat and moisture necessary for life</note> than indigestion <note>original: *cruditas*, meaning "rawness" or undigested food in the stomach</note>. For where nourishment is not thoroughly <term>concocted|The physiological process where the body uses internal heat to 'cook' food into a usable state, or what we now call digestion</term>, on one hand, the substance needed to water the natural moisture <note>the vital fluids believed to keep the body supple and young</note> is missing; on the other hand, decayed matter remains, which, by overflowing, overwhelms the natural heat <note>the internal metabolic fire believed to drive life</note>.

For this reason, Avicenna <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath and one of the most significant physicians of the Islamic Golden Age</note> says that the blood is corrupted whenever digestion itself is corrupted. Following Galen <note>Claudius Galenus (129–c. 216 AD), the Roman physician whose theories of the four humors dominated Western medicine for 1,500 years</note>, he calls digestion the "root of life." 

Therefore, this is the best and almost the only rule of Galen: to care for the digestion of food above all other things. For even what is considered the greatest advice—namely, to use healthy ingredients—will be of no benefit unless you digest them. Indeed, a harmful <term>humor|One of the four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) that determined health and temperament</term> flows from healthy foods just as it does from their opposites if they enter the limbs while still raw <note>undigested</note>. On the other hand, from foods that are even less praised by the medical art, often less harm is received <note>the text cuts off here: likely suggesting that a strong stomach can handle "bad" food better than a weak stomach can handle "good" food</note>...

<summary>This page argues that proper digestion is the foundation of health, noting that even healthy food becomes toxic if the body's 'internal heat' fails to process it. It cites the authorities of Galen and Avicenna to emphasize that digestion is the 'root of life.'</summary>

<meta>This page appears to be from Marsilio Ficino's medical-philosophical treatise (likely "On Life"), as evidenced by the reference to his father, the physician Diotifeci d'Agnolo. It continues advice on the "Six Non-Naturals" of medieval medicine—specifically digestion, exercise, and environment.</meta>

We must also strive most diligently to ensure that we do not hinder our digestion—whether by sexual intercourse immediately after a meal, by frequent and unnecessary afternoon naps, by staying awake through the night, by any ill-timed mental or physical labor, or by any other means. 

When I speak of digestion, I do not mean only the "first" stage, which takes place in the stomach; I also mean the second, which occurs in the liver; the third, which happens within the veins; and finally the fourth, which is completed in the bodily limbs. These processes require a significant interval of time to complete; if any stage is obstructed, the bodily fluid <term>humor: the vital fluids like blood or bile that the Renaissance believed governed health and temperament</term> fails to supply the necessary nourishment. 

¶ Just as we must aid digestion, it is especially necessary for life that we assist in the purging of waste products. It is also necessary to wash away impurities from the skin. We require bodily movement that is as continuous, as balanced, and as varied as the movements of the heavens, the air, the fire, and the water—provided that the requirements for digestion <note>original: "percoctionis"</note> and sleep are respected. One must, however, avoid total exhaustion and physical collapse. 

Furthermore, we grow moldy and decay when kept in the shade. It is under the open sky and in the light that we truly live. This was a saying frequently on the lips of my father, Ficino <note>Diotifeci d’Agnolo di Giusto, the physician to Cosimo de' Medici</note>, who was a distinguished doctor. But to carry these things out successfully, it would be worthwhile to accustom the body from a tender age not so much to city business as to certain rural exercises, and occasionally even to simple, similar nutrients, maintaining a somewhat varied way of life. This is what that wise man often advised me. For he who, at every age, praiseworthy...

### Chapter 5

<margin>All the Greeks, among the precepts most necessary for a long life, command that we be nourished by **good juices** <note>original: *euchimis*, referring to *euchymia*, the production of healthy nutrients during digestion.</note>.</margin> We call <term>euchymia</term> <note>The OCR reads "Euchia," a shorthand for the Greek term for healthy digestive juices.</note> those healthy foods which provide good nourishment—that is, good blood. We call blood "good" when it is not cold, not dry, and not turbid, but warm, moist, and clear. Warm, indeed, with a heat that is not sharp or acrid. Moist, however, with a moisture that is not watery. Clear also, yet not meanwhile too thin. 

Now truly, blood that is too fervent <note>meaning "boiling" or overly hot</note> both sharpens the natural heat and dries out the moisture; and the moisture or heat it introduces is rendered unstable and fleeting. Furthermore, blood that is too moist and prone to wateriness blunts the <term>natural heat</term> <note>The innate warmth believed to be the engine of life and digestion.</note>; it either dulls the natural moisture or forces it to liquefy under heat; it suffocates the heat with its humidity; and altogether, if any portion of the natural moisture is drawn from watery blood, it both rots easily and quickly flows away and dissolves. 

This results in the following: those who eat softer fruits and herbs—unless perhaps they take them rarely and as medicine to soften the bowels—soon fill their veins entirely with a **raw juice** <note>original: *succus crudus*; nutrients that have not been properly "cooked" or processed by the body's internal heat.</note> that is liable to putrefaction. And lest that happen, it is safer instead of such food or...

<summary>This page defines the ideal quality of human blood—balanced in heat and moisture—as the primary requirement for a long life. It warns that consuming too many watery fruits and herbs produces "raw" blood that lacks the stability to sustain the body's natural heat.</summary>

<keywords>longevity, blood quality, humors, euchymia, natural heat, diet, Renaissance medicine, digestion</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of dietary rules and the physiological nature of blood, specifically how its elemental composition affects the "spirit"—the vital link between the body and the soul in Renaissance medicine.</meta>

...are cooked: or at least mixed with bread. Therefore, blood should be neither <term>fiery</term> <note>original: "igneus"; referring to an excess of "yellow bile" or heat that causes agitation.</note> nor watery; rather, it should be **airy**. It should not be like the thicker air, lest it be too inclined to turn into water. Nor should it be like the thinnest, most subtle air, lest it easily catch fire. Instead, it should maintain a moderate substance, in which the "middle air" holds the most dominance. Other nourishments should be included only insofar as they adapt to the rule of this airy quality.

Its substance should not be too thin, lest it generate an unstable moisture and a <term>volatile spirit</term> <note>The "spiritus" was a refined substance in the body that carried life and thought; if it was too "volatile," it would dissipate too quickly, leading to weakness.</note> that is prone to dissolving. It should not be too thick, either; otherwise, it will serve the intellect very little, and will hardly be transformed into natural moisture and spirit. It will block the passages <note>The "meatus" or channels through which humors and spirits flow.</note> and cause suffocation. And the spirit that is eventually created from such thick blood is, because of its density, poorly suited for life; it suffocates the natural heat just as thick smoke immediately suppresses and extinguishes a flame. I will skip over the fact that such blood is so dark that it makes life gloomy and worse than death.

--- Page 169 ---
# ON THE LONG LIFE

Choose carefully those things that produce blood and humors according to one’s strength. For such blood and humor is food for the **vital heat**, just as oil is for a flame, and along with its <term>refinement (subtilitate)</term> <note>In Galenic medicine, "refinement" refers to the thinness or purity of a humor, making it easier for the body to distribute.</note> it also possesses firmness. For it is a precept of **Rhazes** <note>original: "Raſis"; a 9th-century Persian physician whose works were foundational to European medicine.</note> for the preservation of youth to use things that bring blood to the <term>precordia</term> <note>The area around the heart and diaphragm.</note>, thickening it there and nourishing the heart. 

**Avicenna** <note>original: "Auicenna"; the Latinized name of Ibn Sina, author of the *Canon of Medicine*.</note> approves of this, advising that watery and unstable blood is to be avoided. However, a different approach must be taken in this matter according to the different types of bodies. For where the body is denser, one should lean toward the refinement of the blood; but where the body is more porous, one should use all remedies to avoid refining it, leaning instead toward thickening it. Where there is a middle condition of the body, we go more safely by a middle way. Yet we should never attempt to uproot the natural constitution of the body; otherwise, we would uproot life itself.

It is furthermore helpful to remember that when we greatly fear the thinness of the blood, and the stomach is not particularly strong by nature, we must proceed by smaller steps toward thickening it. This is done while we strive to nourish a slender man with thicker foods; meanwhile, the stomach must be supported, sleep induced, and bodily exercise should be performed according to one's strength. At the same time, the exercise of the mind—which very often harms many people—should be reduced. And if the person cannot bear foods that are too viscous, hard, or very cold, let him at least use **corals**, **sandalwood**, **roses**, **coriander**, **myrobalans** <note>A dried plum-like fruit used as an astringent.</note>, and **quinces** <note>original: "cidionioruz"</note>, and so on.

For it is difficult to lead viscous or firmer foods from a stomach that is not very strong through to the <term>third and fourth digestions</term><note>In the medical theory of this era, digestion happened in four stages: the first in the stomach, the second in the liver to produce blood, and the third and fourth within the vessels and the actual tissues of the limbs.</note>, unless they are carried by such "vehicles" and encouraged by light rubbing. When you perform these rubbings, do them with very soft hands, and remember to moisten the hands with a certain fragrant...

->ON PROLONGING LIFE.<-
