[A general rule for eating and drinking, and the quality of feasts. Chapter VI.]

**S**et aside for the present those human bodies that are either extremely sluggish or extremely thin; let us instead arrive at a common way of living, one entirely suited to the common and middle-of-the-road constitution of the body. 

Take care that by no means the passages <note>original: "meatus"; the pores or internal channels of the body</note> of the body are either opened beyond measure or too greatly obstructed. For in the former case <note>excessive openness</note> there is a risk of dissolution and injury brought in from the outside; in the latter <note>obstruction</note>, however, there is danger of putrefaction <note>the 'rotting' of undigested food or stagnant fluids</note> and suffocation. And if I do not restrain you with the tightest bridle of rules—which Hippocrates <note>The ancient Greek physician who warned that an overly strict diet could be as dangerous as an overly indulgent one.</note> condemns—nevertheless, I do not loosen the reins for you to the point of license. 

Take moist herbs and fruits sparingly. Take milk and fish even more sparingly, and both with honey. Take mushrooms most sparingly of all, and only with aromatics and pear seeds. Likewise, be sparing with the drinking of pure water, and use it only in small amounts. 

Season those foods that are more moist or fatty with aromatic and sharp spices; otherwise, they bring into the limbs a great deal of foreign and putrid moisture. Even if they supply some moisture necessary to nature, they provide it in a way that is subject to very quick corruption. This is no different from how watery wine is quickly disturbed and spoiled. From this comes rapid graying <note>original: "canities"; Ficino believed that bad digestion and "watery" blood led to premature gray hair and aging.</note>...

They are therefore best suited for similar bodies, such as those of peasants and robust people who exercise their bodies a lot—especially if the meat is preserved for four days with cloves <note>original: "gariofilis"</note>, prepared coriander, and seasoned with salt. And blood might perhaps be useful if it is cooked with sugar and thoroughly purified <note>original: "defecatus," meaning filtered or cleared of dregs</note> until it is liquid.

But to return to our list, excessively moist meats are not recommended, as we have said; nor are those that are both hard and dry, such as the meat of an older hare or ox. Instead, choose those that are in between, such as: poultry, chickens, capons, peacocks, pheasants, partridges, and perhaps even young pigeons, especially domestic ones. Young roe deer and calves are also of this sort, as are yearling **wethers** <term>wether: a castrated ram, considered in this period to provide more balanced and tender meat than an intact ram</term> and wild boars. I do not reject suckling kids or fresh cheese.

I have indeed omitted small birds. For the frequent use of overly refined <note>original: "subtiliorum"</note> foods results in the stomach being unable to tolerate coarser ones at all. But a person who is stronger may experience a "fleeting smoke" or "humor" from these. However, I do not omit chicken eggs, provided the yolk is eaten together with the white. For the yolk alone is the nourishment of delicate people; indeed, Avicenna <note>The Latinized name of Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose "Canon of Medicine" was a core textbook for Renaissance doctors.</note> proves that no nourishment is more beneficial for the loss of blood and the dissolution of the **cardiac spirit** <term>cardiac spirit: in Renaissance physiology, a vital substance or "gas" generated in the heart that carries life-force and heat through the arteries.</term> than the yolk of a hen, partridge, or pheasant egg. Nor, perhaps, would it be beside the point to feed geese...

<meta>This page continues dietary and lifestyle advice, specifically focusing on the preparation of meat and the balance of the "humors" (the bodily fluids thought to control health) through food, exercise, and sleep.</meta>

...spelt or clear water; and after the slaughter, the meat should be seasoned with salt and coriander prepared in vinegar and kept for seven days before you eat it. Treat venison similarly, provided that the stomach is very strong. For it is probable that certain long-lived animals <note>The author suggests a "sympathetic magic" or early biological theory: eating animals that naturally live a long time might pass that longevity to the consumer.</note> contribute to a long life, provided that young people eat meat of this kind; and similarly, let other meats be taken in turn, both roasted and boiled. 

Let the amount of food be double the amount of drink. Bread should be double the weight [of drink], and six times <note>original: "sex galter," likely a specific weight or ratio measure used in the period</note> for eggs; triple for meat; and quadruple for fish, herbs, and more moist fruits. 

The meal should not begin with a drink, nor should any single drink be too large. Something <term>styptic</term> <note>original: "stipticũ." In this context, a substance that contracts tissues—meant to "close" the stomach after eating to aid digestion.</note> should always follow the meal, taken without a drink or with very little. 

When your <term>complexion</term> <note>A person's "complexion" was their unique balance of the four humors: blood, phlegm, black bile, and yellow bile.</note>, age, location, or the season leans toward the hot or the dry, you too should lean a little toward the opposite qualities. When it leans toward the cold or the moist, similarly turn toward the opposites. Where there is a natural balance, maintain that balance. 

Furthermore, physical exercise should be increased and mental exertion decreased to the extent that we eat tougher foods, which are perhaps sometimes necessary for a long duration of life. Let there be two meals within nine hours of the day, and be sparing in both. Indeed, let the dinner be more sparing than the midday meal. There should be roughly two periods of physical exercise after the first digestion is complete, carried out almost to the point of sweating. 

Nighttime sleep is always good because it is always necessary. But daytime sleep is never good, unless it is extremely necessary.

<meta>This page continues the discourse on longevity, moving from general principles to specific environmental and dietary recommendations. It introduces Chapter VII, focusing on the quality of food sources and the influence of geography on health.</meta>

# ON THE LONG LIFE

Do not use foods that rot quickly, nor live in such regions. Choose wine and wheat above all others; flee decay and dissolution.

### Chapter VII

**A**ny animals that come into our care ought to be nourished with clean and select foods before we eat them. These and all other things should be chosen from higher and fragrant pastures. Among these rules, always keep in mind one approved above all others by the philosopher Arnaldo <note>Arnaldus de Villa Nova (c. 1240–1311), a celebrated physician and alchemist whose works were central to medieval and Renaissance medicine.</note>: that animals, herbs, fruits, produce, and wines ought to be chosen from high and fragrant regions, as we have said. 

These are places which temperate winds clear and the sweet rays of the sun cherish; where no waters stagnate, and the cultivated lands are not made fat with manure <note>original: "sterquilinio"</note>, but by their own natural moisture. In such places, whatever is grown remains uncorrupted for a long time. Here only should one live; on these things also one should feed. 

Nor should we trust that from foods which rot in a short time, we will acquire a lasting <term>humor</term> <note>In the medical tradition of this period, "humors" were the vital bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—that governed health, temperament, and aging.</note> that is resistant to decay. Nor can we hope that we will easily live for a long time in a place where the fruits of the earth are not preserved uncorrupted for long, or where long-lived men are very rare. 

Indeed, the peach <note>original: "pomum persicum," literally "Persian apple."</note> demonstrates how great a difference there is in location and diet: in Persia it is considered a poison, but in Egypt it is a "friend of the heart." Likewise, hellebore <note>A plant used in antiquity to treat mental illness, though highly toxic if the dosage or location of growth was incorrect.</note> is taken without harm in Anticyra <note>A Greek city famous in the ancient world for producing the best hellebore.</note>, but elsewhere it is a poison. Aristotle says that the dwelling...

[One] chooses [a site] situated high up, facing the south and east, beneath thin air that is neither damp nor cold. And <term>Plato<note>The Greek philosopher, who in his dialogues often linked physical health and the environment to the pursuit of wisdom.</note></term> found that long-lived people were discovered in the highest and most temperate regions. It is indeed the worst practice to manure the fields, or to fail to drain stagnant waters away from the fields. For everything born in such places is subject to swift corruption. For this reason, I cannot help but criticize those who, on that account, criticize the wise <term>Hesiod<note>An 8th-century BCE Greek poet whose work "Works and Days" provided advice on agriculture and morality.</note></term> because he omitted the use of a dung-heap in his work on farming. But that prudent man looked to health rather than to fertility. He thought that a field could be sufficiently enriched by the leaves of lupines and beans, if they were turned over <note>original: "subversis"; referring to the practice of "green manure," where crops are plowed back into the soil to provide nutrients.</note> into the soil at the right time. 

But if we are compelled to live in more humid and unclean regions, and to consume food that is likely to spoil quickly, let us observe that way of life which doctors have commanded for use in pestilential air. We have dealt with this matter sufficiently in the book against the plague. In short, we shall use sweet and somewhat warm scents. 

By means of properly prepared aloe, we shall be purged often and gently. We say "properly prepared" if it has been washed with rose water, or the juice of roses, or if it has been perfectly mixed with fresh crushed roses. Then let <term>myrobalan<note>A dried, plum-like fruit used in medieval and Renaissance medicine as a gentle laxative and to "purge" bad humors.</note></term> be added to it, along with <term>mastic<note>A resin from the mastic tree, frequently used in this period to strengthen the stomach and aid digestion.</note></term>, and perhaps rose. This medicine is beyond controversy wonderful for preserving a sound mind in a sound body <note>original: "mentem sanam in corpore sano", a famous phrase by the Roman poet Juvenal emphasizing the link between physical and mental health.</note> for a long time. Furthermore, we shall exercise the body. We shall use a suitable hearth. We shall season our feasts with this powder, [and] myroba-

<meta>This page continues the medical and dietary advice from Marsilio Ficino's 'De Vita Longa' (On Long Life), specifically focusing on preventative measures against the decay of the body’s humors.</meta>

->ON LONG LIFE<-

Let there be a fourth part of an ounce of emblic myrobalans <note>Indian gooseberry (Phyllanthus emblica), a fruit used in Renaissance medicine to cool the body and aid digestion</note>; of sandalwood <margin><unclear>unclear</unclear></margin> let there be a half part; of cinnamon, a whole part; and an eighth part of saffron. With this powder, and alongside other pungent substances, we may perhaps be able to inhibit the corruption <note>original: "corruptionem" — in this context, the "rotting" or imbalance of the body's internal fluids (humors) caused by external factors</note> threatening us from putrid foods and stagnant places. Let us remember, however: where many more people perish from putrefaction <term>putrefaction</term> <note>the Renaissance medical theory that disease is caused by the "rotting" of the body's humors</note> and suffocation than from dissolution <note>the "leaking" or thinning out of the body's vital spirits and heat</note>, there we must resist putrefaction and suffocation most strongly. Where the opposite occurs, one should instead use aromatic seasonings that are somewhat harsh <note>original: "austeris" — often meaning dry or astringent</note> (as we have said); such scents at any time prevent putrefaction. 

Anointing oneself with oil prevents the injury of cold. Using baths made of water and oil prevents the dissolution that threatens after hard labor or during hot seasons. Similarly, one should often rinse the mouth with water and hold licorice juice or crystalline sugar in the mouth. Pour plenty of rose water and a little rose vinegar over the hands and face, and use similar scents. Every seven hours, refresh yourself with moderate amounts of food; rest both the body and the mind, while avoiding excessive heat. 

It is of the greatest importance what kind of wine and wheat we consume regularly. These should be of such quality that they remain intact beyond a year, and preferably up to three years; for we must hope to receive incorruptible nourishment from them. Whether the wine is white or red, let it be clear, pleasant, astringent <note>original: "stypticum" — a quality believed to "bind" the body and prevent the thinning of the humors</note>, fragrant, and of the sort that requires dilution with water—unless by chance you find a wine that is light and yet long-lasting, which is usually a very rare thing.

--- Page 178 ---
But what is even more powerful: the philosopher Isaac <note>Isaac Israeli ben Solomon (c. 832–932), an influential Jewish physician and philosopher whose medical works were standard texts in medieval Europe.</note> says that wine is "vinous" juice cooked by the sun and purified by the winds. He orders this to be tempered with pure spring water some time before we drink it, so that it may be more perfectly blended. However, he warns us to avoid watery wine, as well as wine that is weak or bitter, seeing as it quickly becomes acidic within the veins and limbs, or otherwise putrefies. Indeed, watery wine is subject to decay; yet, if its substance is preserved and it is boiled, it will at least be useful—otherwise, it is not recommended—because it will not create corruptible humors <note>In ancient medicine, "humors" were the bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) that determined health and temperament.</note>. But its sharpness must be tempered with chosen water.

Furthermore, regarding the kind of wine we have approved, Isaac says—according to the opinion of the ancients—that it is very similar to the great <term>theriacs</term> <note>Theriac was a famous "universal" medicine and antidote, often containing dozens of ingredients, used to restore balance to the body.</note>. This wine (as we have said), when properly tempered, warms a cold bodily constitution, cools one that is overheated, moistens a dry one, but dries out one that is too moist; and (as Galen <note>Aelius Galenus (129–c. 216 AD), the most famous physician of the Roman Empire, whose theories dominated Western medicine for over a thousand years.</note> says) it restores the natural humor, fosters heat, and balances both.

Mixing wine in this way is more necessary for the young, less so for the old, and for "cold" elderly men, not at all. For a cold and hard old age (as Plato says) is heated and softened by pure wine just as iron is softened by fire or a lupin bean <note>Plato’s *Laws* suggests that wine acts as a medicine for the "hardness" of old age, making the soul more pliable, much like soaking a hard lupin bean in water.</note> by water. What we said about wine producing opposite effects and tempering conflicting states: know that this also occurs through <term>licorice</term> <note>original: "glycirhizam"</note>, though more weakly. It also happens through oil...

# ¶ ON LONG LIFE.

...rose oil: but applied externally. Let these things, therefore, be familiar to you. Nor should you doubt that whatever is tempered in its quality and powerful in its virtue is able to balance other things: just as cold makes other things cold. Consider this above all to possess the temperament of Jupiter: by which things are also rendered most healthful. But about these matters, we must argue elsewhere.

### ¶ Diet: Food: Medicine for the Elderly. ¶ Chapter VIII.

¶ Those who have now completed their seventh seven-year cycle <note>The "septenary" was a traditional way of measuring life stages; 7 cycles of 7 years equals 49 years.</note> and are reaching their fiftieth year, let them consider that Venus signifies the young, while Saturn signifies the old, and that among astronomers these stars are the most hostile to each other of all. Therefore, let those of Saturn <note>the elderly</note> flee the business of Venus <note>sexual intercourse</note>: which detracts greatly from life even for the young. For it does not provide for those already born, but for those yet to be born; and it even dries out plants themselves as soon as the seed is produced.

Furthermore, they should consider cold and the nocturnal air to be deadly to them. And let them observe a diet entirely from which they may hope for the most blood and the most <term>spirit</term> <note>Original: "spiritum." In Renaissance medicine, "spirit" was a subtle, vaporous substance that linked the soul to the physical body and powered the senses and movement.</note>: namely, from the yolks of fresh eggs and from wine that is somewhat sweet, but especially fragrant. For this yolk specifically restores the blood of the heart, while wine especially restores the spirit. In short, let them use the choicest meats that are easiest to cook in every diet that increases heat and moisture in equal measure. Let them constantly restore the spirit with scents, especially those of wine. Let them avoid wakefulness, hunger, and thirst...

## On gold, golden foods, and the recreation of the elderly. Chapter 10.

Therefore, gold is able to wonderfully balance natural heat with moisture, and to keep the humors from corruption. It infuses a **Solar** and **Jovial** <note>pertaining to the planet Jupiter</note> virtue into the spirits and limbs. But yet...

### On the use of milk and human blood for the life of the elderly. Chapter XI.

**S**often, immediately after the tenth—and sometimes even after the ninth—seven-year cycle <note>original: "septennarium"; This refers to the "Climacteric years." The ninth cycle is 63 years old, and the tenth is 70 years old, seen as major turning points in human health.</note>, the human tree gradually wastes away as its moisture dries up. Then, first of all, this human tree must be irrigated with a human and youthful liquid so that it may turn green again. 

Therefore, choose a healthy, beautiful, cheerful, and temperate girl, and while you are hungry, suckle her milk during the waxing moon. Immediately afterward, eat a small amount of powder made from sweet fennel <term>maratri: fennel, often used in historical medicine to aid digestion and "thin" the blood or milk</term> properly prepared with sugar. The sugar will not permit the milk to curdle or spoil in the stomach, while the fennel—because it is both a "subtle" substance and a friend to milk—will distribute it to the limbs. 

For those whom a "wasting of old age" <term>hectica senilis: a chronic, consuming fever or "consumption" thought to dry out the body's natural moisture in old age</term> has overcome, diligent doctors strive to restore them with the liquid of human blood that has been distilled over a fire with sublime art. What, then, prevents us from occasionally refreshing those who are already nearly worn out by old age with this drink? A certain common and true...

<meta>This page continues the discourse on prolonging life from the previous section, likely from Book II of Marsilio Ficino’s "Three Books on Life" (De Vita Libri Tres), specifically the chapter on "On the Long Life." This section discusses the controversial medical practice of consuming blood for rejuvenation.</meta>

There is a common belief that certain elderly sorceresses, who are also called <term>screech-owls</term> <note>original: "striges." In Roman folklore, these were mythical birds or witches believed to feed on the flesh and blood of infants.</note> in the common tongue, suck the blood of infants so that they may regain their youthful strength as much as possible. Why then should our own elderly, who are clearly destitute of all other help, not also suck the blood of a youth? I mean a youth who is willing, healthy, happy, and <term>tempered</term> <note>A "tempered" person, in Renaissance medicine, was someone whose four bodily humors—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—were in perfect balance.</note>; one whose blood is indeed of the best quality but perhaps excessive in quantity. Let them suck, therefore, in the manner of leeches, from a vein in the left arm that has barely been opened, taking perhaps one or two ounces. 

Immediately afterward, they should take an equal amount of sugar and wine; they should do this when they are hungry and thirsty, and during a waxing moon. If the blood is raw and difficult to digest, let it first be cooked with sugar, or let it be moderately distilled over hot water while mixed with sugar. Then it should be drunk. 

To warm the stomach with pig's blood is also a ready aid at that time. Let a sponge, soaked in wine that has been warmed, drink up this blood as it flows from a pig's vein, and let it be applied warm to the stomach immediately. <term>Galen</term> <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–216 AD), a Greek physician whose work formed the basis of Western medical science for over a thousand years.</note> and <term>Serapion</term> <note>Serapion the Elder was a 9th-century Syrian physician known for his medical compilations.</note> say that the bite of a rabid dog is cured by drinking the blood of a dog. However, they did not see fit to assign a reason for this. 

I, therefore, searching for a reason for two days, finally conclude that the saliva of a rabid dog—impressed with poison—ascends from a man's injured foot through the veins little by little toward the heart, much like a poison, unless something distracts it in the meantime. If, therefore, the man drinks the blood of another dog in the interval, that raw blood floats in the stomach for many hours, and finally, just as pe— <note>The text cuts off here mid-word; it likely intended to say "pervades" or "perceives."</note>

<margin>The marginal notes here are partially cut off by the edge of the original page and are illegible.</margin>

It attempts to provide a "rational" medical explanation for traditional folk beliefs and the cures proposed by ancient physicians like Galen.</summary>

### ON LONG LIFE

...to be expelled through the bowels. Meanwhile, dog blood diverts that canine saliva—which grips the upper limbs—before it reaches the <term>heart</term> <note>original: "praecordia"; this refers generally to the midriff or the internal organs surrounding the heart and diaphragm.</note>, drawing it instead toward the stomach. For there is a <term>virtue</term> <note>original: "uirtus"; in Renaissance medicine, this refers to an inherent power or specific property of a substance rather than moral virtue.</note> in dog blood for attracting the saliva of a dog; and in turn, there is a power in the saliva to pursue similar blood. The poison, therefore, removed from the heart and absorbed by the blood floating in the belly, is led down through the lower parts along with the blood, leaving the person unharmed. 

Why say all this? First, indeed, so that I might reveal the hidden cause of such an obscure remedy while explaining it. Second, to advise that blood can be drunk, and indeed healthily. And that in human blood there is a power by which it attracts human blood and pursues it mutually. Do not, therefore, doubt that youthful blood drunk by an old person can be drawn to the veins and limbs, and be of the greatest benefit there.

...[if one] has digested, to take nourishment: it distracts and fatigues nature; from which weariness, if it happens frequently, untimely old age flies in. 

In winter, let the elderly seek sunny spots like sheep; in summer, let them revisit pleasant places and streams like birds. Let them frequently spend time among green and sweet-smelling plants. For these living and breathing things breathe out to increase the spirit of man. <note>In Renaissance medicine, the "spirit" (*spiritus*) was a subtle, airy substance that acted as a bridge between the physical body and the soul.</note> Indeed, let them take refuge in places generally friendly to bees. 

And let them taste honey in winter. For honey is a food especially friendly to the elderly, unless they fear the "kindling of bile." <term>Bile: In the humoral system of medicine, yellow bile was considered hot and dry; "kindling" it meant causing excessive internal heat or inflammation.</term> Very fresh cheese is a friend. Dates are friends: figs, raisins, capers, sweet pomegranates, ginger, hyssop, scabious, and betony; but pistachios are much more so. 

Pine nuts, however, are the most helpful of all, as we have said. From these they will receive the greatest aid if they keep them in a little warm water for twelve hours before they eat them. For in this way, they will not harm the stomach. 

And furthermore, if while they use these, they also spend time among pine groves, olive groves, and vineyards; or at least receive the vapor and scent of the pine. Likewise, let them often soothe the body with the gum and "tears" <note>original: *lachrimo*; referring to the resin or sap of the tree</note> of the pine mixed with oil or wine. 

For it is probable that trees naturally endowed with a long life—especially if they remain green even in winter—will benefit you toward a long life through their shade, vapor, fresh fruit, wood, and any timely use. Regarding long-lived animals, however, we have spoken above. Now...

# ON LONG LIFE.

...perhaps it will be just as beneficial for you if you live as much as possible among those who are healthy, who are similar to you in nature, and who are your friends. This is perhaps even more effective if they are somewhat younger than you. As to whether and how frequent association with the young is able to delay old age for a short while, we should consult the modest Socrates <note>This refers to the concept of 'gerocomy,' an ancient belief that the vitality of young people could be transferred to the elderly through proximity or breath.</note>.

### What aids the elderly should receive from the planets to cherish all their limbs. Chapter 13.

**B**ut instead, you anxious elders, consult Apollo—the one who judged Socrates to be the wisest of the Greeks. Consult Jupiter as well, and Venus. Phoebus [the Sun] himself, the discoverer of the medical art, will give you nutmeg to warm your stomach. Jupiter, along with Phoebus, provides mastic <note>A resin from the mastic tree, historically used to aid digestion</note> and mint. Venus, however, provides coral.

For warming the head: Phoebus provides peony, frankincense, marjoram, and myrrh combined with savory. Jupiter provides spikenard <note>An expensive, aromatic herbal oil from the Himalayas</note> and mace. Venus, finally, provides sweet fennel and myrtle.

To warm the heart, you shall receive from Phoebus <term>citraria</term> <note>original: "citrariam"; likely lemon balm (Melissa officinalis), used to "make the heart merry."</note>. Also saffron, aloe wood, frankincense, ambergris, musk, <term>doronicum</term> <note>original: "doronicum"; Leopard's bane, a plant used in traditional heart tonics.</note>, a small amount of clove, citron peel, and cinnamon. From Jupiter, you shall receive lily, bugloss <note>A plant used to treat melancholy and "purify the blood."</note>, basil, and mint, as well as "been" roots <note>original: "beeniaſque radices"; likely refers to the White and Red Behen (Centaurea behen and Statice limonium), common in medieval pharmacy.</note>, both the white and the red varieties. From Venus alone, you shall receive myrtle, sandalwood, and rose. In conjunction with Saturn, she provides coriander.

Grind these things diligently: and for those which pertain to the stomach, prepare them with oil of quinces into the form of a <term>cerate</term><note>A cerate is a stiff ointment made of wax, oil, and medicinal substances, designed to be applied to the skin without melting.</note>. These are to be applied externally to the <term>precordia</term><note>The region of the body immediately in front of the heart or the pit of the stomach.</note>.

For this organ, therefore, <term>Phoebus</term><note>Phoebus is a poetic name for the Sun, which in medical astrology was believed to govern specific plants and bodily functions.</note> will always provide aid with agrimony and <term>opobalsam</term><note>A rare, aromatic resin also known as Balm of Mecca.</note>. ¶ A conversation of old men under Venus among green meadows. ¶ Chapter 14.

**B**ut from these weightier deities <note>referring to the more somber planets like Saturn</note>, I recall you old men for a while to Venus, through gardens and green meadows. I call you all to life-giving Venus. She is not indeed playing games with you, but being cheerful. She says this both to you and to me, for I am now an old man myself. First, she pours out this playful oracle: "My sons, if you do not know it, I gave you life through pleasure and movement. I, therefore, through a certain pleasure and movement—even if not the same kind as before—will preserve your life." 

The god <term>Liber</term><note>The Roman name for Bacchus, the god of wine and freedom.</note>, free in his own liberty, will also preserve it: he is the sower of the vine and the propagator of life. Liber himself always hates slaves; and the long life he promises through wine, he fulfills only for those who are <term>free</term><note>original: "liberis". Ficino is making a pun on the god's name, *Liber*, and the Latin word for free people, *liberi*. He suggests that wine and joy are for those with a free spirit, not those enslaved by worry.</note>. 

To my own life and mind alike, back when Saturn was reigning, <term>diminished mint</term> was once beneficial, and it pleases me daily. To you, however, a <term>greater mint</term> profits the mind and life. A diminished one harms. <note>original: "menta/menti". There is a wordplay here between *mentha* (the herb mint) and *mens* (the mind). Ficino suggests that as one ages, the "mint" or "mind" must be kept robust rather than diminished or "chopped up."</note> 

Gather laughter from my gardens. Neglect what is dry. When you pluck these violets, consider that you are plucking lilies; when grasping a lily, imagine you are holding saffron. Jupiter himself, having obtained saffron from Phoebus <note>Apollo, the sun god</note>, propagated it into the lily. I, receiving the lily from Jupiter, transformed it into these violets which you see here. Finally, let the rose be to you a light...

...Evening, but the myrtle. After the oracle, he <note>The "oracle" likely refers to a preceding authoritative statement or a classical source being cited by the author.</note> commands us to consider the nature of green things: insofar as they are green, they are not only alive, but even youthful; they overflow with a completely healthy moisture and a certain vivid <term>spirit|spiritus: in Renaissance thought, a subtle, airy substance that connects the soul to the physical body and transmits sensory information</term>. Therefore, through their scent, sight, use, and frequent habitation among them, a youthful spirit flows from them into us.

While walking among the greenery, we shall investigate the inner cause: Why the color green, above all others, nourishes the sight and delights it in a healthy way. We will find at last that the nature of sight is luminous and a friend of light; yet it is volatile and easily scattered. For this reason, while it expands through light as if toward a friend, it is sometimes snatched away by an excessive amount of light and dissolved by that violent expansion. Darkness, however, it flees naturally as an enemy, and therefore it draws its rays back into a narrow space. 

Sight desires to enjoy light in such a way that it is indeed amplified by this friend of its, but not scattered in the process. Now, in any color where there is much more darkness or blackness than light, the ray of sight does not expand, and thus is not delighted as it wishes. Where, conversely, there is much more brilliant color than black, it is spread wider, distracted by a certain harmful pleasure. Therefore, the color green—tempering black with white <note>In the color theory of this period, all colors were seen as a mixture of white (pure light) and black (pure shadow).</note> most of all—provides both: delighting and preserving at the same time. And moreover, with a soft and still tender quality, just as water reflects the rays... <margin>...the sight of the eyes... further... appearance... bedewed... ...it resists the eyes without causing offense, lest the rays, departing further, should be dispersed and lost. For those things which are both hard and rough break the rays in a certain way. However, those things which are very thin <note>The Latin *rariſſima* refers to materials with low density or porous structure</note> open a path for the rays to dissolve. But those things which possess a certain solidity, and at the same time a smooth evenness—such as mirror-like bodies—neither break the rays nor allow them to be scattered too far.

Finally, those things which, in addition to these benefits, are also tender and soft—such as water and green objects—soothe the liquid rays of the eyes with their softness. Ultimately, the ray of sight is a kind of light naturally kindled within the watery substance of our eyes. It requires a tempered light within that water, which offers a slight resistance. Therefore, the sight rejoices in water. It is delighted by mirrors that resemble water. It is charmed by green things. In these green things, the indwelling light of the sun still retains a spring-like moisture and a subtle water filled with a certain hidden light. This is also why the color green, when it is thinned out, dissolves into a saffron hue <note>original: *croceum*; this refers to the yellowing of leaves as they dry or age</note>.

Why do we say all this? So that we may understand that the frequent use of greenery—insofar as it restores the <term>visual spirit</term> <note>In the medical theory of this period, "spirits" were subtle vapors that acted as the primary instruments of the soul for sensation and movement</note> (which is in some way the most important part of the <term>animal spirit</term> <note>The *spiritus animalis* was the highest grade of these vapors, responsible for brain function and the senses</note>)—also restores the animal spirit itself. And let us also remember: if the color green—because it is the middle and most temperate among the degrees of colors—benefits the animal spirit so much, how much more do those things which are most temperate in their qualities benefit the natural and...

...gathers the spirit in the same way, so it delights and refreshes him inwardly, just as greenness outdoors delights the eyes. It preserves this spirit even among the elderly in a certain natural greenness <note>The author uses "greenness" (viriditas) as a metaphor for youthful vigor and life force.</note> for a very long time, like the laurel, the olive, or the pine, which stay green even in winter. It must be preserved in this very form, so that it may be subtle and yet firm, as we have already said. Let it be entirely bright, but also in some way solid. Furthermore, let it be continually nourished by fragrant, firm, and bright things if we wish to preserve life—which flourishes in the spirit—and to claim celestial gifts for ourselves. <insert>¶</insert> <note>The original contains a blue hand-drawn paragraph mark here.</note> You know that there are five senses: sight, hearing, smell, taste, and touch. Learn, in turn, that there are (so to speak) five modes of reason <note>original: "rationes." In this context, it refers to the logical frameworks or cognitive processes the mind uses to interpret sensory data.</note>. For while your mind is daily steeped in information through the five senses, and from these it conceives for itself the reasons of things, meanwhile five notions and habits for judging things result, acting as five modes of reason. Furthermore, just as there are five senses here, and from them, in a certain way, five modes of reason, so the course of life is arranged in five stages based on sense and reason. From this, five ages of man are counted:

The **First Age** is indeed drawn by sense alone.

The **Second Age** is attracted much more by sense than it is led by reason.

The **Third Age**, after these, is driven equally by the alternating persuasions of reason and sense.

The **Fourth Age** is led by reason rather than by sense.

The **Fifth Age**, finally, ought to be ruled by reason alone.

Therefore, let the first and second ages, being subject to Venus, listen while Venus is speaking (if you please). But let the remaining ages listen to Mercury. I, therefore, address all the rest of you: not indeed for my own sake...

<meta>This page continues a moral-philosophical discourse regarding the planetary deities and their influence on the human soul, specifically focusing on the dangers of sensuality personified as Venus.</meta>

You have seen **Venus**, looking exceedingly youthful, and as if polished with the cosmetics and ornaments of a prostitute. She, therefore, who is always new and always strives for new things, hates what is old. She destroys what has been made so that she may build what is yet to be done. Again, if it is right to say so, like a prostitute she is not content with any one man. She loves the common crowd. And to speak <term>dialectically: using the methods of philosophical logic and classification</term>, she favors the species everywhere rather than the individual.

<note>The red bracket in the margin marks a transition from describing Venus's nature to her specific effects on the senses.</note> Now indeed, she does not merely cast you down through touch; she also deceives you daily through taste, and destroys those she has tricked. Those flavors which you perceive in things as pleasant through a certain moderate temperament, these **Diana** <note>original: "Diana"; here representing natural, wholesome purity</note> has delivered by the gift of **Apollo** and **Jupiter**. But the wondrous allurements of flavors, by which you—wretched people—are daily caught as if by a hook and secretly lose your lives, these the treacherous **Venus** manufactures. 

Why then do you accuse **Mars**? Why **Saturn**? **Mars**, indeed, harms you quite rarely and does so openly. **Saturn** also more often declares himself an enemy by his countenance; he harms more slowly, and denies no one the time for seeking remedies. Only **Venus** comes openly as a friend, but acts secretly as an enemy. It is better, therefore, to accuse her, if it is permitted to accuse anyone among the gods. 

Against her manifold snares: first, equip your eyes like those of **Argus** <note>original: "argiuos"; referring to Argus Panoptes, the mythological hundred-eyed giant who was always watchful</note>; then, arm yourselves with the shield of **Pallas** <note>original: "Palladis"; Pallas Athena, goddess of wisdom</note>. But block your ears against her flattering promises as if against the lethal songs of the **Sirens**. Finally, receive from me this flower of providence, by which the sorceries of this **Circe** <note>The mythical enchantress who transformed men into animals</note>...

<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino's argument from the previous section concerning how celestial influences (the "rays" of the stars) can physically inhabit material objects like metals and gemstones.</meta>

# ->ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS<-
<note>original: "DE VITA CAELITVS." This is the third book of Marsilio Ficino’s *Three Books on Life* (1489), a seminal work on Renaissance magic and medicine that explains how to use astrology and "spirit" to improve health.</note>

...and the lower regions: it joins light with heat. They think, moreover, that the fire blowing from the center <note>of the earth</note> is the Vestal fire. Indeed, they used to think that Vesta was the life and the <term>divinity</term> <note>original: "numen," meaning a presiding spirit or divine presence</note> of the earth. For this reason, the ancients used to build the temple of Vesta in the middle of cities, and they would place a perpetual fire in the center.

But let us not wander further. Let us now conclude that if the rays of the stars readily penetrate the whole earth, it cannot easily be denied that they suddenly penetrate metal and small stones when they are carved with <term>images</term> <note>astrological or talismanic figures intended to "catch" the power of a specific planet or constellation</note>. They impress upon these items wonderful or at least some kind of <term>gifts</term> <note>original: "dotes," referring to the specific properties or powers granted by the stars</note>. Since they also generate the most precious things in the bellies <note>the deep recesses or "wombs"</note> of the earth, who would deny that the rays penetrate through these? Indeed, even air—and its quality and sound—which is less effective than light, passes through solids suddenly and affects them with its own quality.

Now, if hardness resisted the penetrating rays, light would pass through the air much faster than it passes through water. And it would pass through water much faster than glass, and glass much faster than crystal. But since light strikes through all solids in the same moment that it strikes through liquids, it is clearly evident that hardness in no way resists the rays. Therefore, one must say it cannot be denied that metals receive the rays and <term>influences</term> <note>original: "influxus," the celestial energy flowing from the stars into the material world</note> of the heavens. They also preserve them for a time destined for them from above. They preserve, therefore, a certain <term>virtue</term> <note>original: "virtutem," the inherent power or medicinal property of a thing</note> created from the contact of these converging rays.

What then? If harder matter—by the very fact that it seems to resist a powerful cause—actually exposes itself more to the blows? Just as a sword cuts through wood [placed] under wool <note>Ficino's analogy: just as a blade might pass through soft wool to strike the hard wood beneath, celestial rays are more effectively "captured" by hard objects like metal or stone that offer a firm surface for the influence to "hit."</note>.

->n.iii.<- <note>This is a printer's signature mark, used by bookbinders to assemble the pages in order.</note>

<summary>Ficino argues that celestial rays penetrate all matter, including solid metals and stones, just as easily as they pass through air or water. This provides the physical basis for creating astrological talismans that store celestial "virtue."</summary>
<keywords>celestial rays, Vesta, talismans, astrological images, virtue, influence, transparency, Marsilio Ficino</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the nature of celestial influences and how they interact with physical matter, specifically focusing on the "rays" emitted by stars and planets.</meta>

...the wool remains unshorn. In the same way, that lightning bolt sometimes dissolves the metal inside a leather pouch without the leather being harmed. <note>original: "non incisa lana. Sic radius ille fulmineo corio quandoque non laeso dissolvit in eo metallum." This refers to a common Renaissance observation of "cold melting," where lightning affects a conductor while leaving the container intact—used here to illustrate how celestial forces can pass through matter.</note> Since, however, celestial nature is incomparably more excellent than this fire of ours, one should not think that the function of a celestial ray is only the kind of work we clearly see a ray of fire perform—namely: to illuminate, to heat, to dry, to penetrate, to thin out, and to dissolve. These effects are very well known to our senses. 

But these rays possess many more and far more wonderful powers and effects. Otherwise, inferior matter and our fleeting senses would be made entirely equal to the divinity of the heavens. But who is unaware that the "hidden virtues" of things—which physicians call "specific properties"—do not arise from elemental nature, but from the celestial? <term>specific properties: medicinal or physical effects that cannot be explained simply by the balance of the four elements (hot, cold, moist, dry).</term> Therefore, as they say, these rays can imprint hidden and wonderful powers upon images <note>original: "imaginibus"—this likely refers to astrological talismans or symbols used in natural magic</note> beyond the qualities we already know, just as they instill them into other things. 

For these rays are not inanimate like the light from a lamp. Instead, they are living and sentient, as if they were flashing from the eyes of living bodies. They carry wondrous gifts with them from the imaginations and minds of the celestial beings. <note>In this context, the "celestial beings" likely refer to the "Intellects" or souls associated with the planets and stars in Neoplatonic philosophy.</note> They also exert a most powerful force arising from their strong disposition and the incredibly rapid motion of the celestial bodies. They act specifically and most effectively upon the human spirit <term>spirit: in Renaissance medicine, the 'spiritus' was a subtle vapor that linked the physical body to the immaterial soul</term>, which is itself very similar to celestial rays. 

Furthermore, they act upon even the hardest of bodies, for all such things are very weak when compared to the heavens. There are, however, various powers in various stars, and for that reason, their rays differ from one another. Moreover, depending on how the strikes of these rays fall in various and different ways, diverse virtues are born...

...ted. Finally, in the mutual intersections of celestial rays, occurring in one way or another—now here, now there, at one time or another—diverse powers suddenly arise. This happens much more intensely and quickly than in the various mixtures of elements and elemental qualities. It occurs even more quickly than in the tones and musical ratios <note>original: "numeris," referring to the mathematical proportions that define musical intervals</note> of a musician sounding together in various ways. If you consider these things carefully, perhaps you will not doubt someone saying that, through a certain sudden casting of rays, powers are imprinted upon images—and different powers from different castings.

Can it really happen so fast? I will set aside those **fascinations** <term>fascinations: the traditional belief that the gaze can transmit a physical or spiritual influence, often used to explain "the evil eye" or the sudden onset of love.</term> created by a sudden glance, and the most intense loves ignited instantly by rays from the eyes—which are themselves a kind of fascination, as we prove in our book *On Love*. I omit how quickly a bloodshot eye might infect one who looks at it, or how a menstruating woman looking into a mirror might stain it <note>A common belief in natural philosophy of this era, cited by Aristotle, suggesting that the "spirit" of the eyes could physically affect objects.</note>. Is it not said that certain families among the Illyrians and Triballians could kill men with an angry glance? And that certain women in Scythia were accustomed to doing the same?

Even the **Catoblepas** <term>Catoblepas: a legendary Ethiopian creature described by Pliny the Elder as having a heavy head and a gaze so deadly it could kill instantly.</term> and the kingly serpents <note>original: "reguli," often used for the Basilisk or "little king" of snakes.</note> destroy men by the casting of rays from their eyes. The **electric ray** <term>torpedo: a species of ray known in the Mediterranean for its ability to deliver an electric shock.</term> also suddenly numbs the hand by touch, even from a distance through a rod. Furthermore, the little **remora** fish <note>original: "echinnus" (hedgehog), but contextually referring to the *echeneis*, a small fish believed to have the magical power to halt a ship in its tracks.</note> is said to stop a massive ship simply by touching it. Moreover, the **tarantula spiders** <term>phalangia: venomous spiders of the Mediterranean, specifically associated here with "tarantism," where a bite was thought to cause madness or stupor.</term> in Apulia change the spirit and the mind with a sudden stupor by means of some strike, perhaps even an unseen one. What does a rabid dog do, even when no bite is apparent? What about a broom, or even the strawberry tree? Do they not stir up poison and madness with the lightest touch? Will you then deny that the celestial [rays] of the eyes...

...[by] their own rays, with which they simultaneously look upon and touch our worldly affairs, to immediately accomplish wonders? Such is the power and the speed of heaven upon prepared materials. Finally, if fire has this property—to do in the shortest possible time what other things take a long time to do, especially because it is most similar to the heavens—who then would doubt that heaven accomplishes great things almost in a moment? It does this even in material that is less prepared, just as a larger flame is prone to do. Why then, they ask, do you doubt that heaven acts in almost the same way when an <term>image</term><note>Here, "image" refers to an astrological talisman or amulet, specially crafted under specific planetary alignments to capture celestial power.</note> is being constructed?

...in which indeed celestial nature long ago naturally began a power for this very thing which is desired, and it is as if it were just now completing it: like the flame in sulfur. This power, indeed, it finally completes only when this matter is vehemently stirred by human art under a certain similar celestial influx, and, being stirred, it grows warm. Therefore, art awakens the power begun there; and while art reduces the matter to a form <note>original: "figuram," referring to the physical shape or astrological image carved into an object</note> similar to its own certain celestial form, it then exposes it entirely to its Idea <note>Ficino refers to the Platonic Ideas—the perfect archetypes in the divine mind that influence the physical world</note> there. Once it is thus exposed, the heavens complete it with that power with which they had begun. Just as sulfur is presented to a flame, so a certain power for snatching up straws was given from heaven to amber <note>original: "succino"</note>. This power is often somewhat weak, but when it is made stronger through friction and heating, it suddenly snatches them up.

**S**erapio <note>likely Serapion the Younger, a 12th-century author of a famous medicinal herbal and lapidary</note> writes that a similar power is given to the <term>Albugedi stone</term> <note>a stone mentioned in medieval lapidaries, often identified with varieties of jasper or turquoise</note>, which is similar to a hyacinth <note>not the flower, but a blue or yellow gemstone, often zircon or sapphire</note>. But it does not pull straws until hair is rubbed with this stone. So also that <term>Jovian Bezoar</term> <note>A "Bezoar" is a stone-like mass found in the stomachs of animals, highly prized as an antidote. "Jovian" means it is associated with the planet Jupiter</note> stone—that is, "liberating from death"—which we described in the book *Against the Plague*, received power against poison from Jupiter from the beginning. But that power was not so strong that it could hand over the same power to other materials to be used. But indeed, as soon as it has received the form of that celestial Scorpio <note>The zodiac sign Scorpio, which was thought to govern both poisons and their cures</note> under its influx, it is thought to suddenly bring back perfect power against scorpions. It is then able to communicate this power to mastic <note>a resin from the pistachio tree</note> or incense. The same reasoning must be held concerning the hyacinth, topaz, emerald, and others: that the crafting of forms <note>the act of carving specific images or "talismans" into gemstones</note> has efficacy from no other source...

[Large blue initial S] But lest you perhaps distrust figures too much, you will be commanded to remember that in this elemental region under the moon: an elemental quality can also do a great deal, namely in a transformation tending toward something elemental. But for celestial gifts (as they think), they are worth a great deal. For in heaven, lights, and numbers, and figures are almost the most powerful of all things. Especially if there is no matter there, which many Peripatetics <note>original: "Peripatetici"; followers of Aristotle's school of philosophy</note> believe. For in this way, figures, numbers, and rays, since they are not supported there by other matter, seem to be as if substantial <note>existing as the actual essence of the thing rather than just a surface quality</note>. [red mark] Therefore, both the species of natural things are bound to certain shapes, and their motions, generations, and changes are bound to certain numbers.

For these, through both their harmonic rays and their all-penetrating motions, affect the spirit daily in secret, just as powerful music is accustomed to affect us openly.

...the figure of a mourner. And how much the figure of a lovely person immediately affects and moves the eyes, the imagination, the <term>spirit: in Renaissance medicine, a subtle, vaporous substance that carries life-force from the soul to the body</term>, and the <term>humors: the four vital fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—that govern health and personality</term>.

¶ Nor is the celestial figure any less alive or effective. Does not the mild and joyful face of a prince in a city gladden everyone? While a fierce or sad one suddenly terrifies them? What, then, do you think the faces of the celestial bodies—who are the masters of all earthly things—can do in comparison to these?

Indeed, when parents come together to conceive offspring, they often tend to imprint "faces" upon the children born long after—not only faces like those they themselves have at that moment, but even those they are merely imagining.

¶ In the same way, the celestial "faces" immediately imbue earthly matter with their own characteristics. Even if these traits seem to lie hidden for a long time within that matter, they eventually emerge at their proper times.

¶ Now, the "faces" of the sky are the celestial figures. You might call the figures there "faces" <note>Latin: *facies*; here referring to the fixed astrological signs</note> when they are more stable than others. But you might call them "countenances" <note>Latin: *vultus*; implying a changing expression or look</note> when they change more frequently. You can similarly call the <term>aspects: the specific geometric angles, like 60 or 90 degrees, between planets</term>—which are created among the stars by their daily motion—both "countenances" and "figures." For they are named hexagons, pentagons, and squares.

¶ "So be it," someone might say. They will answer that they do not primarily argue that our man-made figures are powerful in their own right, but rather that they...

### What kind of celestial figures the ancients used to engrave on images. And on the use of images. Chapter 18.

**A**nyone, however, will ask which celestial figures they were most accustomed to engrave on images. For there are forms there that are quite conspicuous to the eyes, and described by many as they appear, such as Aries, Taurus, and similar figures of the zodiac, as well as those manifest outside the zodiac.

There are, furthermore, a great many forms there, not so much visible as imaginable, perceived—or at least devised—by the Indians, Egyptians, and Chaldeans through the **faces** <note>The "faces" (also known as decans) are 10-degree subdivisions of a zodiac sign, each associated with specific imagery used in astrological magic.</note> of the signs. For example, in the first face of Virgo, a beautiful maiden is sitting, holding two ears of grain in her hand and nursing a boy. And the rest are of the kind described by Albumasar <note>Abu Ma'shar (787–886 AD), a highly influential Persian astrologer whose work transmitted much Greek and Indian astrology to the West.</note> and several others.

Finally, there are certain <term>characters</term> <note>These are astrological symbols or "sigils"—abstracted geometric drawings believed to represent the essence or "spirit" of a planet or sign.</note> of the signs and planets designed by the Egyptians. They desire, therefore, for all these things to be carved into images. For instance, if someone expects a particular benefit from Mercury, they ought to place him in Virgo, or at least place the Moon there with an <term>aspect</term> <note>An "aspect" refers to the specific angular distance between two celestial bodies which determines how their influences blend.</note> of Mercury. And then they should complete the image out of tin or silver, on which the whole sign of Virgo is present, along with its character and the character of Mercury. And if the first face of Virgo...

<summary>This page introduces the specific astrological iconography used in talismanic magic, distinguishing between visible constellations, the 'faces' of the signs described by Albumasar, and Egyptian sigils.</summary>

...which you are about to use: you should also add the figure which we said was observed on the first side; and likewise for the others.

The most recent authors of [astrological] images have adopted a round shape for their overall form, in imitation of the heavens. The ancients, however—as we have read in a certain collection of the Arabs—preferred the figure of the cross above all others. This is because bodies act through their virtue <note>original: *virtutem*; in this context, it refers to the inherent occult power or influence of a celestial body</note> upon a surface that has already been spread out. Indeed, the primary surface is defined by a cross. For in this way, it possesses both length and width. This is the primary figure, the straightest of all, and it contains four right angles.

Furthermore, the effects of the celestial bodies result most powerfully through the straightness of their rays and angles. For the stars are greatly powerful when they occupy the four angles—or rather, the "hinges" <term>cardines: the four pivotal points of an astrological chart (Ascendant, Descendant, Midheaven, and Imum Coeli)</term>—of the heavens: namely the East, the West, and the two midpoints of the sky <note>The points directly above and directly below the observer</note>. When arranged in this manner, they cast their rays into one another in such a way...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the use of astrological images and signs, moving from the symbolism of the cross to the specific construction of planetary talismans used by ancient practitioners.</meta>

-># ON THE CELESTIAL LIFE<-

¶ But as for the excellence of the cross among the Egyptians before Christ, I think it was not so much a testimony of the gift of the stars as it was a prophecy <note>original: "praesagium"</note> of the power it was to receive from Christ. However, the astrologers who lived immediately after Christ—seeing the wonders performed by Christians through the cross, yet being ignorant or unwilling to attribute such great things to Jesus—attributed its power to the heavens. Although they ought to have considered that miracles are not performed by the cross itself without the name of Jesus. It is perhaps probable that the cross is suitable for [astrological] images because it represents the strength of all the planets and stars. Yet, it does not possess such immense power for this reason alone. It may indeed have some power, when joined with other necessary things, perhaps toward a favorable state of bodily health. But let us return to the opinions of others, as we began to relate them.

¶ For longevity, the ancients used to make an image of **Saturn** on a stone called <term>Feyrizech</term> <note>A transliteration of the Arabic "firuza," meaning turquoise.</note>—that is, on a sapphire—during the hour of Saturn, while Saturn was ascending and favorably positioned. However, this was done under the condition that Jupiter or Venus behold the midheaven <note>The highest point of the zodiac at a given time</note>, and that the noxious <term>malefic planets|Planets traditionally considered to have a negative influence, specifically Saturn and Mars</term> fall away and are in an unfortunate position.

¶ I heard from Mengo, a famous physician, that an image of this kind—made while Jupiter was joined there with the Sun—freed Giovanni Marliani, a mathematician of singular renown in our age, from the terror he used to suffer during thunder. <meta>This page continues a discussion on the efficacy of astrological talismans and whether their power resides in the carved images or the physical materials themselves.</meta>

...medicines rather than in figures <note>original: "figuris"; referring here to the specific shapes or symbols engraved onto astrological charms or talismans</note> they can have efficacy. For it is probable that if images possess any power, they do not acquire it so much through the figure recently added, as possess it naturally through the material being disposed in such a way. And if anything is acquired anew while the material is being engraved, it is obtained not so much through the shape as through the heating that comes from a certain striking <note>original: "contusione"; the friction or pounding of the engraver's tool</note>. 

This striking and heating, performed under a celestial harmony <note>original: "harmonia coelesti"; the belief that the movements of the planets create a mathematical or musical resonance that affects earthly objects</note> similar to the harmony that once infused the material with its power, excites and strengthens that very power—just as a breath fans a flame—and makes manifest what was previously hidden. It is just as the heat of a fire brings into view letters written with onion juice, which were previously hiding. Or like letters inscribed on a stone with goat's fat; though completely hidden, if the stone is submerged in vinegar, they emerge and stand out as if they were sculpted in relief. 

Indeed, just as the touch of a broom <note>original: "scopae"</note> or a strawberry tree <note>original: "arbuti"</note> rouses a dormant madness, so perhaps a certain pounding and heating alone brings forth the latent power in the material, provided it is done at the opportune time. It is certainly beneficial to use this celestial opportunity <note>Choosing a moment when the planets are favorably aligned</note> when preparing medicines; or, if one perhaps wishes to work with metals and stones, it is better simply to strike and heat them than to give them a specific shape. For besides the fact that I suspect such figures to be empty ghosts <note>original: "manes"; meaning vain or hollow things without true substance</note>, we must not rashly admit even the shadow of idolatry <note>The author, likely Marsilio Ficino, is being careful to stay within Christian orthodoxy by rejecting the "worship" of carved images</note>. 

Likewise, one should not rashly use stars—even beneficial ones—to expel diseases that are very similar to them, for they often increase such ailments. In the same way, harmful stars sometimes diminish diseases that are similar to themselves; a point which **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the influential 2nd-century astronomer and astrologer</note>...

<summary>The author argues that the power of astrological talismans comes from the physical act of "awakening" a material's natural properties through heat and timing, rather than the carved images themselves, which he rejects to avoid the charge of idolatry.</summary>

¶ ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS.
...and Haly <note>original: "Haly"; referring to Haly Abenragel, an 11th-century Arab astrologer whose work was central to Renaissance astrology.</note> clearly teaches.

¶ On constructing an image of the universe. ¶ Chapter 19.

**S**ed why indeed do we overlook the universal itself—that is, the image of the universe itself? From which, however, they <note>astrologers and practitioners of natural magic</note> seem to hope for a benefit from the universe. Therefore, perhaps a follower of theirs who is able will engrave a certain archetypal <term>archetype: a primary model or ideal form</term> form of the whole world, if he pleases, in bronze. Which he should then opportunely press into a gilded silver plate.

But when most especially should he press it? When the Sun reaches the first minute of Aries <note>The first minute of the sign of Aries marks the Vernal Equinox, the traditional beginning of the astrological year.</note>. For from this point, astrologers, as if from the revolution of its own birthday, predict the fortune of the world for at least that coming year. He, therefore, on this very birthday of the world, will engrave the figure of the whole world. 

But do you see how beautifully the argument for the world having been born at some time has come to our aid during the discussion? Since indeed it is reborn every year: Do not astrologers, in the very birth-chart <note>original: "genesis"; the calculation of a person's birth stars</note> of a human, first measure in which sign, which degree, and which minute the Sun was positioned? And there they lay the foundation of the whole figure. And in every year thereafter: as soon as the Sun enters that same minute, they consider the human as if reborn, and they predict the fortune of the year in the same way. 

Just as, therefore, they would not be able to do this for a human unless he were, in a sense, reborn—and he could not be reborn unless he had at some point been born—so too it is permitted to infer that the world was born at some time, specifically with the Sun positioned at the first minute of Aries: since indeed, through that same position every year, a certain fate of the world as if reborn revolves...

<summary>Marsilio Ficino explains how to create a powerful talisman representing the entire universe, arguing it should be made during the Sun's entry into Aries, which acts as the world's annual "birthday."</summary>

There are indeed three universal and simultaneously singular colors of the world: **green, gold, and sapphire-blue**, dedicated to the three Graces of heaven. Green indeed belongs to **Venus** as well as the **Moon**. Being moist, it is suited to moist things; it is proper to things being born, and accommodated to mothers. 

No one doubts that gold is the color of the **Sun**; and it is not foreign to **Jupiter** or **Venus**. Finally, we dedicate sapphire-blue most of all to **Jupiter**, to whom the sapphire itself is said to be consecrated. From which also **lapis lazuli**, endowed with this color, because of its Jovian virtue against the <term>black bile</term> <note>original: "bilem atram"; the substance thought to cause melancholy, which was ruled by Saturn. ->### ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS <note>original: "DE VITA CAELITVS"</note><-

...[The lapis lazuli] <note>The text likely refers to lapis lazuli, which was associated with the blue of the heavens and the "gold" of the stars.</note> has a special prerogative: it is born with gold and is distinguished by golden marks, thus it is a companion of gold, just as **Jupiter** is a companion to the **Sun**. The <term>Armenian stone</term><note>Lapis Armenius, or azurite, a blue carbonate of copper used as a pigment.</note> has a similar power, possessing a color that is in some way similar to green. They <note>referring to the Neoplatonic philosophers</note> will therefore judge it beneficial for obtaining the gifts of celestial graces to look very frequently at these three colors above all others. And in the **model of the world** <note>original: "formula mundi"</note> which you build, you should insert the sapphire-colored hue into the spheres of the world. They will think it worth the effort to add golden stars to the spheres in the very likeness of the heavens, and to clothe **Vesta** herself—or **Ceres**, that is, the **Earth**—in a green garment. A follower of these [philosophers] will either wear such a model himself, or he will look upon one placed before him.

It will truly be useful to look at a <term>sphere</term><note>An orrery or mechanical model of the solar system.</note> endowed with its own motions, such as **Archimedes** once made, and which recently a certain Florentine of ours named **Lorenzo** <note>Lorenzo della Volpaia (1446–1512), a famous clockmaker who built a complex planetary clock for the Medici.</note> has constructed. And one should not only look at it, but also reflect upon it in the mind. Accordingly, in the innermost chambers of his house, he should construct a room with a vaulted ceiling, marked with figures and colors of this kind, where he may primarily wake and sleep. And when he has gone out of the house, he will look not so much at the spectacles of individual things, but rather he will perceive the form and colors of the universe. 

But let those makers of images <note>meaning artists or craftsmen</note> see to these matters. You, however, shall fashion a more excellent image within yourself. For since you know that nothing is more orderly than **heaven**, nor can anything more temperate be imagined than **Jupiter**, you will finally hope to attain the benefits of heaven and Jupiter if you show yourself to be most orderly and most temperate in your thoughts, affections, actions, and your way of life. But since I have come to the mention of celestial temperance...

--- Page 224 ---
<meta>The text begins mid-sentence, likely continuing a discussion from the previous page regarding the nature of the heavens and the composition of celestial bodies.</meta>

...celestial things we have come upon, it would perhaps be opportune to remember that there is no excess of elemental quality in the heavens, to speak in the <term>Peripatetic</term> <note>original: "peripatetice"; referring to the philosophical school of Aristotle, who taught while walking in the Lyceum</note> manner. Otherwise, if it were so composed <note>i.e., if the heavens were made of a volatile mix of elements</note>, it would have been dissolved through so many ages; or even if it were a simple substance, it would have at least lost its other attributes by such great magnitude, power, and motion. 

But indeed, as something most moderate, it moderates all things and blends diverse things into one. Furthermore, both by this very temperance of its own and by the excellence of its form, it has divinely merited life. For we see composite things finally attain life only when a perfect mixing of qualities is seen to have already broken the prior opposition <note>the conflict between opposing qualities like heat and cold</note>, as is seen in plants. 

Next, we find a more perfect life in animals, insofar as there is in them a <term>complexion</term> <note>original: "complexio"; the physical and spiritual constitution or "temperament" of a living being</note> further removed from this opposition than in plants. In humans, again by the same reasoning, life is even more perfect and, in a certain way, already celestial. Indeed, the human complexion has already approached that celestial temperance, especially...

<summary>This page discusses the Aristotelian idea that the heavens are a perfectly balanced "middle ground" that enables life, establishing a hierarchy where humans are the most "celestial" of earthly beings due to their balanced physical constitution.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of celestial influence and the physiological effects of the planets, moving from the definition of qualities to the argument for a living, "vivifying" heaven.</meta>

[Red pilcrow symbol] ### ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS <note>original: "DE VITA CAELITVS." This is a chapter from Marsilio Ficino’s influential 1489 work "Three Books on Life," specifically the third book which deals with astral health.</note>

...rather, effective powers of tempered qualities; or if there are qualities there that are in some way similar, they possess a sort of airy temperament. And when we name certain things there "cold" and "dry," we accept this according to Platonic reasoning <note>Referring to the Platonic school of thought which views physical qualities as expressions of higher mathematical or spiritual ratios.</note>—that is, so that it may be called "cold" because it is the cause of the least heat. Indeed, "dry" is that which provides us with the least moisture. Thus the astrologer Abraham <note>Likely referring to Abraham Ibn Ezra, a 12th-century Jewish scholar whose astrological texts were widely translated into Latin and used by Renaissance philosophers.</note> says that Saturn leaves our body cold and dry in a certain way, because it brings less heat and moisture to our own. By the same reasoning, the meat of the ox and the hare are warm and moist in themselves, yet they are "cold" and "dry" to us <note>In Galenic medicine, foods were classified by their "complexion." While a living animal might be warm, the effect its meat has on human humors could be cooling or drying.</note>. From this induction, receive these two corollaries <term>corollary: a logical conclusion that follows naturally from a proven point</term>: first, that if bodies that are more perfectly tempered live more fully, then the heaven—being the most tempered—must live most fully. Or rather, conversely, it can be inferred from the fact that the heaven is most exactly tempered and possesses within itself the most absolute life: that to the extent that other things approach its temperament and life, they are allotted a more excellent life.

[Blue pilcrow symbol] The other [corollary] is that life is a form perfect in itself, bringing the body to perfection and providing the principle of motion. An internal principle, I say, of motion also acted from within, while also setting every part in motion from the outside. Therefore, if this very thing is life, consider him to be out of his mind who does not recognize that such a form exists within the heaven—the most excellent body, always circling in a perfect motion, giving life to all things, and doing so more to those things which, step by step, either to...

<summary>Ficino argues that the heavens are a living, "tempered" entity that provides life to the world, explaining that astrological qualities like "cold" or "dry" refer to the relative absence of heat or moisture rather than absolute states.</summary>
<keywords>Saturn, Abraham Ibn Ezra, temperament, celestial life, Platonic reasoning, principle of motion, astrology, Galenic medicine</keywords>

### ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS

...to admit, and to be able to waste away. However, I do not sufficiently understand whether images possess any power over distant objects. But I suspect they have some effect on the person wearing them: though I do not think it is of such a kind as most people imagine. I consider this effect to occur more by reason of the material rather than the shape; and thus (as I have said) I place medicines far above images by a wide margin.

Although the Arabs and Egyptians attribute so much to statues and images fashioned by astronomical and magical art, they believe the spirits of the stars are enclosed within them. Now, regarding these "spirits of the stars," some understand them to be the wonderful powers of the celestial bodies; others, however, take them to be <term>Daemons</term> <note>From the Greek 'daimon'; in Ficino's Neoplatonic view, these are not 'demons' in the modern evil sense, but intermediary spirits or attendants that bridge the gap between the divine stars and the earthly realm.</note> who are the attendants of this or that star. 

Therefore, whatever these spirits of the stars may be, they believe they can be inserted into statues and images, in much the same way as Daemons are accustomed to sometimes occupy human bodies: speaking through them, moving, being moved, and performing wonders. They believe the spirits of the stars perform similar things through images. 

They think that Daemons, who inhabit the worldly fire, insinuate themselves into our bodies through fiery <term>humors</term> <note>The bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—which were believed to determine health and temperament.</note>, or through ignited spirits and passions of that sort. Likewise, they believe the spirits of the stars can be inserted—through rays received at the opportune moment, through fumigations <note>The ritual burning of incense or herbs to attract specific planetary influences.</note>, lights, and powerful tones—into the appropriate materials of the images. They believe these can then work wonders upon the person wearing the image, or even someone standing nearby. These things we indeed believe can be done by daemons, though not so much through a specific material...

<summary>Ficino explores the Hermetic and Arabic belief that celestial spirits or 'daemons' can be trapped in physical talismans, though he remains skeptical, preferring the medicinal use of materials over their symbolic shapes.</summary>
