¶ The Arabs relate <note>Ficino frequently references Arabic astrological magic, particularly sources like the *Picatrix*, which provided instructions for capturing the power of the stars in physical objects.</note> that when we rightly fashion images <note>original: *imagines* — here referring to astrological talismans or icons</note>, our spirit—if it has been most attentive to the work and to the stars through both imagination and emotion—is joined with the spirit of the world itself and with the rays of the stars. It is through these rays that the spirit of the world acts. Thus joined, the human spirit itself acts as a channel, so that from the spirit of the world, by way of these rays, a certain "spirit" of a specific star—that is, an invisible power <note>original: *invisibilis virtus*; the OCR "inui, dia" is a likely corruption of this term</note>—is infused into the image, becoming immediately present to the spirit of the person performing the work. Such work is also aided by fumigations <term>suffumigations|the ritual burning of specific herbs, resins, or woods to attract planetary influences through scent and smoke</term> adapted to the stars. This is because such fumigations thoroughly affect the air, the rays, the spirit of the craftsman, and the very material of the image.

¶ However, I judge that odors—which are very similar in nature to the spirit and the air—are particularly effective under the rays of the stars when they are burned. If these odors are Solar or Jovial <note>associated with the Sun or the planet Jupiter, considered the most beneficial "planets" in Renaissance astrology</note>, they strongly influence the air and our own spirit toward the gifts of the Sun or Jupiter that are then ruling and which must be captured opportunely under their rays. The spirit, once affected and gifted in this way, can through a certain stronger emotion act not only upon its own body, but upon a nearby body—especially one that is naturally similar but weaker—and affect it with a matching quality. I believe, however, that the harder material of a physical image can hardly receive even the slightest influence from the odors and the imagination of the worker. Yet the spirit itself, from the odor...

...to be so affected that out of both a single thing is made. This is indeed clear from the fact that a scent no longer acts upon the sense of smell once it has acted sufficiently. For the sense of smell—or indeed any other sense—suffers nothing further from itself or from anything very similar to it. But concerning these things, we shall speak elsewhere. Therefore, I believe the intensity of the imagination has power not so much in the manufacturing of images or medicines, as it does in the applying and receiving of them. 

For instance, if someone carries an "image" <note>In this context, a "talisman" or astrological amulet</note> (as they say) that has been properly made, or certainly uses a medicine in a similar way, and strongly desires help from it, and believes and hopes most firmly without a doubt: from this, surely, a very great heap of assistance will be added. For when the power of the image (if any exists), having been warmed by contact, penetrates the flesh of the person touching it—or at least the natural power within its chosen material—or certainly when the vigor of a medicine taken internally flows into the veins and marrow, carrying a <term>Jovial</term> <note>relating to the planet Jupiter, associated in this period with health, benevolence, and "vital spirit"</note> property with it, the spirit of the man is transferred into that kind of Jovial spirit by means of affection—that is, by love. 

For the power of love is to transfer <note>The author refers to the Neoplatonic idea that love transforms the lover into the object of their affection</note>. Moreover, faith and undoubting hope place the man's spirit, already thus moved, deep within the Jovial spirit and strengthen it there. If, as Hippocrates and Galen <note>Ancient Greek authorities on medicine</note> teach, the love and faith of a sick person toward an "inferior" and external doctor contribute greatly to health—indeed, Avicenna <note>An influential Persian physician and philosopher</note> even says this confidence accomplishes more than the medicine itself—how much more must we think that affection and faith lead us toward heavenly help, when directed toward the celestial influence already implanted in us and acting within?

**W**ords, moreover, pronounced with a certain sharp emotion, are judged to have great power regarding images for directing their effect specifically toward that place where the emotions and the words are aimed. Since there are seven levels—corresponding to the number of the seven planets—through which an attraction <term>attraction: the occult process by which earthly objects "pull down" or resonate with the specific energies of a planet</term> is formed from higher things to lower ones, voices occupy the middle level and are dedicated to Apollo. 

Indeed, the lowest level is held by harder materials, such as stones and metals; these seem to relate to the Moon. The second place in the ascent is held by those things composed of herbs, the fruits of trees, gums, and parts of animals. These correspond to Mercury, if we follow the celestial order of the Chaldeans <note>Ancient Babylonian astronomers and astrologers</note>. The third level consists of very fine powders and their vapors chosen from the things mentioned before, as well as the scents of simple herbs and flowers, and ointments belonging to Venus. The fourth level consists of words, chants, and sounds; all of these are properly dedicated to Apollo, the author of the rules of music. The fifth consists of powerful...

concepts of the imagination—its forms, motions, and passions—referring back to the power of Mars. The sixth level involves the reasonings and deliberate discussions of human logic, which pertain to Jupiter. So that you may understand how from a certain composition of herbs and vapors, crafted through art—both medical and astronomical—a certain common form results, much like a harmony, endowed with the gifts of the stars. In the same way, from musical tones—first chosen according to the standard of the stars, and then composed among themselves for their mutual harmony—a common form is created; and within that form, a certain celestial power arises.

It is indeed very difficult to judge which tones best suit which stars, or which compositions of tones agree with which particular stars and their planetary aspects. But partly through our own diligence, and partly through a certain divine chance, we can achieve this—just as Andromachus <note>Andromachus the Elder (1st century CE), physician to Emperor Nero and inventor of the famous theriac antidote.</note>, who worked himself to exhaustion for a very long time composing the <term>theriac</term> <note>A complex medical "polypharmacy" or universal antidote consisting of dozens of ingredients, believed to be effective only if the proportions were perfect.</note>; and finally, after his diligence, he attained the power of the theriac through divine fortune.

Therefore, Phoebus <note>A name for Apollo, the god of both music/prophecy and healing.</note>, the prophet of medicine...

They propose three rules above all for this purpose. We shall present them, provided we first warn you not to think we are speaking at present about worshipping the stars; rather, we speak of imitating them and, through imitation, capturing their gifts. Nor should you believe we are dealing with gifts that the stars might give by their own choice <note>original: *electione*. This distinguishes "natural magic" from "demonic magic," where a spirit would consciously choose to help the practitioner.</note>; but rather through <term>natural influence</term> <note>original: *influxu naturali*. This is the "flow" of energy or spirit from the stars into the physical world.</note>. To this influence—which is indeed manifold and hidden—we strive to adapt ourselves in refined ways, just as we daily adapt ourselves to healthfully receive the visible light and heat of the Sun. Truly, to adapt oneself to its hidden and wonderful gifts is the duty of the wise man alone. 

But now let us come to the rules for adapting song to the stars. 

**The first rule** is to seek out what powers any star, constellation, or <term>aspect</term> <note>original: *aspectus*. The angular relationship between planets that determines their combined influence.</note> has within itself, or what effects they produce; what things they take away and what they bring. And one must insert these things into the meanings of our words: detesting what they take away and praising what they bring.

**The second rule** is to consider which star rules most over a specific place or person. Then, observe what tones and songs these regions and people commonly use. You should then apply similar tones, together with the meanings mentioned above, to the words you intend to offer to those same stars.

**The third rule** is to notice the daily positions and <term>aspects</term> of the stars, and under these conditions, explore what kinds of speech, songs, movements, dances, behaviors, and actions people are most prompted to <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence here: *incitari* (prompted/incited).</note>

Then, by a flowing outward, it subsequently moves what is nearby. It affects both its own body and that of others with a certain <term>stellar property</term> <note>A quality or influence derived from the stars and planets.</note>, which it has conceived both from its own form and from the chosen opportunity of a favorable time. For this reason, many Eastern and Southern peoples, especially the <term>Indians</term> <note>Ficino refers to the people of India, who were believed in the 15th century to possess ancient magical knowledge and a deep connection to the Sun.</note>, are said to possess a wonderful power in their words. This is also true of anyone in other regions who is particularly <term>Phoebian</term> <note>Related to Phoebus Apollo, the Greco-Roman god of the Sun, music, and healing.</note>. 

Song, however, when conceived with this power, timing, and intention, is almost nothing other than another spirit recently conceived within you, alongside your own spirit—a spirit made <term>Solar</term> <note>Relating to the Sun. Ficino believed the Sun was the "heart of the world" and that its energy could be captured through music.</note> and acting both upon you and upon your neighbor with Solar power. For if a certain vapor and spirit, sometimes emitted outward through the rays of the eyes or by other means, can fascinate, infect, or otherwise affect a neighbor, how much more powerful is the spirit that proceeds from the imagination and the heart...

...flowing much more abundantly and more fervently, and more powerful in motion. Thus it is not entirely surprising that certain diseases of the soul and body can sometimes be taken away or introduced in this way. This is especially true because a musical spirit of this kind properly touches and acts upon the *spirit* <note>In Renaissance philosophy, the "spiritus" was a vaporous substance that acted as a bridge between the physical body and the immaterial soul.</note> which is the medium between the body and the soul, affecting both with its own disposition first.

You will indeed grant that there is a marvelous power in the stirred and singing spirit if you concede to the Pythagoreans <note>Followers of Pythagoras, who believed the universe was structured according to mathematical and musical ratios.</note> and Platonists that the heavens are a spirit, arranging all things by their movements and tones. Remember, indeed, that all music proceeds from Apollo <note>The god of music, often identified with the Sun in this context.</note>. And to that extent Jupiter is musical, in so far as he is in harmony with Apollo. Moreover, Venus and Mercury derive their music from their proximity to Apollo. Likewise, musical harmonies <term>concentus (harmonies)</term> belong only to these four planets <note>The Sun/Apollo, Jupiter, Venus, and Mercury.</note>. The remaining three, however, have voices but not songs. 

Now, we attribute slow, deep, hoarse, and complaining voices to Saturn. But to Mars we attribute the opposite: fast, sharp, harsh, and threatening ones. To the Moon, we give the middle-range voices. As for harmonies, we assign those of Jupiter to be deep and earnest, sweet and steadily joyful. On the other hand, to Venus we ascribe voluptuous songs filled with playfulness and softness. Between these two extremes, we place the harmonies of the Sun and Mercury. If they are venerable, simple, and earnest, along with grace and sweetness, they are judged to be Apolline. If they are somewhat more relaxed along with their pleasantness, yet vigorous and complex, they are Mercurial...

Meanwhile, it becomes Solar <note>original: "Phoebeus", referring to Phoebus Apollo, the sun god</note>, since the power of Apollo himself, the leader of music, flourishes in every musical consonance. And in turn, by becoming Solar yourself through the song and sound of Apollo, you simultaneously claim for yourself the power of Jupiter, and of Venus, and of Mercury. Furthermore, from the spirit being thus affected within, you similarly affect the soul and the body. 

Remember, however, that a prayer <note>original: "orationem", which can mean a formal speech or a religious prayer</note> that is suitably and opportunely composed, full of emotion and meaning, and delivered with intensity, has a power similar to songs. ...others affirm of Achilles. For we are not speaking now of deities to be adored, but of a certain natural power of speech, song, and words. That there is indeed a Phoebian <note>original: "phębeā"; relating to Apollo/the Sun, the god of music and healing</note> and medicinal power in sound, and a specific power at that, is clear from this: those in Apulia <note>a region in Southern Italy</note> who have been bitten by the tarantula <note>original: "phalāngio"; the bite of this spider was thought to cause "tarantism," a state of melancholic stupor</note> are all struck senseless and lie half-dead until each hears a certain sound specific to himself. For then he dances to the sound appropriately, sweats from the exertion, and recovers. And if after ten years he hears a similar sound, he is suddenly moved to dance again. I conjecture from these signs that such a sound is Phoebian and Jovial <note>original: "Iouialē"; related to the planet Jupiter, associated with health and vital spirit</note>.

### How we can adapt ourselves to the celestial beings in seven ways; and for whom Saturn is harmful, and for whom favorable; whom Jupiter defends from Saturn; and how the heavens act upon the spirit, body, and soul. Chapter 22.

**S**ince the heavens are composed according to harmonic proportion, and move harmoniously, and produce all things through harmonic motions and sounds, it is rightly through harmony alone that not only humans, but all these lower things are prepared, according to their capacities, to receive celestial influences. We distribute this harmony, which is capable of receiving higher things, through seven levels among the higher things. Namely:
- Through images (as they believe) harmonically constructed; <margin>Through images</margin>
- Through medicines tempered with a certain consonance <note>meaning a balanced mixture of ingredients</note> of their own; <margin>through consonance</margin> <margin>medicines</margin>
- Through vapors and odors prepared with a similar elegance; <margin>through harmony</margin> <margin>habits</margin> <margin>exposition</margin> <margin>with</margin> <margin>harmony</margin> <margin>odors</margin>
- Through musical songs and sounds, to whose order and power we intend bodily gestures, dances, and rhythmic movements <term>tripuda: a rhythmic three-step dance, often used in ancient religious or military rituals</term> to be related. <margin>provide</margin> <margin>I said</margin> <margin>Ancient</margin> <margin>turned</margin> <margin>ratio</margin> <margin>proportion</margin> <margin>songs</margin> <margin>thus</margin> <margin>is</margin> <margin>above</margin> <margin>spirit</margin> <margin>which</margin> <margin>seven</margin> <margin>which</margin> <margin>stages</margin> <margin>nature</margin> <margin>stands</margin>

->### ON THE CELESTIAL LIFE<-

Through the concepts of the imagination and harmonious movements; through the appropriate discourses of reason; and through the tranquil contemplations of the mind.

¶ Just as we daily expose the body to the light and heat of the sun through its own harmony—that is, through posture, bearing, and form—so too we prepare the <term>spirit<note>In Renaissance medicine, the "spirit" (spiritus) was a fine, vaporous substance that acted as a bridge between the physical body and the non-physical soul.</note></term> for the hidden powers of the stars. This is achieved through a similar harmony of its own: by means of images (as they believe), and certainly by means of medicines and scents harmonically composed. And finally, through the spirit thus prepared for the powers above, as we have often said, we expose the soul and the body to those same things. I say the soul, insofar as it is inclined by affection toward the spirit, and the body. 

In the soul, we now identify the imagination, the reason, and the mind. Certainly, our imagination—either because of the quality and movement of the spirit, or through our own choice, or even through both—can be so disposed, composed, and shaped to Mars or the Sun <note>original: "Marti, Soli ve"</note> that it immediately becomes a fitting vessel for the influence of Phoebus <note>A poetic name for the Sun.</note> or Mars. Similarly, reason—either through the imagination and spirit together, or through deliberation, or both—can prepare itself for Jupiter through a certain imitation. Because of its own dignity and proximity, reason may receive Jupiter himself and the gifts of Jupiter much more effectively than the imagination or the spirit can. This is just as the imagination and spirit, for the same reason, receive celestial things much more than any lower physical objects or materials do.

Finally, the **contemplative mind**—insofar as it withdraws itself not only from those things we perceive through the senses, but even from those things we commonly imagine and the human ways in which we reason, and as it recalls itself through its affection, intention, and way of life toward separate, higher things—exposes itself in a certain way to **Saturn** <note>In the Renaissance, Saturn was the planet of "melancholy," believed to govern both deep depression and the highest forms of intellectual and divine contemplation.</note>. To this mind alone is Saturn favorable. 

For just as the **Sun** is an enemy to nocturnal animals but a friend to those active by day, so is Saturn opposed to those men who either lead a common life openly, or who, while indeed fleeing the customs of the crowd, do not yet let go of common desires. For he granted the common, social life to **Jupiter** <note>Jupiter was seen as the planet of "civic life," governing law, social order, and the active life of humans in society.</note>, but claimed for himself a separate and divine life.

# [blue paragraph mark] CONCERNING LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS.

Those dwelling among the clouds, living very soberly, content with the fruits of the earth, and dedicated to the study of secret wisdom and religion, taste the happiness of Saturn. They lead a life so prosperous and so long-lived that they are considered not so much mortal men as immortal <term>spirits</term> <note>original: "Demones". In the Renaissance sense, these are not evil "demons" but intermediary beings or "daemons" that bridge the gap between gods and men.</note>. Many call these "heroes," a golden race rejoicing in a certain Saturnian age and kingdom. This, I think, is perhaps what the <term>Arabic astrologers</term> <note>Medieval and Renaissance scholars heavily relied on Arabic texts for astronomical data and astrological theory.</note> intended when they say that beyond the equinoctial line <note>the Equator</note> toward the south, there are certain very subtle inhabitants—spirits—who seem neither to be born nor to die. They claim that there, Saturn and the <term>Tail of the Dragon</term> <note>The South Node of the moon; in traditional astrology, it is often associated with Saturn and has a restrictive or "malefic" influence.</note> hold power.

This indeed <term>Albumasar</term> <note>Abu Ma'shar (787–886 AD), one of the most influential Persian astrologers in the Latin West.</note> seems to confirm in his *Book of Sadar* <note>Likely the "Kitab al-Sahar," known in Latin as the "Great Introduction" (Introductorium Maius).</note>, saying that certain regions of India are subject to Saturn; there, men are very long-lived and pass away only in extreme old age. He assigns the reason for this: since Saturn does not harm those of his own household, but only those from the outside <note>In astrology, a planet is "at home" in its own zodiac signs (Capricorn and Aquarius) and is thought to act more benevolently toward things aligned with its nature there.</note>.

[red paragraph mark] But you, do not neglect the power of Saturn. For the Arabs claim he is the most powerful of all. Indeed, the planets take on the powers of those they approach; yet all planets approach him rather than the reverse <note>Because Saturn is the slowest-moving and outermost planet in the traditional geocentric model, other planets "catch up" to him in the zodiac.</note>. Any planets joined to him act according to his nature. For he himself is the head of the widest sphere among the planets. Truly, every planet is the head, the heart, and the eye of its own sphere. Likewise, Saturn is closest to the innumerable fixed stars; he is most like the <term>First Mover</term> <note>The "Primum Mobile," the outermost sphere of the universe that imparts motion to all the inner spheres.</note>; he completes a long circuit and is the highest of the planets. Whence...

-> ### ON LIFE FROM HEAVEN ### <-

...they devote themselves with their whole mind to the contemplation signified by Saturn itself. <note>original: "contemplationem ab ipso Saturno significatam." In Renaissance astrology, Saturn was the planet of both profound intellectual depth and misfortune. By engaging in high-level "Saturnian" thought, a person could align with the planet's positive side and avoid its "malignant" physical effects.</note> For the Chaldeans <note>Ancient Babylonian star-gazers and sages</note>, Egyptians, and Platonists believe that in this way the malice of fate can be avoided. For since they do not want the celestial bodies to be empty shells, but rather divinely animated and, moreover, guided by divine minds, they certainly hold that from there flow to humans not only many things pertaining to the physical body and the spirit <term>spirit: in this period, the "spiritus" was thought to be a subtle, airy substance that acted as a bridge between the physical body and the immortal soul</term>, but also many goods that in some way overflow into the soul. These flow not from the physical bodies of the planets into the human soul, but from the souls of the planets themselves. Indeed, they believe these things flow even more abundantly from those Minds that exist above the heavens.

If you wish to consider all the reasons why Moses commanded the rest of the Sabbath to the Hebrews, perhaps beyond a more sublime and secret allegory, you will find that the day of Saturn <note>Saturday</note> is unfit for civil or warlike actions, but well-suited for contemplations. On that day, divine protection should be sought against dangers. Abraham, Samuel, and many Hebrew astrologers testify that such protection against the threats of Mars and Saturn can indeed be obtained through the elevation of the mind toward God and through vows and sacrifices. They thereby confirm that Chaldean precept: *namely, if you raise an ardent mind toward a work of piety, you will also save your frail body.* <note>original: "si mentem ad pietatis opus ardentem erexeris: corpus quoque caducum seruabis." This is a quote from the "Chaldean Oracles," a text Ficino highly valued.</note> It is also worthy to consider the saying of Iamblichus <note>A 4th-century Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote extensively on rituals and the divine</note>: that the celestial and worldly divinities possess certain superior powers within themselves, and some inferior ones.

Through these influences, indeed, we are bound by the effects of fate; yet through those others, in turn, we are released from fate. They possess, as Orpheus says, keys for both opening and closing <note>original Latin: "ad aperiendum... et claudendum." Orpheus, the legendary poet and musician, was considered by Renaissance thinkers like Ficino to be a primary source of ancient theological "mysteries" regarding the soul's liberation.</note>. Therefore, the Divinity that is far superior to the world redeems us much more from fatal necessity. It is also most worthy of investigation—that Hebrew tradition regarding the slaughter of animals and the dispersing of our goods in sacrifice: the idea that evils threatening from the heavens are deflected away from ourselves and onto our possessions. Second, those same gifts first flow through their rays into the spirit naturally, or in whatever way the spirit is exposed to them. Third, the "goods" of the celestial souls partly leap into that same spirit through rays and then overflow from there into our souls; and partly they reach the souls of those men who are open to them, coming from the souls of the celestial bodies or from the angels. I say "open" to them not so much through a natural contract, but through the choice of free will or through love. In short, consider that those who imitate the beneficence, action, and order of the heavens through their vows, study, life, and character are more like the beings above. These men receive more abundant gifts from that source. However, those men who are artificially <note>The author suggests a deliberate or forced deviation from the natural, divine order.</note> unlike and discordant with the celestial arrangement are secretly miserable, and ultimately, they turn out to be openly unhappy.

->### ON THE CELESTIAL LIFE.<-

¶ To live and act prosperously: first, know your **natural talent**, your **star**, your **guiding spirit**, and a **place** suitable for them; dwell there. Follow your natural calling.

### Chapter XXIII.

**W**hoever is born of sound mind and in control of themselves is naturally ordained by the heavens for some honorable work and way of life. Therefore, whoever desires to have the heavens favor them should first undertake this work and this way of life, and pursue it diligently. For the heavens favor their own undertakings. 

You are made by nature for this specific thing above all others: that which you first do, speak, imagine, desire, dream of, and imitate from your tender years; that which you attempt most frequently, which you accomplish most easily, in which you make the greatest progress, and in which you delight above all else; that which you leave behind unwillingly. This is truly that for which the heavens and the ruler of the heavens brought you forth. To that extent, therefore, the heavens will favor your undertakings and breathe life into your path, insofar as you follow the signs of your progenitor. 

This is especially true if that Platonic teaching is correct—with which all of antiquity agrees—that for every person born, there is a certain <term>daemon<note>original: "Daemon". In Renaissance Neoplatonism, this is not an evil spirit, but a protective "genius" or guardian spirit assigned to a person at birth based on their astrological chart.</note></term> who is the guardian of their life, assigned to them by their own star. This spirit assists in the very duty to which the celestial powers assigned the person at birth. Whoever, therefore, examines their own natural talent through the evidence we have just mentioned, will find their natural work; and in finding it, they will simultaneously find their star and their <term>daemon</term>. Following the guidance of these, they will act prosperously and live happily. Otherwise... <note>The text breaks off with "Alioquin" (Otherwise), likely continuing on the next page.</note>

<summary>Ficino explains that human happiness depends on identifying one's natural vocation, which is determined by the stars and a personal guardian spirit (daemon). He provides a list of childhood inclinations—such as dreams and effortless skills—as clues to discovering this celestial calling.</summary>

...indeed, he will also experience adverse fortune and feel the heavens to be his enemy.

¶ There are, therefore, two kinds of unfortunate men above all others: one consists of those who, having professed nothing, do nothing. The other consists of those who undertake a profession alien to their natural talent <note>The Latin 'ingenio' refers to one's innate character or natural ability</note> and contrary to their guiding spirit <note>original: 'genio'. In this context, it refers to the personal genius or 'spirit' that guides an individual's life</note>. The former, indeed, grow numb with laziness, even while the heavens—which are always in motion—internally urge them toward action. The latter, while they act in ways alien to their celestial patrons, labor in vain because they are destitute of these superior guardians. 

The first point is confirmed by an ancient proverb: *The gods help those who act, but they are hostile to the lazy.* The second is confirmed by a similar proverb: *Do nothing against the will of Minerva* <note>original: 'Nihil agas invita Minerva'. This Roman idiom means one should not attempt a task that goes against their natural bent or talent</note>. For this reason, I believe Jupiter is beseeched in the Pythagorean verses <note>This refers to the 'Golden Verses of Pythagoras,' a collection of moral exhortations popular in the Renaissance</note> to either lift the human race from so many evils himself, or at least show us which Spirit <note>original: 'Demone'. Here used in the classical sense of a 'Daimon' or personal guardian spirit</note> we should employ as our guide. 

Accordingly, it would be a worthwhile effort to investigate for which region especially your star and your Spirit have designated you from the beginning to inhabit and cultivate. For in that place, they breathe upon you more favorably. That is the place where, as soon as you have arrived, your spirit is in some way refreshed, where your senses remain more vigorous, where your bodily health is stronger, where most people favor you, and where your prayers succeed. Try these things, therefore. Choose this region. Cultivate it happily. You will clearly be unhappy departing from here, unless you both intend to return and proceed to similar places. But meanwhile, exercise yourself in the customs frequent in this region, and move in certain circles like those of the celestial bodies. For by a circular motion...

->**ON OBTAINING LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS**<-

...because having been born in this manner, you will be preserved by things similar to you. Furthermore, as far as it pertains to where you live, you will find it useful to remember this: Just as the countryside supplies the necessary nourishments of life to the city, while the city consumes them, so too is life itself greatly increased by frequent <term>rusticating</term> <note>original: "rusticatione"; the practice of retreating to a rural villa to refresh the spirit</note>—provided boredom does not begin to take hold of you. However, life is worn away by urban leisure and business alike. 

Regarding both your place of residence and your profession, that teaching of the Eastern astrologers is by no means to be despised: namely, that by a change of name, profession, clothing, diet, or location, the celestial influx <note>The "influx" refers to the "spiritus" or influence flowing from the planets into the human body</note> upon us can be changed for either the better or the worse. The Platonists <note>Followers of Plato, like Ficino himself, who believed in a hierarchy of spiritual beings</note> judge that our <term>daemons</term> <note>In this context, "daemons" are not evil spirits, but intermediary guardian spirits or "geniuses" that guide an individual's destiny</note> are also either changed by these shifts, or that we relate to the same ones differently here than we do elsewhere. 

Furthermore, the astrologers agree with the Platonists that the guardian daemon of each person can be twofold: one is proper to one’s birth <note>determined by the horoscope</note>, while the other is proper to one’s profession. Whenever one’s profession agrees with one’s natural disposition, the same daemon belongs to both—or at least a very similar one is present with us. In such a case, our life will be more concordant with itself and more tranquil. But if the profession disagrees with one’s natural character, the daemon acquired through one's trade will be discordant with the natural spirit of one's birth; thus, life becomes laborious and full of anxiety.

For those wishing to find what kind of daemon presides over each person from the moment of their birth, Porphyry <note>A 3rd-century Neoplatonist philosopher</note> investigates a rule based on the planet that is the <term>lord of the birth</term> <note>The "Almuten" or the planet with the most significant astrological power in a birth chart</note>. Julius Firmicus <note>Julius Firmicus Maternus, a 4th-century Roman lawyer and astrologer</note> says the planet ruling the birth is either that one which has more [dignities] there...

...possesses dignities: or rather, according to a more established opinion, he considers that planet whose domicile the Moon is soon about to enter after the sign she already occupies at the moment of the person's birth. ¶ The ancients used to hope that their own **Daemon** had descended to them from one of the **cardinal points** <term>Cardinal points: The four 'corners' of the sky: the East (Ascendant), West (Descendant), Midheaven (MC), and Lower Midheaven (IC).</term> of the heavens: namely from the East, the West, or from the Midheaven on either side. Or, at the very least, from the eleventh or fifth **region** <note>These 'regions' correspond to what modern astrologers call the Twelve Houses.</note>. The eleventh region follows the Midheaven directly above our heads; it is nicknamed the **Good Daemon**, and it views the ascending degree in the east through a **sextile** <note>An auspicious astrological aspect where two points are 60 degrees (two signs) apart.</note> aspect. The fifth region follows the Midheaven of the **antipodes** <note>The 'Bottom of the Sky' (Imum Coeli), the point directly opposite the Midheaven, associated with the feet or the other side of the world.</note>; it is called **Good Fortune**, and it gazes at the ascending degree through a **trine** <note>A highly favorable astrological aspect where two points are 120 degrees (four signs) apart.</note> aspect. In the third place, they hoped that if the **Daemon** must come from a **cadent** <term>Cadent: Regions of the sky that 'fall away' from the cardinal points, traditionally considered weaker or less active.</term> region, it would at least come from the ninth or the third. For the ninth is called **God**; but the third...

# ON THE CELESTIAL LIFE.

->They say that Apollonius of Tyana <note>original: "Ioniū theaneū" (Apollonium Tyaneum). Apollonius was a famous 1st-century Neopythagorean philosopher and reputed miracle-worker.</note> discovered an old man in Ephesus; under whose form a Demon was hiding, who by his presence alone was contaminating the entire city with plague. # By what means scholars may recognize their own genius, and follow a way of life agreeable to their spirit. <note>The "spirit" (*spiritus*) in this context refers to a subtle substance in the body that links the soul and the physical flesh.</note> **Chapter XXIV.**

[Large initial Q] Since I speak to those devoted to literature, I want each one who is captivated by the love of letters to remember that he is, first and foremost, **Mercurial**; furthermore, he is **Solar**, insofar as Mercury itself is Solar <note>In Renaissance astrology, Mercury was often seen as being closely associated with the Sun's power, as it is the planet closest to it.</note>. And this is the common condition of all these people. Specifically, however, beyond the Mercurial nature, whoever prevails by grace of eloquence, charm, dignity, and beauty should recognize **Apollo** and **Venus** within themselves. He who is more inclined toward laws or natural and common philosophy should not be ignorant that he has **Jupiter** as his patron. But he who is urged on more curiously to investigate even the most secret things should know that he is not only Mercurial, but **Saturnine**. Under Saturn's leadership also are all those who are diligent in any study right to the end, especially those who are negligent in other matters. Finally, if it is true what some—both physicians and astronomers—hand down, that the soul endowed with intellect descends into the human conception in the month of the Sun (that is, the fourth month), then those who live mostly by the intellect are from the beginning especially Solar, and become more so every day. Of these planets, therefore...

q.iiii.

### Astronomical diligence in procreating children, in preparing banquets, in buildings and habitation, and in clothing: and how much it is permitted to care for such things.
### Chapter XXV.

**S**o, it is helpful also to speak for a little while with the severe overseer <note>original: "antistitem", a high-ranking cleric or religious authority</note> of religion. Tell me then, Overseer, what indeed do you condemn in the use of the stars? Whatever, you will say, detracts from our free will; whatever derogates from the worship of the one God. These same things I not only condemn with you, but I also strongly detest. 

Indeed, I know you curse—and I, moreover, shudder at—certain people who, when they wish to entreat God, flee as wretchedly as they do foolishly toward Jupiter entering that vast "head of the dragon" <term>head of the dragon: the "Caput Draconis" or North Node of the Moon, considered a point of great power and increase in astrology</term> in the middle of the sky; they are destined, evidently, to be devoured by that very dragon who once fell from heaven, dragging a third part of the stars with him <note>The author is referencing the biblical fall of Lucifer from Revelation 12:4, using astrological terminology to mock those who prioritize planetary alignments over divine grace</note>. 

But will you concede that one may choose "opportune hours" for performing contracts, marriages, conversations, journeys, and similar works? I see you will not easily assent to these things; I know not what you fear for free will. Therefore I—even if that great theologian Albert <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a renowned scholar who argued that natural astrology was compatible with Christian faith</note> will grant those things, and a certain reason will perhaps dictate that through the very **election** <term>election: "electio" is the astrological practice of picking the most favorable moment to begin a specific task</term> pertaining to our choice, celestial things are subjected to the use of our prudence just as herbs serve the physician—nevertheless, for the present, I will believe you instead. And as you now permit these things with difficulty, I myself shall just as easily let them go. The observations...

It is like a contagion from an epidemic lurking in a garment, which long afterward infects and destroys an unwary person who wears it.

Farewell.

## Chapter XXVI.

**B**ut lest we wander too far from that which we established at the start while interpreting Plotinus <note>Plotinus (c. 204–270 AD) was the founder of Neoplatonism. The author, Marsilio Ficino, is here summarizing his commentary on Plotinus’s ideas about how the soul animates the body.</note>, summarize it briefly in this way. The world was created by the "Good" itself—as Plato teaches along with Timaeus the Pythagorean—to be as perfect as it could possibly be. It is, therefore, not only corporeal <note>Meaning made of physical matter.</note>; it is moreover a participant in life and intelligence. 

For this reason, besides this body of the world familiar to our senses, there lies hidden within it a certain "spirit"—a body of sorts that exceeds the capacity of our fleeting senses. In the spirit, the soul flourishes; in the soul, intelligence shines. And just as in the region beneath the Moon <note>The "sublunary" realm, which in ancient cosmology was the earth-centered sphere where all things are subject to change and decay.</note> air does not mix with earth except through the mediation of water, nor fire with water except through air; so in the universe there is a certain <term>bait (Latin: *esca*)</term> or <term>kindling (Latin: *fomes*)</term> for joining the soul to the body: it is that very thing which we call the spirit. The soul also acts as a kind of kindling within the spirit and the body of the world for the purpose of receiving intelligence from the divine.

This is much like how extreme dryness in wood prepares it to be penetrated by oil. The oil soaked into this wood then becomes fuel for fire—or more precisely, for heat. Heat itself is the vehicle for light. And if this wood were of such a kind that it would shine in the presence of fire without being consumed—as we have occasionally seen—we shall now see by this example: whether a human being, or some other thing beneath the Moon, can through certain preparations (partly natural and partly sought through art)...

<summary>This page explains the Neoplatonic "chain of being," describing the spirit (spiritus) as the essential intermediary or "kindling" that allows the divine soul to connect with the physical body. Ficino uses the analogy of oiled wood and fire to illustrate how material things can be prepared to receive celestial light and intelligence.</summary>

<keywords>Neoplatonism, Spiritus, Plotinus, Plato, Timaeus, soul and body, elemental theory, celestial influence, Marsilio Ficino</keywords>

...such things: and also perhaps it may be able to receive certain intellectual goods <note>original: "intellectualia quaedam bona"</note> opportunely, in a sense, from above. 

But what pertains here to religion we shall discuss elsewhere, where Plotinus <note>Plotinus (c. 204–270 AD) was the founder of Neoplatonism, whose works were translated and commented upon by Renaissance scholars.</note> brings these matters into consideration. But as for what pertains to natural influences, of whatever kind they may be coming from above: know that they can finally be obtained in us and in our materials through <term>art|The Latin 'ars' here refers to a specialized technique or 'natural magic'—the skill of aligning earthly materials with heavenly timing.</term> only when nature has supplied the fuel for them to us and our things, and the heavens have conspired more opportunely toward the same. 

Is it not true that in the fetus itself, nature is the artisan of the fetus? When it has affected and shaped the little body in a certain way, by this very preparation—as if with a kind of bait—it draws down spirit from the universe. And through this, as through kindling, it draws in life and soul; and finally, through a certain form and disposition of the soul, the body living in this way is at last worthy of the presence of the mind, granted divinely. 

¶ Therefore, nature is a magician everywhere <note>original: "natura maga est"</note>, as Plotinus and Synesius <note>Synesius of Cyrene (c. 373–414 AD), a philosopher-bishop who wrote on dreams and providence.</note> say, clearly enticing certain things everywhere with certain baits: not otherwise than drawing heavy things to the center of the earth, light things to the concavity of the moon, leaves by heat, roots by moisture, and other things similarly. By this very attraction, the wise Indians <note>A common Renaissance reference to the "Gymnosophists" or ancient Eastern sages believed to possess primordial wisdom.</note> testify that the world is bound to itself, saying that the world is a living being <note>The 'Anima Mundi' or World Soul concept, where the universe is viewed as a single, living organism.</note> that is everywhere male and female at once, and through the mutual love of its members, it joins with itself everywhere, and thus it subsists.

<meta>Continuing from the previous discussion on how physical objects like mirrors and walls can catch and reflect immaterial qualities like light and sound.</meta>

->[Red paragraph mark] **ON LIFE FROM THE HEAVENS.**<-

...the glass before your face, or the wall opposite your voice, suddenly undergoes an effect from above—that is, from a most powerful agent, from a marvelous power and life present everywhere—and it carries back a certain virtue <note>virtue: here meaning a specific power or characteristic quality</note> through this experience. In no different a way, the mirror represents an image from the face, and the wall represents an Echo from the voice. Plotinus <note>a major Greek philosopher (c. 204–270 AD) and the founder of Neoplatonism</note> himself uses these very examples where, imitating Hermes <note>original: "Mercurium," referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Egyptian sage</note>, he says: the ancient priests or magi were accustomed to receive something divine and wonderful into statues and through sensible sacrifices.

However, he wishes, along with Trismegistus, that through these material things they do not receive deities that are entirely segregated from matter, but only "worldly" <term>mundana: celestial influences or spirits that are tied to the physical structure of the cosmos</term> ones, as I said from the beginning and as Synesius <note>a 4th-century philosopher and bishop who wrote on dreams and alchemy</note> approves. I say "worldly"—that is, a certain life or something vital from the Soul of the World <term>Anima Mundi: the spiritual essence that Neoplatonists believed animated the entire physical universe</term> and from the souls of the spheres and stars, or even a certain motion and a vital presence, as it were, from daemons <note>original: "Daemonibus"; in the classical sense, these are intermediary spirits between gods and humans, not necessarily evil "demons"</note>. 

Indeed, Hermes himself—whom Plotinus follows—says that sometimes these daemons are present in such materials; he calls them "daemons of the air," not celestial ones, let alone more sublime spirits. Hermes himself composes statues out of herbs, trees, stones, and spices that possess within themselves (as he says) a natural power of divinity. He adds songs similar to those of the heavens, in which he says these spirits take delight, and thus they remain longer in the statues and either benefit men or harm them. He adds that the ancient Egyptian sages, who were also priests, because they could not persuade the people through reason that there are Gods—that is, certain spirits... 
                                                                      r.i.

<summary>This page discusses the Neoplatonic theory of theurgy, explaining how ancient priests used "worldly" materials and celestial music to attract spiritual influences into physical statues. It distinguishes between transcendent deities and the cosmic spirits (daemons) that reside within the air and the stars.</summary>
<keywords>Theurgy, Hermes Trismegistus, Plotinus, Anima Mundi, Astral Magic, Daemons, Ancient Egypt, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 262 ---
...[that they] devised this magical enticement above other men, by which, drawing in demons, they declared them to be deities within statues. But Iamblichus <note>Iamblichus (c. 245–325 AD) was a Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote extensively on theurgy and Egyptian mysteries.</note> condemns the Egyptians because they did not only accept demons as certain steps for investigating the higher gods, but they worshipped them excessively. He prefers the Chaldeans <note>Ancient people of Mesopotamia, often associated in early modern texts with the origins of astrology and pure magic.</note> to the Egyptians, as they were not occupied with demons. He calls the Chaldeans the high priests of religion. 

For we suspect that the astrologers, both Chaldean and Egyptian, attempted in some way to draw demons into clay statues through celestial harmony. This seems to be indicated by the astrologer Samuel the Hebrew, relying on the authority of David Bil <note>possibly David Bel, a figure mentioned in medieval astrological and talismanic texts</note> the astrologer. Namely, that the ancient makers of images made statues that predicted the future. 

He records that the harmony of the heavens was adapted to these statues: to cast metal into the form of a beautiful man on the day of Mercury <note>Wednesday</note> at the third hour, namely the hour of Saturn. This is done when Mercury enters Saturn in Aquarius, in the ninth region of the sky, which signifies prophecy. And the constellation of Gemini rises, signifying prophets (as they say). Mars is combust <term>combust: an astrological term meaning a planet is positioned so close to the Sun that its influence is overwhelmed or hidden</term> by the Sun, nor does it "look" at Mercury. The Sun, however, regards the place of that conjunction. Meanwhile, Venus occupies a certain angle: she is in the west and powerful. The Moon regards the rising degree from a trine <term>trine: an aspect where two celestial bodies are 120 degrees apart, usually considered a harmonious or beneficial alignment</term>; likewise Saturn. 

These are their views. But I, for my part, think first according to the opinion of Saint Thomas <note>Saint Thomas Aquinas, who argued that any "speech" from statues was the work of demons rather than celestial forces.</note>, if indeed the matter of speaking statues...

<meta>This page continues a discussion on whether the power of animated statues comes from the stars or from indwelling spirits. The previous page likely introduced the concept of "wise men" or "ancients" who constructed these statues.</meta>

->### ON THE CELESTIAL LIFE<-

...[those wise men] brought it about that it was not simply the influence of the stars themselves that formed the words there, but demons. <note>original: "daemonas." In Ficino's Neoplatonic context, these are "daemons"—spirits that act as intermediaries between the divine and the human, which could be either good or deceptive, rather than strictly "demons" in the modern sense.</note> Furthermore, if it should happen that they enter such statues, I do not believe they were bound there by celestial influence; rather, they obeyed their worshipers of their own accord—and ultimately, they did so to deceive them. For a superior nature may indeed sometimes be won over by an inferior one, but it cannot be restrained. Moreover, that specific arrangement of the stars described a little earlier <note>This refers to Ficino's previous chapters on how to time the creation of talismans based on the positions of the planets.</note> might not even coincide with their arrival.

Even though demons are not confined to statues by astronomical logic, Porphyry <note>A 3rd-century scholar and Neoplatonic philosopher.</note> says that when they became present through the worship offered to them, they delivered oracles according to astronomical rules—and for that reason, they were often ambiguous. This is for good reason, since Iamblichus <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher who defended the use of "theurgy," or ritual acts, to communicate with the divine.</note> proves that true and certain prophecy does not belong to evil spirits, nor to human arts or nature, but comes from divine inspiration to purified minds.

But let us now return to Hermes <note>original: "Mercurium," referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Egyptian sage thought to be the father of alchemy and magic.</note>—or rather, let us return to Plotinus <note>The 3rd-century founder of Neoplatonism.</note>. Hermes says that the priests received a power from the nature of the world that was suited to the task, and they mixed it [into their statues]. Following him, Plotinus thinks that all of this can be accomplished through the mediation of the World Soul <note><term>World Soul</term> (Anima Mundi): The concept that the entire universe possesses a soul that connects the spiritual "Ideas" of God to the physical matter of the world.</note>. This is possible insofar as the Soul generates and moves the forms of natural things through certain <term>seminal reasons</term> <note>original: "seminales rationes"; these are the "form-giving seeds" or blueprints within the World Soul that dictate how physical things grow and take shape.</note> divinely implanted in her. He even calls these principles "Gods," since they are never separated from the Ideas of the Supreme Mind. Thus, through such principles, the World Soul easily applies herself to the materials she has formed from the beginning through...

...the same: when a <term>mage</term><note>original: "magus"; a practitioner of natural magic who understands the hidden properties of nature</note> or priest, at the appropriate times, has employed the forms of things properly gathered which specifically relate to this or that <term>reason</term><note>original: "rationem"; referring to the "seminal reasons," the metaphysical blueprints or powers embedded in nature</note>: just as the magnet attracts iron, rhubarb draws out bile, saffron aids the heart, agrimony and <term>spodium</term><note>a medicinal powder, often made from burnt ivory or metallic oxides</note> the liver, and spikenard and musk the brain.

<meta>This page continues the defense of the author's work—likely Marsilio Ficino’s *Three Books on Life*—against critics who believe a priest should not concern himself with secular sciences like medicine or astrology.</meta>

...what then has a priest to do with medicine? What, again, has he to do with the business of astrology? Another says the same: what has a Christian to do with magic or images? And another, indeed unworthy of life itself, will begrudge life to the heavens. Finally, all those affected by such views will be far too ungrateful for the benefit we offer them. And against our charity—through which we consult for the public life and prosperity according to the best of our talent—they will not be ashamed to be cruel.

Therefore, this labor shall be shared among you. But so that it might be somewhat lighter, since there are three of you, you shall undergo a divided struggle against these three enemies. Nor will you refute invective with more invective—for I know your character—but you shall overcome the bitterness of another's gall with your wonderful sweetness, the sweetness of your own honey.

<note>A red paragraph mark appears here in the original text, signaling a shift in the argument.</note> To begin, most sincere Nero <note>Likely Francesco Neroni, a friend and patron of Ficino</note>, respond to the first critics: that in ancient times, the most venerable priests were also physicians, as well as astronomers. Truly, the histories of the Chaldeans, Persians, and Egyptians testify to this. Furthermore, the duties of singular charity belong to no one more than to a pious priest; these duties shine brightest when performing the greatest benefit of all. Truly, the most excellent duty is undoubtedly that which is also the most necessary and desired by everyone: namely, to ensure that people have a **healthy mind in a healthy body**. <note>original: "mens sana in corpore sano," a famous phrase from the Roman poet Juvenal</note> However, we can only provide this if we join medicine to the priesthood.

<meta>This page continues Marsilio Ficino’s "Apologia" (Apology), specifically his defense of combining medicine and astrology within the duties of a priest. This section justifies why a priest might use "celestial" medicine, drawing parallels between human healing and the natural instincts of animals.</meta>

->APOLOGY.<-

But since medicine without celestial favor <note>original: "favore caelesti." In Ficino’s Neoplatonic philosophy, this refers to the astrological influences or "emanations" from the stars and planets that give earthly things their medicinal power.</note>—which both Hippocrates and Galen <note>The two most famous physicians of antiquity; Galen notably argued that a physician must also be an astronomer.</note> admit, and we ourselves have experienced—is more often useless, and often even harmful, then certainly astronomy belongs to that same priestly charity to which we have said medicine belongs. In this manner (as I believe), Holy Scripture <note>original: "sacrae literae." Ficino is referencing the Book of Sirach (Ecclesiasticus) 38:1: "Honor the physician for the need thou hast of him: for the Most High hath created him."</note> commands that the physician be honored, since the Most High created him because of necessity. And Christ himself, the giver of life, who commanded his disciples to heal the sick throughout the whole world, will also command priests that, if they cannot heal by words as those disciples once did, they should at least heal with herbs and stones. And if these things are not sufficient on their own, he will command them to combine these materials with the opportune influence <note>original: "afflatu," literally a "breathing upon" or inspiration from the heavens.</note> of the heavens and apply them to the sick. For he himself, through that same celestial influence, incites animals everywhere to find their own medicines; to such an extent does he provide most abundantly for the life of all.

Thus, by a divinely prompted celestial instinct, snakes heal their eyes with fennel, <note>A common ancient belief, recorded by Pliny the Elder, that snakes ate or rubbed against fennel to clear their vision after shedding their skins.</note> and swallows heal theirs with celandine. Eagles, troubled by the pains of birth, have divinely discovered the <term>aetites: also known as the "eagle-stone," a hollow geode with a loose stone inside that rattles; ancient lore claimed eagles placed it in their nests to protect their eggs and aid in delivery.</term> by which they might immediately and successfully lay their eggs. Therefore, God himself, who through the heavens incites all animals to their medicines, certainly permits priests—not for profit, I say, but out of charity—to drive out diseases with medicines strengthened by the heavens. To these things, you will then add more, if it is useful, with the sharpness of your own intellect.

->r.iiii.<- <note>A signature mark used by early printers to help binders assemble the pages of the book in the correct order.</note>

<summary>Ficino argues that priests should use medicine and astronomy to heal, just as God provides animals with natural instincts and "celestial" remedies like herbs and stones to preserve their lives.</summary>
<keywords>astrological medicine, priestly charity, Hippocrates, Galen, eagle-stone, medicinal herbs, Neoplatonism, animal instinct</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a defense of Marsilio Ficino’s work on Neoplatonic philosophy and "natural magic," likely following a discussion of his commentaries on Plotinus.</meta>

Arise now after these things, you too, energetic Guicciardini <note>Likely referring to a member of the prominent Florentine Guicciardini family, perhaps an associate of Ficino</note>: and respond to those overly curious minds that Magic or images are not indeed approved by Marsilio <note>Marsilio Ficino (1433–1499), the priest and scholar who led the Platonic Academy in Florence</note>, but are merely recounted by him while he interprets Plotinus <note>A 3rd-century philosopher whose work "The Enneads" formed the basis of Neoplatonism</note> himself. This his writings clearly declare, if they are read with an unbiased mind. Nor is there a single word asserted here concerning profane magic, which relies on the worship of demons; rather, mention is made of natural magic, which seeks to capture the benefits of the heavens through natural things for the thriving health of our bodies. 

This faculty surely seems as permissible to those using their talents lawfully as medicine and agriculture are rightfully permitted. And it is even more so, insofar as this industry is more perfect, coupling celestial things with earthly ones. From this workshop, the Magi <note>The "Wise Men" from the East mentioned in the Gospel of Matthew</note> were the very first of all to adore Christ immediately after His birth. Why then do you, fearful one, dread the name of "Magus"? It is a name favored by the Gospel. It does not signify an evildoer or a poisoner <note>original: "maleficum & veneficum," terms often used in legal codes to describe harmful witchcraft</note>, but rather sounds of the wise man and the priest.

What indeed does that Magus, the first worshiper of Christ, profess? If you wish to hear, he is like a certain kind of farmer: surely, he is a <term>cultivator of the world</term> <note>original: "mundicola," a word-play on "agricola" (field-cultivator/farmer)</note>. Nor does he adore the world because of this, any more than a farmer adores the soil. But just as the farmer tempers the field to the air for the sake of human sustenance, so that wise man, that priest, for the sake of human health, tempers the lower things of the world to the higher ones; and just as one does with hen’s eggs, he opportunely places earthly things beneath the heavens to be nurtured. Which—

It frames the biblical Magi as the primary examples of these "wise men" who use the natural properties of the universe to promote health and honor the divine.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's defense of his work, shifting from a general justification to a specific classification of magic and its relationship to divine order and medicine.</meta>

->### APOLOGY<-

God himself always brings this about: and by doing so, he teaches and persuades us to act, so that the lowest things are generated, moved, and governed by the higher ones <note>The author is describing the "Great Chain of Being," where celestial bodies and divine will influence the physical world below.</note>. Finally, there are two kinds of magic. One indeed is of those who, through a certain specific worship, win over Demons to themselves: relying on their work, they often manufacture portents. This, however, was utterly driven out <note>original: "explosum," a term originally meaning to hiss an actor off the stage.</note> when the prince of this world <note>A reference to the Devil; see John 12:31.</note> was cast out. But the other kind is of those who opportunely subject natural materials to natural causes, to be formed by a certain wonderful method.

¶ There are also two types of this craft. One is indeed curious <term>curiosa: here meaning "vain" or "excessive," referring to magic done for mere spectacle rather than utility</term>; the other is necessary. The former surely fashions superfluous wonders for display: as when the Persian magi, from sage rotted under dung while the Sun and Moon occupied the second face of Leo <note>The "second face" refers to a decan, a ten-degree segment of a zodiac sign.</note> and held the same degree there, generated a bird similar to a blackbird with a serpent's tail; and having reduced it to ash, they poured it into a lamp. Whence the house was immediately seen to be full of snakes. This, however, as something vain and harmful to health, must be avoided at all costs. Yet the necessary type must be maintained, which joins medicine with astrology. But if anyone stubbornly insists further, deal with him in this way, Guicciardini <note>Francesco Guicciardini (1483–1540), a famous Italian statesman and historian, and a friend of the author.</note>: see that he does not read these works of ours, nor understand them, nor remember them, nor use them—if he is even a man, being utterly unworthy of so great a benefit. There are many other things besides that you, with your talent, will be able to bring forward against ungrateful ignorance.

¶ What then are you doing and

<meta>This page continues a philosophical or theological argument, likely addressed to Piero Soderini, concerning the "World Soul" (Anima Mundi). It bridges the gap between classical Greek poetry and Christian scripture.</meta>

And you, our vigorous Soderini? <note>Likely Piero Soderini (1450–1522), the statesman who led the Florentine Republic and was a patron of scholars.</note> Will you tolerate those superstitious and blind people—whoever they may be in the future—who see life clearly in animals (even the lowliest ones) and in the most common herbs, yet do not see it in the heavens or in the world itself?

Indeed, if those "little men" grant life to the smallest particles of the world, what madness is this? What envy? To neither know nor wish to admit that the whole in which <note>original: "in quo uiuimus, & mouemur & sumus"</note> **we live, and move, and have our being** is itself alive. This is indeed what Aratus <note>Aratus of Soli (c. 315–240 BCE), a Greek poet whose didactic poem *Phaenomena* was famous in antiquity.</note> sings, clearly signifying "Jupiter" as the common life of the world’s body.

I seem to have stumbled upon these words of Aratus now quite opportunely. I remember that Luke the Evangelist and Paul the Apostle freely used these words. In their writings, those wise men did not shudder at the idea of the "life of the world." 

But a certain superstitious person will object to this, arguing that it is not easily proven from such words that Paul agreed that the world has a soul; rather, he might mean that the world is merely subject to God, and that we live in this same God. So be it. Let us not use the name "soul" <note>original: "anima"</note> for the world since that is displeasing. Let the term "soul" be considered profane if you wish. But will it be permissible, at least, to speak of some kind of "life"? This is the life which God himself, the <term>craftsman of the world</term> <note>original: "mudi faber" — likely a reference to the Platonic Demiurge or Creator.</note>, might mercifully breathe into this work of his, so happily completed. Since he is not stingy toward even the lowliest living things, and daily through the heavens he most generously bestows life upon the many things that are within it. 

Tell me, I pray—do you not see that oxen and donkeys (O you ox! O you donkey!) who silently produce living things from themselves, are alive? If, therefore, these things moreover, from themselves...

<summary>The author argues that the universe itself is a living entity, citing the Greek poet Aratus and the Apostle Paul to defend the concept of a "living world" against critics who find the idea of a "World Soul" heterodox.</summary>
<keywords>World Soul, Anima Mundi, Piero Soderini, Aratus, St. Paul, Neoplatonism, Stoicism, Creation, Natural Philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 271 ---
<meta>This text appears to be part of Marsilio Ficino's "Apologia," a defense of his work "De Vita Libri Tres" (Three Books on Life), specifically addressing accusations of magic or heresy by using Neoplatonic philosophy and the support of his intellectual circle.</meta>

-># APOLOGY<-

...generated living things by their very appearance, would you not judge these things to be alive in an even more wonderful way? This is so, if you have any judgment or any life yourself. The Sky, as the husband of the Earth, does not touch the Earth (as is the common opinion). He does not lie with his wife; instead, he **illuminates** his wife from all sides with the rays of the sun and his stars, which are like his eyes. By this illuminating, he makes her fertile and procreates living things. Would you then say that he who bestows life even by looking has no life of his own? And that which gave the ostrich <note>original: *strutho*; this refers to the ancient and medieval belief that ostriches could hatch their eggs simply by staring at them intensely</note> its life and its life-giving gaze: is the Sky much worse than that bird itself? Once these arguments are brought into the open, unless you have persuaded that superstitious man, leave him half-dead—or rather, not alive at all.

Therefore, so that we may plead our case with more advocates, my Peter Nero <note>Pietro Nero, a close friend of Ficino and member of the Platonic Academy in Florence</note>, add to our side that "Amphion" <note>In Greek myth, Amphion moved stones to build the walls of Thebes using only the music of his lyre; here it refers to the power of persuasive eloquence</note> of ours, Cristoforo Landino <note>A famous Florentine humanist, poet, and commentator on Dante and Virgil</note>, who is equally an orator and a poet. Our Amphion will quickly soften the stony hearts of our enemies with his wonderful sweetness.

But you, my dearest Guicciardini <note>likely Piero Guicciardini, a prominent Florentine citizen and father of the historian Francesco Guicciardini</note>, my companion, go now, go swiftly, and summon Politian <note>Angelo Poliziano, the preeminent scholar and poet of the Medici circle</note> as our Hercules. Hercules once called upon Iolaus whenever a more dangerous struggle was at hand; you now likewise call upon Hercules. You surely know that our Hercules, Politian, has attacked, torn apart, and slain the "barbarian monsters" <note>Ficino refers here to the "barbaric" Latin of medieval scholasticism which the humanists sought to replace with classical purity</note> already devastating Latium <note>The region of Rome, representing the Latin language and culture</note>, and how fiercely he conquers them everywhere. How safely he defends! For here he [faces] even the hundred heads of the Hydra threatening our children <note>Ficino frequently referred to his published books as his "children"</note>...

<summary>Marsilio Ficino defends his philosophical views on the animated nature of the heavens and calls upon his famous friends—Landino and Poliziano—to help defend his "children" (his writings) against superstitious critics.</summary>
<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, Neoplatonism, Cristoforo Landino, Angelo Poliziano, Astrology, Humanism, Florence, De Vita</keywords>

--- Page 272 ---
<meta>This page concludes a letter from Marsilio Ficino to Pier Soderini regarding his friend Pico della Mirandola, and begins a new letter addressed to Bernardo Canigiani, Giovanni Canacci, and Amerigo Corsini concerning the philosophical "hunt for truth."</meta>

...with a club, and burn them with flames. ¶ Come, my sweetest Soderini; arise, go and greet our Phoebean Pico <note>original: "Picum salutato Phoebeum." Referring to Giovanni Pico della Mirandola, whom Ficino identifies with Phoebus Apollo, the god of light and truth</note>. I often call him my Apollo. He, in turn, calls me his Dionysus <note>Dionysus or Bacchus; in Ficino's philosophy, this represents the "divine madness" or inspiration that complements Apollo’s reason</note> and Bacchus. Therefore we are brothers. Tell my Apollo that a venomous python <note>A reference to the mythical serpent slain by Apollo; here, it likely refers to critics or rivals attacking Ficino’s work</note> is even now emerging from the marsh against us. Let him bend his bow, I beg and pray. Let him hurl his arrows immediately. He will stretch it forth at once, I know what I am saying: and he will kill all that poison at once with a single death. ¶ Farewell now happily, my most beloved brothers, worthy not only of happy health, but of happiness itself. Take care of the health and happiness of my children <note>Ficino frequently referred to his books and published writings as his "children"</note> who are now coming into the light. ¶ September 15, 1489. In the fields of Careggi <note>The site of the Medici villa near Florence where Ficino’s Platonic Academy met</note>.

### That security and tranquility of mind are necessary for life.

**M**arsilio Ficino <note>The text begins with a large blue initial M with a smaller 'm' nested inside</note> sends greetings to his most beloved brothers in the hunt for truth: Bernardo Canigiani, Giovanni Canacci, and Amerigo Corsini. Since we established a certain kind of "hunt" here in these very first words, we perhaps rightly and immediately employed dogs and runners. Indeed, we have appropriately called those practicing philosophy "hunters," as they are always laboring in the breathless pursuit <term>indagine</term> <note>From the Latin 'indagine,' meaning a search or tracking of game; Ficino uses it to describe the methodology of philosophy</note> of truth. But is the term "dogs" also appropriate? "Most appropriate," says Socrates in the *Republic*. For those practicing philosophy are either legitimate or spurious. Both are dogs. Those who...

<summary>Ficino concludes a letter by calling on Pico della Mirandola to defend his works from critics, then begins a new discourse using the metaphor of a "hunt" to describe the philosophical search for truth.</summary>
