--- Page 273 ---
<meta>This page continues a sentence from the previous page, likely discussing the nature of different types of thinkers.</meta>

-># APOLOGY<-

...they sagaciously investigate the truth itself and hold onto it tenaciously once it is found. Others, however, bark, bite, and tear for the sake of mere opinion. Indeed, dogs claim so much for themselves among philosophers that they have not only inserted themselves into a particular sect, but even dedicated a sect of their own at one time called the <term>Cynic</term> <note>From the Greek word *kynikos*, meaning "dog-like." The Cynics were a school of ancient Greek philosophers who emphasized living simply and often displayed a biting wit toward social conventions.</note> sect. Furthermore, the Academy <note>The "Academy" refers to the intellectual circle or school of thought, following the tradition of Plato’s Academy in Athens.</note> has its own dogs. 

Therefore, I call hither to you, sagacious dogs of the Academy; hither to you, swiftest runners. For there are three of you. I pray you, defend my three children—still quite tender—who I fear will be lost immediately among the wolves. Run, I say, with alacrity; for I entrust to you tasks that are desired, not burdens. 

You know my friend **George Benignus Salviati** <note>original: "Georgiū Benignū Saluiatum." This refers to Juraj Dragišić (c. 1445–1520), a prominent Franciscan theologian and philosopher known as "Benignus." The name Salviati refers to his Florentine patrons.</note>: He has long ago sagaciously attained that truth whose tracks you are now hunting and running after everywhere. Like the Sun <note>original: "Solis instar"</note>, he is the greater light who illuminates his smaller brothers. Therefore, if you hear any howling of wolves, report it to him. That most brave George will easily put all the wolves to flight, he who once even transfixed a vast dragon <note>This may be a metaphorical reference to George Benignus's theological victories or a reference to Saint George.</note>. He, therefore, will relieve me of my anxiety and you of your care at the same time.

¶ For someone among you is accustomed, and indeed quite often, to say: that one experiences nothing more healthful for life than to swallow down time with great security. The others, truly, smile immediately at the speaker. But come, tell me, **Canacus**, what is this saying of yours that you repeat so often...

<summary>The author addresses the "dogs of the Academy" (intellectual defenders), asking them to protect his "children" (likely his writings) and look to George Benignus Salviati, a brilliant scholar, as their protector against critics.</summary>
<keywords>Cynicism, Academy, George Benignus Salviati, allegory, truth-seeking, philosophical defense</keywords>

--- Page 274 ---
<meta>The text continues from a previous discussion regarding the consumption or experience of time and the nature of the soul.</meta>

...repeated? To devour time? What, after all, do you want for yourself? You will say not to eat it, but rather to drink it. Not to chew or grind it, but to gulp it down with a full throat. For time itself is a kind of liquid nature (if I may call it that) and slippery. Indeed, this is the nature of liquid things: if you try to confine them into a narrow space, you lose them instantly. For when they are forced together, they flow away and quickly flee. 

If you happen to squeeze a sponge soaked with water, you force the water out and it is immediately scattered. But if you hold it more loosely, you will retain it. This is even more true for air, fire, and the ether <note>original: "etherem". In ancient and medieval science, ether was the "fifth element" that filled the celestial regions, thought to be even more subtle and expansive than air or fire.</note>. Thus, in the works of the poets, those who struggle to clasp the vast images or hands of the gods in their arms labor in vain. 

Things that are extremely broad must be received in a very broad manner. Liquid and vast things must be possessed in a vast way. Certainly, distress <term>angustia: literally "narrowness" or "tightness," used here to describe mental or spiritual pressure or anxiety</term> weighs heavily upon us whenever we reduce the soul and its movement—which are broad by nature—into a narrow space. Whoever weighs their studies and business by the balance <note>The Latin ends with "exam-", likely part of "examen," referring to the needle of a scale or a careful examination.</note>...

<summary>This page uses the metaphor of "liquidity" to argue that time and the human soul are vast and elusive; attempting to "squeeze" or confine them only causes them to slip away. It contrasts the natural expansiveness of the spirit with the "narrowness" (angustia) of worldly distress.</summary>
<keywords>philosophy of time, nature of the soul, liquid metaphor, ether, narrowness, spiritual freedom, classical elements</keywords>

--- Page 275 ---
<meta>This text concludes an "Apology" (a formal defense) by the Renaissance philosopher Marsilio Ficino, likely written in defense of his controversial work *Three Books on Life*. It follows a discussion on the intersection of medicine, astrology, and priestcraft.</meta>

**APOLOGY.**

<note>original: "angustia"</note> Live, then, joyfully and far from distress, oh friends. Live happily. The heavens created you with their own joy. This joy the heavens declare by their own "smile"—that is, by their expansion, motion, and splendor—as if they were leaping for joy. Your own joy will preserve you for heaven. Therefore, live joyfully every day without hesitation. For anxiety about the present snatches the present away from you and steals the future; curiosity about the future quickly hands you over to the past. Therefore I pray again and again: live joyfully. For the fates allow it, so long as you live <term>carefree|original: "securi" (without care/concern)</term>.

But so that you may truly live without care, do not take on even this one worry: that you should ever anxiously fret over what specific <term>diligence|original: "diligentia" (careful attention or effort)</term> you might use to escape cares. For this one care, alas, burns the hearts of wretched mortals with every possible worry. Therefore, neglect diligence. Love <term>negligence|original: "negligentia" (a state of being relaxed or unconcerned)</term> instead—and even love that negligence itself in a negligent way. I say this to you insofar as it is permitted and proper. 

I command these things to you, my friends, not so much as a priest, but as a physician. For without this one thing—which is, as it were, the very life of all medicines—all other medicines applied to prolong life will simply fail.

September 16, 1489. In the fields of <note>Careggi was the site of the Medici family villa outside Florence where Ficino lived and worked.</note> Careggi.

->[Circular library stamp: THE LIBRARY OF ... with a central figure in profile]<-

<summary>In this concluding passage, Marsilio Ficino advises his friends to prioritize joy and a relaxed spirit over anxious diligence to maintain health, framing his advice as both medical and spiritual. He emphasizes that worry about the future or present destroys one's experience of time, whereas joy mirrors the nature of the heavens.</summary>

<keywords>Marsilio Ficino, Careggi, medical philosophy, joy, Renaissance medicine, anxiety, Florence, Neoplatonism</keywords>
